Summary

Carol stays at her lake house for the summer, in desperate need of finding inspiration to write the fifth Wycaro novel. When she meets her neighbour who looks exactly like the female version of her famous character Raban, more than inspiration strikes Carol.

I'm still fact she's fiction

    Chapter 1: a house with a view

    A soft beeping noise brought her focus back to the road. Carol looked down at the car's dashboard and saw a coffee mug icon appear on the screen. Break Time?, it read. 

    Carol looked at the clock. She had already been driving for over an hour and a half, and the gas tank was still over half full. She didn’t want to make unnecessary stops, restless to arrive at her destination, but the growl of her stomach made her realize she could indeed use a break.

    She decided to listen to the car’s suggestion and stop at the next rest area. She put more gas in the car so she wouldn’t have to stop again, used the bathroom, and bought a sandwich and a red Gatorade at the café. She was about to buy a tall black coffee when she saw the bottle in the fridge and, amused, let herself be tempted. 

    She took the time to eat her lunch at a picnic table outside. The buzz of the highway was all but calming, and the only empty table was under the hot early June sun, but it felt good after spending so much time in the AC of the car. She sat on the burning seat and put on her sunglasses. Carol took the first bite of her sandwich and downed it with a long sip of the refreshing drink. She let out a satisfied sigh and decided to finally make the phone call she had pushed back until now. 

    Helen Umstead was still the first contact in her phone. Now that they were separated, even if they had parted ways on good terms, the nature of their calls remained strictly professional. It had been difficult at first; it had taken Carol a very long time to feel slightly normal about the separation. Time had healed the wounds, but the scars were still there and sometimes, they itched. Even if she was beginning to feel at peace with their relationship, she was certain she would never feel totally normal about Helen.

    “Well, if this isn’t my favourite writer. To what do I owe this pleasure? Do you happen to have any good news for me?” 

    The sound of Helen’s soft voice still made Carol’s chest flutter. 

    “You can say that. Guess where I’m headed,” she smiled, playing with the cap of the bottle absentmindedly. 

    “Mmm… To my office, to bring me the first chapters of the next Wycaro in person?”

    “No… Not yet. But,” she said before Helen could reply, “Hopefully this can happen soon.”

    “You have all my attention.”

    Carol could picture Helen in her office, sitting at her large desk in front of the bay window overlooking downtown Albuquerque. She smiled as she heard the creaking of the chair as her manager leaned back, waiting to hear what Carol wanted to share with her. 

    “Well, I’m on my way to the lake house.”

    There was a brief silence before Helen spoke. “Really?”

    “Yes, I decided to give it a try. I think you were right about a change of scenery. The lake house is the perfect place for that.”

    “You didn’t agree so much last time when I mentioned it.”

    Carol felt her cheeks turn red, and not because of the sun. There was nothing but kindness in Helen’s words, and she knew she was teasing her, but she couldn’t help but feel ashamed. The drunken words slurred at her composed and caring ex-wife always made her feel like the worst person in the world. 

    “I’m sorry,” Carol almost whispered. She wasn’t sure Helen could hear it with the highway roaring right next to her. 

    “I’m glad you changed your mind. I trust you will find something there, and it will bring you back on track. But, even if you don't, I will need the first chapters of the book, Carol. There’s a limit to the time I can buy you, and we are approaching that deadline. The editor won’t be very patient anymore. And your fans too.” 

    Carol nodded as she felt her rib cage tighten around her chest.

    Writing the first Wycaro novels hadn’t always been a walk in the park. At first, it had been mornings spent writing in a diner on legal pads she had stolen from the temp job she kept in order to save money to buy a laptop. Then, there had been some... creative choices regarding her characters that she had made out of fear and shame. Decisions she had regretted from the moment the first book was greenlit, and now, had to live with. And finally, becoming a best-selling writer with a horde of fans who had high expectations — expectations she wasn’t sure she could live up to anymore — certainly put a weight on her. 

    Now that Helen had left her, it was even more difficult to go on with the books. They had remained friends and Helen was as supportive as ever of Carol, but to see her live her life fulfilled, happy and loving someone that wasn’t Carol hurt more than she could ever admit out loud.

    Even if Carol knew in her heart that Helen deserved someone who wasn’t a closeted drunk. Someone she didn’t need to monitor the drinking. Someone who didn’t complain about stupid little careless details during a romantic getaway trip. 

    Carol was happy for Helen, but she felt miserable for herself and, well, writing a life-altering romance in this condition was difficult even for the most skilled novelist. 

    “I know, don’t worry, Helen,” Carol managed to say. “I realize everything you’re doing for me, and I don’t want to let anybody down. I’m… I started outlining some ideas, actually.”

    “Wonderful!” Helen replied enthusiastically. 

    It wasn’t really true, but it wasn’t exactly a lie either. 

    She had started writing down more ways she could create conflict between her heroine Lucasia and Raban, the fan favourite handsome corsair. She was getting tired of this dance, but it was all she could come up with and she knew her fans would love it. 

    “Well, I won’t take any more of your time. I should get back on the road if I don’t want to arrive too late. There’s gonna be a lot of cleaning up to do there.”

    Carol hadn’t been to the lake house for almost a year before the separation, which was over two years ago. And she knew for certain that Helen hadn’t been there either as her new partner had a cabin in the desert and she knew Helen had only agreed to buy the lake house because Carol wanted to. She thought it was way too far from Albuquerque. 

    “Yes, good luck with that!” Helen chuckled. “Thanks for calling, Carol. Keep me posted on the writing and don’t hesitate if you need anything. I’m here for you.”

    Carol knew Helen meant well, but her kindness made her feel pathetic. She didn’t have anyone else to turn to and Helen knew it. “Thanks,” she sighed nonetheless.

    They hung up and Carol finished her lunch. She got back in the car and drove smoothly to the lake house, humming to the songs on the radio.

    She wasn’t delusional; she knew the way back to writing would be difficult, but she had high hopes that this time, out in nature, her luck would turn and not only make her feel inspired again, but make her fall back in love with writing. 

    She didn’t have the choice. She had to write another Wycaro novel. And she had to do it fast.

     

    *****

     

    Carol stopped the car in the gravel driveway. It was a path that went slightly downhill and served the three lake houses in her corner of the street. 

    Carol and Helen’s cabin was in the middle, facing the lake and surrounded by pine trees. It was a two-story wooden house with large, old windows on each side. 

    She put the car in park and took a moment to look at the house. The exterior was in good condition as Helen and Carol paid their neighbour’s handyman to take care of the grounds during their absence. 

    She noticed the cabin to the right, the one that belonged to a quiet man named Manousos Oviedo. Carol never minded him much. He didn’t talk to them, she didn’t talk to him. They each minded their business and it was perfect like that. Carol noticed his car wasn’t there, but the house seemed lived in. Maybe he had gone to town. 

    Carol got out and noticed a vintage bright blue sedan parked in front of the house on the left. It was a long, one-story cabin that belonged to a Polish family. The husband, a commercial liner pilot, was almost never there. When Carol came before the separation, she mostly saw his wife, a brunette who was also the only neighbour to be nice to Carol. She visited with her three children during some summer weekends. They never talked much, and Carol thought the loud children were annoying. She guessed they must have changed their car; she remembered they owned a white family van. This small car was certainly a choice for a family of five. 

    Carol walked to her cabin and unlocked the door. It creaked open and a strong smell of closed air hit her. She walked in, leaving the door open behind her. 

    She entered a small living room area that led to a kitchen. There were windows all around, looking over the lake and to the neighbour’s cabins. All furniture was covered by white sheets, closed off for the season, three years ago. A heavy pile of dust sat on the fabric. She opened all the windows around the living room and kitchen, hoping to get some fresh air in. 

    She stood by the counter in the kitchen and saw the backyard grass was freshly cut and the path to the beach was hidden by large trees that had grown since the last time she was here. She took in the sight of at the lake. The view never failed to amaze her. There were many boats sailing on the lake, all going in different directions. Through the open windows, she could hear kids playing in the water from afar.

    Carol looked over at the cabin on the left and saw that it was rather quiet. Probably the children weren’t there this time. 

    Bless, she thought. 

    Carol made her way to the guest bedroom on the first floor and opened the windows before climbing up the stairs to the mezzanine-level bedroom. She looked around the space where she used to sleep with Helen. The unmade bed was hidden under a white sheet. Framed pictures of the couple on the beach were also covered by dust. She looked at their happy faces and sneezed. 

    Once all the windows were open to let a fresh breeze in, Carol took all of the white sheets off the furniture around the house. She shook them on the back porch outside and threw them in a dirty laundry bag. She swept the dust, vacuumed and washed the floors thoroughly. It was a tiring chore, especially after hours of driving. 

    When she was finally done, Carol grabbed her suitcases and made herself at home. 

    She put the books she had brought with her on the coffee table in the living room, put away the picture frames of her and Helen and stacked the food she had brought in the kitchen cabinets. She opened the freezer and found a bottle of vodka. A smile lit up her face. She could work on this bottle tonight and go to town to buy more liquor the following day. 

    Carol poured herself a glass and took a long sip, letting the ice-cold beverage cool her sweaty body. She stood by the window in silence and already felt more at peace in the clean space. 

    That’s when she saw her, coming out of the trees that led to the private deck she shared with her two neighbours. 

    The first thing she noticed was the shoulder-length dark streaks of hair that came out of the blue bandana tied around her head. She wore a loose white linen shirt, buttoned almost all the way down, revealing sun-kissed skin and a black swimsuit top. She wore black jeans cut halfway through the thighs and her very long legs were strong and tan. Her feet were covered in sand and she carried slippers in her hand.

    Carol held her breath at the sight of her.

    She was the prettiest woman she’d ever seen. 

    The woman carried a beach bag on her shoulder and walked up the path to the house next door.

    So she was the owner of the blue sedan. She had never seen her here before.

    Maybe the Polish family has sold the cabin, Carol thought. 

    The woman walked out of view, but Carol was unable to take her eyes off her. She moved to the living room and peered through the window. From there, she could see the woman walk up the stairs to her back door and enter the cabin, never noticing the eyes on her. 

    Carol went back to the kitchen and poured herself another glass. She frowned, lost in her thoughts. She had never seen this woman before — certainly, she would remember if she had — yet, she seemed oddly familiar. 

    Carol downed these thoughts with her drink and decided to get back to work and create herself a space where she would write. She was here to work, after all. 

    When she came with Helen before, Carol wrote in the guest room. There, she had a small desk and a whiteboard to outline her ideas. This time, she wasn’t here on vacation and she was alone. She didn't need a large bedroom.

    Carol brought the heavy desk upstairs and pushed the queen bed against the wall, leaving room to create an office space. She lined up the desk in front of the window overlooking the lake and noticed the parked Pontoon boat on their private deck. So this is where the woman was coming from. 

    The image of the woman came back to her mind. She couldn’t let go of the idea that she’d seen her somewhere before. But where? Certainly not in this cabin. Perhaps in the village? In Albuquerque? 

    Carol finished arranging the work space in a way that she liked. She was looking at her work, arms crossed on her chest when it hit her.

    She ran down the stairs to a small bookshelf in the living room and grabbed a Wycaro novel. She looked at the cover and almost burst into laughter as she stared back at the bandana woman.

    This time, she wore pirate clothes and a moustache. She was looking at the painted picture of her character Raban. 

    The resemblance was laughable, almost shocking. It was as if someone had put a moustache on the woman and boom, there was Raban. 

    Carol smiled, amused, and put the book into place. She peeked through the window and saw her again. This time, she stood in the kitchen, focused on something below the window frame. Probably cooking something for dinner after a day on the lake, Carol guessed. 

    The writer felt weird and intrusive to be watching a stranger like this, but she couldn’t take her eyes off her. Perhaps it was the way she looked exactly like the female version of her character. The character she had initially written as a woman.

    It felt so bizarre. Like she was seeing a fragment of her own imagination. She never really had a clear image of her characters in mind. To her, Raban didn’t look exactly like the painting on the cover, though there was a resemblance. She could imagine some of his features, but there was a certain blur to him that she liked. Now, seeing this beautiful woman, it was like she could see him for the first time. Like she was seeing something new. Something she couldn't even explain to herself. 

    Carol was so lost in her own thoughts that she wasn’t prepared for the woman to look up and stare straight back at her. 

    Caught in the act, Carol didn’t act naturally and smiled or waved. 

    She hid. 

    “Seriously?” Carol cursed herself, rolling her eyes and shaking her head at her own ridiculous behaviour. It was normal to be looking outside one’s window. Why did she make it look like she was a weirdo?

    Carol hesitated, wondering which would be worse: to reappear in the window and smile awkwardly or to simply hide for the rest of the evening. She decided she had embarrassed herself enough for the day and went on with setting up the lake house for the summer.

    Once the sun had set and she had brought the cabin back to life, she went to the kitchen and cooked an omelette for dinner. She ate in silence, writing down what she would need to buy in town.

    She cleaned the dishes and walked out on the porch. The night was comfortable and the soft summer breeze was welcome after such a warm day. The blonde woman closed her eyes and listened to the sound of crickets and the soft waves splashing against the boats parked on the dock. 

    She was tired and thought about going to take a well-deserved shower before crashing down in bed, but the quietness of the night energized her and she wanted to enjoy this peaceful evening – a rare moment her brain wasn’t spinning and overthinking – to go and take a walk around the neighborhood. 

    She walked on the deserted sidewalk that went along her street. Only dozens of cabins were built on both sides of the road, all benefiting from the intimacy of the surrounding forest and trees. Carol looked at the summer houses as she walked, looking through the neighbour’s windows to see them eat dinner or catch them sitting outside by their fireplace, drying their wet hair after a day spent out on the lake. 

    The last time she’d walked here, she was with Helen. Side by side, hands only slightly brushing against each other, even in the dead of the night, when the shadows of the trees protected them from the eyes of the neighbours, who would never be able to see them hold hands, even if they cared to try. 

    The writer felt a pinch in her heart. She hated that she had been such a coward. That she still was to this day. It had cost her so much: her dream of writing the stories she really wanted to tell and the love of a lifetime she had shared with the most patient and kindhearted woman she had ever known. 

    Carol pushed the idea away. She was starting anew. She was finally taking the steps to help herself be happy. While the nostalgia of memories with Helen was valid, Carol forced herself not to dwell on it and focus on what was ahead. Peaceful few days spent alone in nature and – hopefully – a new Wycaro novel.

    Just as she was about to complete the trip around the street, loud disco music took her out of her own head. She looked up and noticed one of her neighbours was having a party. She smiled and walked towards the house. Koumba Diabaté, famously, or infamously, known for throwing the best parties in the area at least once a week, stood on the sidewalk in front of her, kissing one of his guests goodbye.

    Allegedly, the best parties, because Carol herself had never been invited to one. 

    Koumba waved as his guest’s car drove off, a bright smile on his face. His easy and happy smile always annoyed her. She didn’t know if it annoyed her because he constantly wore it on his face or because it actually looked genuine. The tall, curly-haired man turned around and stopped dead in his tracks at the sight of her. 

    Somehow, his smile grew wider.

    “Ma parole!” he exclaimed in French. “Look what the cat dragged in. Carol Sturka, I’ll be damned. So you are still alive.”

    “So you still have the same definition of fun as a frat boy,” she said plainly, pointing at his house with her chin.

    “Had I known you were back, I would’ve invited you.”

    Carol stopped in front of him and studied his face. He hadn’t changed at all. In the three years since she’d last seen him, he hadn’t aged a day. He probably could not tell the same for her, but he was polite enough not to show it. 

    “Aw, we both know my time is way too precious to spend it here. So, disco, uh? You really ran out of ideas.”

    He shrugged. “It’s fun and timeless. So, do you wish to come in?”

    Carol chuckled and looked up at the house. “Thanks, I’ll pass…”

    She trailed off her words as she noticed her, standing on the deck that stood high on stilts. She looked somewhat out of place, too beautiful and glamorous to be among Koumba’s guests. 

    Her new neighbour wore a light summer dress and her dark hair was pulled up in a bun. She was leaning on the fence, taking a long inhale of a cigarette, her eyes fixed on Carol, who was taken aback by the sight of her here. 

    Koumba followed her gaze and threw Carol a smug smile. “I see you’ve met Zosia.”

    “Who?” Carol asked, forcing her eyes off the woman.

    “Your neighbour.”

    She shook her head. “No, I haven’t. I just arrived this afternoon. Did Henryk and Iza sell?”

    Many things could happen in three years, Carol thought. Perhaps the Polish couple had sold their cabin and this Zosia was the new owner. 

    “No, Zosia is Henryk’s sister. She’s staying for the summer. You should go introduce yourself. You might see a lot of each other if you plan to stay the summer.”

    Carol risked another look in Zosia’s direction. The tall woman had left. It was a relief just as it was a disappointment. The writer rolled her eyes in her head. A disappointment. She knew nothing about this woman. Why did she need to take another look at her so badly?

    “I’ll have the chance to run into her. Enjoy your party.” Carol started walking towards her house.

    “Carol,” Koumba called after her. She turned and he smiled warmly. “Welcome back. It’s good to see you.”

    She felt her cheeks turn pink. None of her neighbours had ever said anything like that to her before. Maybe he had noticed she wasn’t walking with Helen and took pity on her.

    “Thanks. It’s good to be back.” She waved lightly at him and headed home.

    Carol stood for a moment in the shower, letting the lukewarm water temper her body temperature. The refreshing cool of the night after such a warm day had left her trembling on her way back. 

    She got out of the water and got into her bathing robe. She brushed her teeth as she walked around and got the cabin ready for the night. She closed all the windows on the first floor with one hand and kept brushing her teeth with the other. She stopped in the kitchen and reached to close the curtains when she saw light coming from the house across from hers.

    So Zosia was back home already. 

    Carol briefly looked, but there was no sight of the woman. Good thing, if she saw her looking at her house yet again, she would think Carol was a psycho. She closed the curtain before the woman had the time to reappear. 

    She decided to sleep in the guest room, wanting to leave the office for working purposes, but mostly because the idea of sleeping in the bed she had once shared with Helen brought her to a place she didn’t want to go. She had already overcome that feeling inside her own house about a year ago, when she stopped sleeping on the couch and went back to her own bedroom.

    Besides, the bottom bed of the bunk bed would be more than suitable. 

    Carol lied down between the clean sheets and took a deep breath. She was asleep in seconds.

    It was the morning light that woke her. She tossed and turned for a while, until it became clear that she wouldn’t fall asleep again. She got up, head pounding as she rubbed her eyes and made her way to the kitchen. She fumbled around the kettle found a French press in the back of the cabinets. 

    Carol yawned and opened the curtains and windows, letting the morning breeze in. She looked over at the clock on the oven and saw it wasn't even 7am. She sighed. She wasn’t exactly a morning person, but she guessed it was a good thing that she woke up so early. Maybe it could become a new habit here in the wild and she would be more productive with her writing this way. 

    While the water was boiling, she leaned down on the counter and looked over at the lake. It was as still as ice in the morning and a cloud of mist flew over it. 

    The muffled sound of a door opening brought her attention next door.

    Carol leaned down and saw Zosia walk out of her house and lock the door behind her. She wore light jean shorts and a white knit sweater. Her wavy hair was loose on her shoulders, still carrying the same beach bag as the previous day.

    Carol watched her. She looked like a real top model. A top model on her way to drive a boat. Except, she should’ve been in the South of France, not a lake in southern Colorado.

    Zosia made her way down the path to the private beach. Just before she disappeared behind the trees, she stopped and looked over her shoulder.

    She looked straight at Carol and waved at her, a smile on her lips.

    Chapter 2: a friendly neighbour

    Chapter Notes

    apologies for the delay! I didn't plan on letting that much time pass between chapters, but last week was crazy busy!! thanks for the nice comments on chapter 1, I hope you enjoy this one too and I'm already working on chapter 3 hihi

    Carol blinked. She wasn’t expecting the woman to notice her, let alone wave and smile at her.

    What a beautiful smile she has, Carol thought.

    She hesitated, unsure what to do. She slowly lifted her hand to wave back, but Zosia had already disappeared behind the trees.

    She stood there, looking like an idiot and rolling her eyes at her own stupid behaviour. She had just got to the forest and she had already lost her social skills.

    The shrill whistle of the kettle brought her back to her initial mission: make coffee and get started with writing.

    Carol threw two spoons of old ground coffee from her house into the French press and filled it with water. She put the press and a cup on a platter that she carefully carried to the office. She filled the cup to the rim and took a first sip of the black coffee.

    She closed her eyes as the caffeine worked its way through her and woke her up. She was finally ready to get on with the day despite the fatigue and turned on her computer. Carol was full of good intentions for this new book. She opened a blank document; it was time to start anew. A fresh start. Carol watched the cursor blink once, then twice. She rested her fingers on the keyboard and caressed the keys’ shapes with the tip of her fingers.

    What happens now?

    She closed her eyes and tried to visualize it. The purple sands. Lucasia and her ship. Her lover.

    Raban.

    Except it wasn’t Raban that she visualized. She couldn’t even remember how she had imagined him before. Now, all she could see was the flesh and bones version of him she’d seen with her own two eyes.

    The version of him that was a her.

    Carol rubbed her eyes. She could still feel the fatigue from the previous day: travelling to the lake house, reorganizing it, and cleaning it. All this was topped by the emotional challenges she had faced over the past years that she was still only just starting to be able to work through.

    She gently slapped her cheeks to get the blood running. “Come on, Carol,” she told herself. “Focus.”

    She blinked a few times and looked back at the blank page. Willing her mind, she started to see an image slowly get to focus.

    The ship. Sailing smoothly in the night. The twin moons shining bright on a woman standing at the front of Mercator. Except the woman wasn’t her usual heroine. It was her again.

    Carol’s eyes trailed up above the computer, through the window that looked directly over the lake. Just like that, she was brought back to the present time and space. She looked at the calmness of the water and noticed there were almost no boats sailing just yet. It was still early.

    She wondered which belonged to Zosia.

    She could imagine her, standing in her white knit sweater, her soft hair pushed around her head by the wind. The freshness of the morning air giving her goosebumps on her arms and bare legs.

    Her vision became crystalized, images clear as water.

    The writer let the thoughts run in her mind until she realized she’d been imagining her neighbour on her boat — a neighbour she hadn’t even met in person — for way too long, and she wasn’t getting any work done.

    Carol got up and occupied herself with chores. Perhaps it was still too early to get to work. Before she sold Winds of Wycaro, she had been more productive writing in the mornings, but when she stopped needing a side hustle and could focus only on her writing, she developed the habit of writing later in the day. Instead, she started to go out and do the chores when there were less people outside, less chances of running into other humans.

    She waited until the stores opened and got in the car. She drove through town and stopped at the grocery store. Carol allowed herself to buy some food she never usually allowed herself to get, but this was her first days back in the cabin, and she decided she could treat herself.

    As she pushed her cartwheel through the aisles, she noticed another of her neighbours far ahead. Laxmi was one of Kouma’s friends and, just like Koumba, they never got along with each other. She found the woman to be too loud and aggressive for her liking. Perhaps some parts of her reminded Carol of herself, and she didn’t appreciate having a distorted version of herself in front of her.

    Laxmi and her son stood in the middle of a section Carol needed to go to complete her shopping, but rather than face the woman, she turned and disappeared into an alley she had already crossed three times. Lots of changes had been made in the store since the last time she’d been here and she had trouble finding her way around.

    Carol carefully pushed the cart around, making sure she wasn’t about to run into Laxmi and kept a safe distance, far enough to hide before the woman could notice her.

    When Laxmi was nowhere to be found, Carol lowered her guard. Perhaps she had finished shopping and had gone home.

    Carol gathered the remaining articles on her list in the dairy section and rapidly made her way to the cashier when she almost crashed into someone.

    “Look out!”

    Clearly, luck wasn’t on her side. Laxmi hadn’t left. She was still here and had bumped her cartwheel in Carol’s. Or the other way around.

    When their eyes met, a weird tension suddenly became tangible.

    “Oh, hi, Laxmi,” Carol broke the silence.

    “I saw you look at me over there. You don’t need to pretend the contrary. I don’t want to talk to you anymore than you do.”

    Carol blushed. She had had a chance to start over with her neighbours after years of absence. If she didn’t want to spend an entire summer alone and not have any human connection, she should have been nicer to them. Again, they weren’t exactly the type of people she’d want to hang around. Laxmi and Koumba weren’t like her. They could get on with any situation the world offered. They wanted to please and to be delusionally happy so badly that they didn’t stand up for anything they wanted. Hell, Carol had been engaging with them for years, and she didn’t know anything about them.

    “Then, I guess I’ll be on my way.”

    Carol started pushing her cart away, but Laxim’s voice stopped her dead in her tracks.

    “Are you here alone?” she asked, looking around Carol to see where Helen was hiding.

    Clearly, word had spread that Carol, who was always hanging close to her wife, never seen without her at her side, was alone. She knew the nature of their relationship had been obvious to the neighbours, but she knew it was also a big mystery to them. After all, it was a mystery even to Carol how a woman like Helen had kept up with her all these years. She knew they had questions, and the gossip was probably going strong, but she didn’t want to give them anything to feed on. They hadn’t been part of their lives during their relationship; they wouldn’t be part of the separation.

    “I’m here to work. I’m writing my new book,” she stopped herself before she could overexplain herself.

    Laxmi smiled wickedly. She always thought Carol’s books were mindless crap and she had never been shy to hide her opinions. “A new Wycaro novel?”

    “I’ll let you read the first chapters exclusively,” Carol mumbled and she headed to the cashier.

    Carol parked the car in her driveway and killed the engine. She sat in the silence of the car and let the remaining of the AC cool her off. It was barely 10 in the morning and her day was already going downhill.

    She knew inspiration wouldn’t come just like that, that it would take time and more work before she could come up with fresh and passionate ideas. She knew that coming back to the lake house meant running into these people who had nothing better to do than grill under the sun on their private decks all summer long. Yet, she felt disappointed. Keeping her motivation up during her journey to the cabin had taken a lot of her energy, and she felt exhausted.

    Carol got out of the car and carried the first of her multiple grocery bags inside the house. She looked over at the house on the left. She knew Zosia wouldn’t be back so soon from her boat trip, but she couldn’t help the peek. Just like expected, there were no signs of life.

    She slammed the truck door shut and threw the last bag over her shoulder when she noticed Manousos watching her from a distance. He was hesitant, not sure if he should go talk to Carol. He finally only raised a hand and waved at her.

    Carol waved back, more enthusiastically than expected. After her annoying encounters with Koumba and Laxmi, Manousos’ calm and respectful wave was more than welcome. She even shot him a faint smile, which he didn’t mirror.

    Manousos went on to split woodlogs and ignored Carol as she headed home.

    The rest of the day passed by uneventfully. She locked herself in her new office and wrote a few paragraphs of Wycaro that she would probably toss over the following day to restart, but it was something.

    To celebrate her first day of writing, Carol poured herself a large glass of whisky bought in the village earlier. She got out on the back porch and sat on a red Adirondack chair. She looked over at the empty chair at her side and felt a pang of sadness.

    Helen had left her; that was a fact. It still hurt when she thought about it, but she was happy to see the woman she had once loved more than anything on earth, and still loved dearly to this day, be happy. It wasn’t the separation that was so painful for Carol. It was the loneliness and the stillness of her life.

    For Helen, the separation had brought many good things, despite the sadness of ending a life together. Her work had blown up: she now managed more clients than ever, her business running in full swing. She had bought a new house that was lively and warm. She was sharing her life with a kind woman who brought out the best of her.

    Carol was struggling to come up with the fifth book of her series, the one she had grown to despise over the years. She was a fifty-year-old closeted drunk who wouldn’t let herself live openly and pursue the life she only dared to long for in her deepest fantasies.

    She was stuck in the same internal inferno as she had been when she was with Helen, and she didn’t know how to get herself out of it. Only Helen had been her guiding light, and now that she was gone, she was left in complete darkness.

    The writer took a sip of whisky and swallowed it hard. It burned as it made its way down her throat. Carol looked ahead at the sun setting on the quiet lake. There could still be some light; she just had to find it.

    She heard footsteps approaching on the gravel path. The first sips of her drink had already slowed her reflexes. She realized someone was approaching only when she poked out of the trees. Carol sat straighter in her chair.

    The pirate lady walked up the small hill to the lake, slightly breathless. She looked up, and her eyes met Carol’s. A wide smile lit up her face and warmed Carol’s chest.

    “Oh, hi!” she exclaimed and made her way to Carol’s porch. Carol got up to meet her halfway, leaving her drink by the chair.

    The white knit sweater had been tossed out, and now Zosia stood only in her bikini top and jean shorts. “It’s so nice to finally meet you. I’m Zosia,” she extended a hand for Carol to shake. Carol leaned down over the porch fence and shook the woman’s strong, warm hand.

    “Nice to meet you, Zosia,” she said, her voice slightly hoarse. “I’m Carol.”

    “I know, I’ve heard a lot about you.”

    Carol chuckled ironically. “A lot of bad things, I would presume.”

    Panic flashed in Zosia’s eyes for a second, but when she saw Carol put on an unbothered face about it, she tilted her head adorably. “Mostly.”

    Her honesty made Carol smile.

    Carol cleared her throat, looking away at the lake. It was really hard for her to resist looking down at the exposed flesh and keep her eyes on Zosia’s face. She thought the woman must have been a top model at some point in her life.

    “Are you staying here for the summer?” Carol asked.

    “Yes. Henryk is my brother and he lets me stay here and take care of the house while he went back to Poland.”

    Carol nodded. She couldn’t help scrutinizing. The resemblance was even more striking from up close.

    “You have a boat,” was all she could think to say.

    Her face lit up. “Yes. Don’t you? I feel so many people around here own one.”

    The writer shook her head. With this beautiful and warm woman looking at her with those sparkling brown eyes, she was losing her ability to speak like a teenager with a crush. Get yourself together, she told herself.

    “No, I… I don’t really… I never thought of getting one.”

    “It’s so great, and this lake is absolutely amazing. You should come along with me sometime.”

    The idea of spending the day with this woman, alone in the middle of the lake under the warm summer sun, was more exciting than it should be. She was, after all, a stranger she had met barely five minutes ago.

    “Yeah, that could be great.”

    Zosia waited for a second, expecting Carol to share a date or give her something to work with, but nothing came. “Alright, well… I should get going,” Zosia started trailing back to her cabin. “See you around, then.”

    “See you around.”

    Carol watched her go and cursed herself.

    The next morning, Carol peeked through the window as the water for the coffee boiled. She looked over at Zosia’s house, but it was all dark. It was a bit later than the previous day, and she realized she might’ve missed her.

    The writer poured coffee in a mug, wrapped up in a green cardigan, she slid open the patio door. The fresh morning air hit her and woke her a bit more. She walked down the porch and took the path that led to the private deck. Zosia’s pontoon was indeed gone. She squinted her eyes and thought she might see it very far, sailing away for the day.

    Carol sat at the end of the dock and dipped her feet in the cold water. She sipped her coffee and took in the view as the sun fully rose on the lake. She looked down at the small waves crashing on her ankles. She thought about her book. She had to get some writing done. She desperately wanted to have some good news to tell Helen, tell her she had been right and coming to the lake inspired her. And she knew this phone call would inevitably come in the following days.

    You’re thinking too much, she told herself. Just let the story come to you.

    She closed her eyes and took a deep breath. The first image that came to her was Zosia on her boat. She imagined her, in her white knit sweater and blue bandana, sitting behind the wheel, looking ahead with a peaceful look on her face.

    She thought of Raban. Their images merged into one in her mind. The next image that came to her made her blush. Lucasia in Raban’s arms, kissing him deeply as the ship sailed away. Kissing her.

    Carol got up and walked back to the house.

    She spent the day sitting in front of the blinking cursor. Her thoughts kept wandering back to Zosia on her boat. She thought about the invitation she had thrown to Carol.

    You should come along with me sometime.

    Carol blushed at the thought of sitting at the front of the boat while Zosia drove her around. She was probably just being polite, inviting Carol to join her. They were strangers after all. And Carol had mentioned the boat. She hadn’t really meant for Carol and her to plan a day at sea together.

    But then, why had she waited after Carol to say something else? Did she think Carol would tell her she was free to come the day after?

    Carol shook her head and focused on her story. She was here to write – Helen had made it very clear she needed to get the first chapters done – not fraternize with the neighbours. No matter how pretty they were.

    By the end of the day, she had written a couple of words, but it wasn’t going as well as she hoped. She thought perhaps a drink would help, and she went downstairs to stretch and pour herself a glass. She found herself peeking through the window. Zosia was probably about to come back any time soon.

    Carol downed a first glass. “Why do you even care?”

    When the sun set, Carol realized she hadn’t seen Zosia come back. She looked at the dock and saw the boat parked. She probably had been too focused on her writing – on trying to write – to notice her. She sighed as she sat back and looked at her document.

    That night, when she got to bed, she set her alarm for early. She managed to convince herself that she really was setting an alarm to get writing done in the morning and not to get a glimpse of Zosia as she left for the day.

    For the following mornings, they waved at each other every time Zosia left, on her way to the boat, and Carol prepared coffee. Carol observed her new neighbour through the window. Both of them kept a very strict routine. Carol with her writing and Zosia sailing all day long.

    The writer couldn’t help but wonder about her. What was her job? Why was she spending the summer alone at her brother’s cabin? She was curious about her and wanted to get to know her, but she didn’t know what to tell her other than the “Hello, how is it going?” they exchanged when they ran into each other in the backyard.

    One thing was for sure: the more she watched her, the more Carol found her to be the most breathtakingly beautiful woman she’d ever seen. Just the sight of her, sleepy in the morning, and tanned and relaxed at the end of a day on the lake, made Carol flush and gave her butterflies.

    It was about a week after her arrival, and Carol had written a chapter of the new Wycaro, but she wasn’t convinced about the material. She’d sent it to Helen, who thought it was a good start and was eagerly waiting for more. Carol wasn’t an idiot; she knew Helen didn’t like what she’d send her. They talked over the phone for hours, going over the outline Carol had described to her agent. Helen wanted Carol to get back into trusting her own writing before she made her do any changes to the paragraphs.

    That morning was windy and dark. Carol looked at the temperature predictions and it was far below the previous days. Carol saw the light being switched off in Zosia’s house and thought for a second.

    She grabbed a travel mug and poured a cup into it. She rushed outside and caught Zosia walking down the stairs of her porch. She looked over as she heard Carol rush to her. A bright smile lit up her face at the sight of her. It sent shivers down Carol’s spine.

    “Hi, Carol.”

    “Hey. I was just making coffee for myself, and well, I thought today is going to be colder, so I poured you a cup. I don’t know if you like coffee?”

    Zosia seemed pleasantly surprised. She met Carol halfway and took the mug from her hand. Their fingers brushed slightly, and Carol felt her cheeks colour.

    “I do, thank you so much. That’s very nice of you.”

    “Would you like milk in it?”

    Carol buried her hands in the pockets of her green cardigan. She balanced on one foot to the other.

    “It’s perfect just like that.”

    Carol smiled at her, pleased with herself. Zosia looked down at the mug in her hands. “Do you stay alone in the cabin?” she asked.

    Carol was taken aback by Zosia’s question. She fumbled over her words. “I’m - yes, I stay here alone.”

    Somehow, Zosia seemed surprised by the answer. “Oh,” she said, thoughtful. “Well, I also stay here alone. If you ever want to hang out, I think it could be nice to have company.”

    Carol nodded dumbly. “Yes, that sounds great.”

    “I imagine you have things to do, but if one day you are free, you could come with me on the boat.”

    “I’d like that,” Carol said in a low voice.

    “Tomorrow’s weather will be perfect. If you are free, of cour-.”

    “I’m free.”

    Zosia seemed pleased. Her sparkling brown eyes were intent on Carol.

    She looks absolutely adorable, Carol thought.

    “Wonderful!” the brunette cheered. “I’ll meet you here at the same time. Wear a lot of sunscreen.”

    Carol nodded and smiled shyly. “Sounds good.”

    “Have a good day. I’ll see you tomorrow.” Zosia turned to walk to the path.

    “You too.”

    Carol watched her go, unable to believe what had just happened.

     

    Chapter 3: the pirate lady

    Chapter Notes

    Apologies to the boat experts out there... it's been like... over 10 years since I've been on a pontoon, BUT I assume you aren't here to read about boats so....... stursia boat date for y'all!!

    Carol didn’t write a single word that day.

    She spent the morning sitting in front of her computer only to get up only a few moments after, feeling restless, and pacing around the house and out in the backyard.

    She even went to take a walk outside to clear her mind.

    What was making her so nervous, she didn’t know. Was it the fact that she wasn’t going to write at all for an entire day, while she was more than behind on her new Wycaro chapters? Or was it the fact that she was to spend the day alone on a boat with a stranger while she barely had had any social interactions in the past weeks? Did it matter that the “stranger” in question was a breathtakingly beautiful woman she found herself tongue-tied around?

    If she were honest with herself, it was probably the latter. Not that the stagnation of the Wycaro novel didn’t stress her.

    While on her walk to calm her nerves, she realized she wasn’t ready for her boat date with Zosia. Not at all. Her bathing suit was so ancient that the colour had faded. What once was a night-colour dark blue was now grey, or light blue, depending on the lighting, and the fabric had softened to a ridiculous shape. There was no way on earth Zosia would see her in this old, ragged thing.

    As soon as she got home, Carol jumped into the car and drove to town. It didn’t really matter what Zosia thought of her; Carol was well aware of that. The woman probably wouldn’t look twice at Carol, let alone notice her swimwear, but for her own pride, it was important for the writer to look her best. Zosia wasn’t interested in how Carol looked, but for a reason she could not explain, it mattered to Carol how she looked to her neighbour.

    Buying a bathing suit was an arduous task, and it always took her multiple shopping sessions to find the right fit, but this time, she didn’t have that luxury. Not only was the time limited, but so were her store options. She nervously tapped her thumbs on the wheel as she turned into the parking lot, hoping her luck would turn.

    Carol spent – or wasted, as she considered this task – an entire afternoon shopping. She tried almost all the different models the store had to offer until she found the one that made her feel the most confident and the most comfortable. 

    It was a bright red, two-piece bathing suit with small shorts for the bottom and a top that made her cleavage look quite good.

    It would have to do, because the store was about to close for the night.

    Carol bought it and headed home. She realized on her way out of town that she couldn’t remember the last time she’d shaved. She stopped by the pharmacy and bought a razor. She really couldn’t make sense in her head why she was putting so much effort into her appearance.

    Zosia is not going to observe you, she had to remind herself yet again.

    As soon as she got home, Carol opened a bottle of wine and took a large sip to calm her nerves.

    The relief was instant. She felt more peaceful when she washed the bathing suit and shaved her legs in the bathtub. She was slightly tipsy when she started cooking dinner.

    The blonde woman stood by the window, focused on cutting vegetables without chopping the tips of her fingers, when a light switched on in Zosia’s house caught her attention. She put down the knife and looked through the window.

    Zosia stood in front of her own kitchen window, looking down as she, as Carol could only assume, washed her hands. She turned to grab a towel, and her eyes met Carol’s. This time, the writer didn’t hide.

    The tall brunette smiled and Carol smiled back, shyly.

    Then Zosia went on to cook her own dinner.

    Carol barely slept that night. She tossed and turned in the narrow space of the bunk bed, willing sleep to come to her, but there was a twitch in her stomach at the thought of what tomorrow would bring. She was nervous about what she was going to talk about with Zosia. After all, they didn’t know each other at all. They had barely exchanged a few words. She was nervous that even after a week spent waking up before the sun, she would somehow miss her alarm that morning, and that Zosia would leave without her. She also had a throbbing headache from the wine and the digestif she had after dinner.

    Eventually, morning came, and Carol came to the conclusion that she had had a sleepless night. She sat up in bed when her alarm rang and rubbed her eyes, yawning. It was a cold morning and a part of her regretted accepting Zosia’s invitation.

    She had a novel to write, after all, and she had barely written anything in a week. Helen was waiting after her and she didn’t want to disappoint her agent. Yet, there she was, dropping everything to go on a boat adventure with her neighbour, just because... Because what? Because she found her pretty?

    Carol shook her head and grabbed her bathing robe on her way out of the bedroom. She put water to boil and looked out the window. Zosia was running around the kitchen, clearly more energized than Carol.

    Great.

    While the water boiled, Carol went to change into her bathing suit. She put on white cargo pants and threw her green cardigan over a white t-shirt. Through blurry eyes, she placed her dishevelled hair so it didn’t look like she’d just gotten out of bed and applied concealer over her eye bags. She listened to Zosia’s advice and put some sunscreen on her face before throwing the bottle in her bag. She grabbed the book she was reading, filled a bottle of water, and was ready to go.

    Carol looked through the window to see how much more time she had.

    Zosia was still moving around the kitchen so Carol filled two travel mugs with coffee for them, one of which was Helen’s. The blonde had bought it as a Christmas present about fifteen years ago. It felt strange to see it now. Helen hadn’t used it in years.

    She grabbed the mugs, threw her bag over her shoulder and headed to the back door. On her way out, she thought of taking a swimming towel and ran to the bathroom.

    Zosia was waiting by the stairs of her cabin when Carol walked out. She turned and smiled brightly at her guest. “Good morning,” she said, her voice hoarse with sleep and lower than usual.

    “Good morning,” Carol said, handing her her own coffee mug.

    Zosia’s face lit up. The sight of her eyes, bright at Carol’s attention, was worth all the hours spent shopping in the heat, the sleepless night, being even more behind on writing Wycaro, and much more.

    “Thank you,” she said, taking the mug and once again, their fingers brushed. This time, their eyes met for a brief second, and Carol flushed. “Shall we go?”

    Carol cleared her throat. “All aboard!” she joked lamely.

    She followed Zosia down the path to the deck. The sun was rising and the first ray of sunshine reflected in her soft brown hair. She had an aura around her head. Carol’s gaze lowered down her back. She still wore that white knit and loose black shorts. Her tan legs were strong and -

    Carol’s foot caught in a tree root. She almost lost her balance, burning coffee spilling out from the small hole on the cover of the mug. “Fuck,” she mumbled in her breath.

    Zosia turned and put a steadying hand on her shoulder. She had, thankfully, missed the scene. “All good?” she asked, worried.

    “Yeah, all good.” Carol forced a smile.

    “Be careful, it’s still quite dark.”

    “I can see that,” Carol grumbled, which made Zosia smile. Did she really find her grumpiness amusing? Carol softened and followed her to the dock, licking the coffee burn off her hand.

    Zosia threw her bag on the side of her boat and gently put the coffee mug down. “Can you help me with the enclosures?”

    “Sure,” Carol imitated her and put her stuff next to hers.

    Zosia showed Carol how to remove the enclosures she put on every night to protect the boat in case of rain. While she worked on a tied knot, Carol felt a drop of sweat form on her forehead. It was way too early for this.

    Zosia’s experienced hands worked on her side while she watched Carol, amused. “So,” she asked, “have you been on one of these before?”

    “I have,” Carol said, attentive as her fingers worked the fabric, “but not like this. I just walked out and stepped in and sailed away. You really have to do this every morning?”

    “I’m used to it now. Here,” Zosia walked to her and took over Carol’s task. Carol let her and watched as her slender fingers easily freed the enclosure.

    When they were set to go, Zosia opened the door on the low fence and surprised Carol by offering her a hand to get in. Carol froze in her steps. She hesitated and grabbed Zosia’s hand – though she really could get in easily without any help.

    “Thanks,” Carol mumbled as she stepped in.

    Zosia grabbed her own bag and followed.

    Carol looked over the pontoon. The brown leather seats were perfect, almost untouched. There wasn’t a single stain on the beige carpet on the floor. Zosia put her bag next to the captain’s seat and put the keys in the ignition.

    “It’s yours or your brother’s? I feel I never saw Henryk and Iza use this.”

    Zosia walked out, untied the ropes that held the boat to the dock and climbed back in. She closed the door and turned on the engine. She sat on the captain’s chair and smiled at Carol. “They bought it last year and almost never used it, because Iza doesn’t have her license yet. I’m the lucky one who gets to take her out for a spin.”

    Carol sat awkwardly on the bench in the back, next to Zosia. She watched her put the boat in reverse and swiftly turn to sail away on the lake. The view was even prettier from here, especially with the sun rising behind the mountain. She smiled at the sight. She turned to Zosia. “I haven’t been on a boat in so long. Especially not on this lake. It’s gorgeous.”  They exchanged a smile. “Thanks for inviting me to join you. You seem very dedicated to your routine of solitude.”

    Zosia waved it away. “Oh, no. I’m happy to have company. I just come here by myself because I have no one to go with.”

    Carol nodded and looked ahead as dozens of questions crossed her mind. It seemed impossible that this seemingly kind and loving person didn’t share her life with anyone.

    They sat in silence for a long moment, wind in their hair and the rising sun caressing their faces. Carol looked over at Zosia and told herself this could count as research. In a way, she was sailing on a ship with Raban. She smiled to herself at the thought.

     

    **** 

    Carol lost track of time. All she knew was that the sun had risen in the sky and was burning their skin. They hadn’t had much of a chance to talk since they left the dock. The sound of the wind seemed too loud for the early hour. The lake had been so peaceful, it felt disrespectful to pollute the silence with their shy words.

    But now, they came across more boats, and after seeing drivers and passengers wave at each other, Carol realized there must be some sort of code that she wasn’t aware of, so she started imitating her captain and waved at the boats that passed by.

    They were in the middle of the giant lake when Zosia put the boat to a stop. She got up from her seat and stretched. Carol watched from the corner of her eye as she elegantly took off her sweater, exposing her toned and tanned torso. She threw her sweater on the back bench, and Carol blushed as she forced her eyes away from her.

    “For how long have you had your license?" Carol asked, trying to change her mind.

    Zosia smiled and grabbed a bottle of water. “Why? Are you questioning my competence?” She took a long sip of water.

    “Ha! No, I’m just curious.” Carol said, amused.

    Zosia finished her sip, thoughtful. “Over fifteen years now.”

    “You said Henryk was back in Poland. Did you come from Poland? I mean, do you usually live there?”

    Zosia walked to the front of the boat. She climbed over the fence and made a sign for Carol to follow her. She sat by the front of the pontoon and dipped her feet in the water. Carol rolled up her pants and imitated her.

    “I’m also from Poland. We come from the city of Gdánsk, but I’ve been living in New York for many years now.”

    “You’re a long way from home.”

    Zosia looked over at her through squinted eyes, hiding from the sun. “Why all these questions?”

    Carol felt her cheeks turn pink. “I… Just curious.”

    “Do you know Henryk and Iza very well?”

    “No, not really. I mean, you’ve heard what my neighbours think of me. I assume they think the same. We never interacted much, and I haven’t been to the cabin in a few years.”

    Carol looked at her feet in the water and made small circle motions, the water tickling between her toes.

    “They are not the most friendly either,” Zosia said. “We got closer in the past year, but how many years he owned this cabin and he never invited me.”

    There was no resentment in Zosia’s voice; she was only stating facts. She sighed and lay back on her arms. She lifted her face to the sun and closed her eyes, a pleased smile on her face. “But how I’m glad he did this time.”

    Carol took the time to watch her. She noticed her dark eyebrows, the subtle wrinkles at the corners of her eyes, her long nose, and the little dimples of her cheeks.

    “What do you do for living?”

    “I’m a pilot.”

    That was the last thing Carol expected. The surprise, and the plain way in which the woman said it, brought a smile to her lips. “A pilot?”

    Zosia looked at Carol, eyebrows raised. “Are you surprised?”

    “I don’t think I’ve ever met a woman pilot before.”

    “Yeah, there aren’t that many.”

    “So what? You drive commercial planes?”

    “Stop talking like that, you will insult me,” Zosia joked coolly.

    “Sorry,” Carol chuckled. “I don’t want to be rude, I just… Didn’t picture you as a pilot.”

    “What did you picture me for?”

    She put a strand of sand-coloured hair back behind her ear, thoughtful. “Like… A top model?”

    Zosia laughed and the low, clear sound made Carol smile like a teenager. “Very original. And what about you? What do you do?”

    Carol turned to her, pleasantly surprised. “Oh, you’ve never heard of me before?” she asked. She lifted a foot out of the water and rested her chin on her knee.

    “What? Are you famous? Am I supposed to know who you are?”

    Normally, Carol would present her books in a negative way. Depends, are you a fan of mindless crap? she would tell people she met. But again, she wanted Zosia to see her in a good light.

    “Well, I don’t know, but if you work in airports, you’ve seen my work at some point.”

    Zosia searched her eyes, confused.

    “I’m a writer,” she eventually said. “I write fiction novels.”

    Zosia was clearly surprised by this news. Her face lit up. “Really?”

    “Why? Does it surprise you?” she teased her.

    “You don’t look like a writer,” Zosia responded, amused. “I would’ve taken you more for, I don’t know… A banker? A lawyer?”

    Carol puffed. “Jesus, do I look that square?”

    “A bit.”

    They laughed, and Zosia held Carol’s gaze. She noticed the iris of her left eye was almost entirely black, but the sun reflected in the brown of her right iris.

    Carol looked down when the weight of her eyes became too much.

    “Anything I might have read?”

    “Have you heard of Winds of Wycaro?”

    Zosia blinked. Clearly, the name didn’t ring a bell.

    “I suppose that, with your job, you don’t need to read books in airports.”

    “What is your book about?”

    Carol found herself excited to talk about her books. She hadn’t felt like this in a very long time, and just for that, she thought that coming to the boat with Zosia had been a good idea.

    “Well, it’s a series of books. I wrote four. Now I’m working on the fifth one. It’s a fantasy romance about the adventures of a ship captain named Lucasia and her lover, the corsair Raban.”

    Zosia smiled. “Wow... I’m sorry that I don’t know your books,” the tall woman admitted. “I would love to read them, though. They seem very interesting.”

    “I… I can give you a copy if you want. I have one back at the cabin.”

    Zosia’s face lit up. “Really?”

    “Sure, why not?”

    They stayed in silence for a moment. It was Carol’s turn to start feeling warm. She took off the white t-shirt, revealing her new swimsuit. She didn’t dare to look in Zosia’s direction, but she could feel the weight of the woman’s eyes on her. The pink on her cheeks turned to the same colour as her top when she threw the t-shirt over the fence.

    “Do you read a lot?” Carol asked to smooth her uneasiness.

    “When I can. Now that I’m here for the summer, I try to take more time to read.”

    “You can really take the entire summer off work?”

    For the first time, she saw the features on Zosia’s face harden. Carol feared she might have said something. Quickly, Zosia looked up at her and flashed a smile like nothing had happened. “I took a sabbatical.”

    She didn’t give any explanations, and Carol didn’t dare to ask more questions.

     

    ****

     

    They sailed on the lake, under the bright midday sun. Carol sat at the back of the boat, next to her captain. Her eyes were closed as she let the wind caress her face and the sunshine warm her. She couldn’t help the pleased smile on her face.

    At that moment, all her worries were gone. There was no frustration, no broken heart. She understood why Zosia wanted to spend all her time here.

    Carol opened her eyes and met Zosia’s stare. Her brown eyes were soft on her. The tall woman smiled shyly and looked ahead. Carol couldn’t help but blush. Maybe she had underestimated the attention her neighbour would give her.

    “Do you wanna take the controls before we reach the river? Zosia smiled. "It’s fun.”

    “Can I?”

    Zosia shrugged. “If you want to.”

    Carol cleared her throat and got up from her spot. She joined Zosia, who stepped aside to leave her more room and looked ahead. When Carol grabbed the wheel, Zosia let go. 

    It was easy to drive. The waters were calm, and the lake was so spacious that there was no traffic. Carol looked up at Zosia, beaming. “It’s fun!”

    “I know,” Zosia grinned. She pointed to the right side of the lake. “We’re going this direction.”

    Carol nodded and focused on the road. Zosia watched her, amused. They slowly made their way to the river entrance and Zosia slowed the pace while Carol still held the wheel.

    “Do you want to listen to some music?”

    “Sure,” Carol said, taking her task seriously.

    Zosia grabbed a CD case and flipped through the pages. “Let’s see… We have… Fleetwood Mac, the greatest hits; Madonna, Like a Prayer or Boat Playlist 1, 2 and 3.”

    Carol smiled. “Let’s go with Boat Playlist 1.”

    Zosia slid the CD out of the plastic pouch and inserted it into the player. There was a brief silence before the first chords of Summer Breeze by Seals and Crofts piped through the speakers.

    Carol looked ahead at the waters and the pine trees surrounding the small hills by the river. She waved at the passengers of the jet boat, slowly passing them.

    “You look like you’ve been doing this your entire life,” Zosia chuckled, waving at the passengers as well. “Head left here, I want to show you a place.”

    Zosia took Carol to a reclusive area of the river. It was a small bay, hidden from the river and lake by a deserted mountain. There were no cabins, no other boats around. They were completely alone. Carol watched as Zosia cut the motor. “It’s so peaceful here. I like it.”

    They spent a few hours parked in their little side of heaven. Carol lying down on her stomach in front of the boat, reading the novel she brought with her, The Left Hand of Darkness, and Zosia by her side, reading a thriller she said she bought at the pharmacy on her way to the cabin.

    She tossed and turned, and eventually gave up reading. “I’ll go swim a bit.”

    She got out and slid down from the boat into the river. Carol watched disappear under the water, a smile on her lips. When she emerged, she pushed her wet hair away from her face. “It’s very refreshing. You should come, Carol,” Zosia encouraged her.

    She liked the way her name sounded on her lips.

    “I don’t really like to swim in lakes. What if a fish bites my toe?” she joked, shielding her eyes from the sun to get a better look at her.

    Zosia crawled away. “Just tell yourself they are more afraid of you than you are of them.”

    “Maybe another time. I want to know what happens next,” she said, lifting her book.

    “Suit yourself.”

    Carol watched Zosia swim away. 

    For the first time in a very, very long time, she was excited to get back behind her computer and write.

     

    Chapter 4: storms

    Chapter Notes

    So... I spent the entire day of Sunday writing and I planned to post this chapter much sooner, but I really couldn't stop writing lmao anyway!!! thank you so much for reading and for the kind comments! I really appreciate it! I hope you guys enjoy reading this fic as much as I enjoy writing it :D

    The backdoor of the cabin burst open as Carol rushed into the living room, slamming against a table that shook at the impact. She abandoned her beach bag along the way as she headed to the bookshelves.

    Her eyes scanned the titles of novels and old encyclopedias that filled the shelves: Winds of Wycaro, four copies of them; followed by three copies of Stormshadow of Wycaro and five copies of Bloodsong of Wycaro. She didn’t have the third novel of the series, so she made a mental note to go buy one in case Zosia would ever read this far.

    Zosia turned and smiled proudly. “Thank you. It was completely neglected when I arrived and I thought ‘What a beautiful space to waste’ so I started growing some herbs and then some vegetables.     “Oh, yes,” Carol handed it to her.

    She watched Zosia's face as she observed the cover. Her eyes lingered on something and the writer noticed a flicker in her eyes. “Nice cover,” she finally said, showing it to Carol, her thumb subtly brushing right next to Raban’s face.

    Carol felt her cheeks flush. Certainly, Zosia could not see the resemblance too? This was just a piece of art that reflected his traits. Not a picture. The resemblance only made sense in Carol’s head. Or so she believed… Now she wasn’t so sure and she suddenly felt the urge to run back inside her cabin and hide.

    Instead, she cleared her throat and played it cool. “Thanks. If you don’t like it, be honest with me. It won’t hurt my feelings,” she lied. 

    Zosia tilted her head and smiled. “Let me read it first.”

    Zosia closed the book and looked up at her. “Yes, I had a really good time with you.”

    Her eyes were so sweet on her it made Carol visibly gulp. 

    “Me too,” she said in a low voice.

    “I hope you will come back soon. I probably won’t take the boat out tomorrow, I’ve got some chores to do, but next time you need a break from writing… You know where to find me.”

     

    ***

     

    It was past midnight and Carol was still glued to the screen of her computer, frantically typing down words to the soft tapping of rain against the window She was so immersed in her own universe that she hadn't notice neither the weather, nor the time. 

    It had been a long time since the vision had come to her so clearly. She didn’t need to force ideas out of herself and instead wrote with a sly smile hung on her lips.

    She had to be honest with herself and admit that all she saw in Raban was Zosia now. Seeing her on the boat had been like a feverish vision. Perhaps it made no sense at all, but if it was what it took to be inspired again, she would gladly take it.

    Only when her vision became blurry and her yawn made her eyes water did she realize that she was completely exhausted. After a sleepless night and a day spent under the sun, the fatigue had spread through all of her limbs.

    She got ready for the night and noticed the light was still on in Zosia’s house before she collapsed into the bottom bed of the guest bedroom, where she now took permanent residence. She closed her eyes and smiled at the soft rocking feeling, like she was still on the boat.

    She did feel a bit uncomfortable and like she was overstepping to picture Zosia as Raban. After all, the brunette was a real person. Her neighbour and a stranger. 

    Not so much a stranger anymore. She had learned a lot about her in only a day and it had only managed to intensify Carol's urge to discover more about her. Certainly, she was a fascinating woman. Carol wanted to ask about her life in New York, about her growing up in Poland, about working as a pilot, about her relationship with her brother. About whether or not she was single and… what her dating preferences were. 

    Carol felt her cheeks warm up.

    It was a big mystery to her, not that it really mattered if she liked women. 

    It’s not like something’s gonna happen, Carol told herself. 

    She turned on her side and faced the window. She watched as the raindrops slid down the glass through a cack between the curtains.

    Carol thought about numerous occasions she was pretty certain she’d caught Zosia watching her. She also suspected she’d seen her peek at Carol while she undressed into her bathing suit. 

    Carol shook her head. It was ridiculous. Still, she couldn’t help but wonder. 

    She thought it could possibly go both ways. She never really had a good “gaydar”. It was always Helen who could spot lesbians from miles away. Carol was so oblivious to these kinds of things. She never noticed when women hit on her, she never noticed when they were overly friendly. It was the first time since the beginning of her relationship with Helen that she tried to see if there were signs of a certain interest, or if Zosia was just being friendly.

    Of course she is just being friendly, Carol told herself. You only just met her.

    “Whatever,” Carol mumbled and let sleep take control over her thoughts.

     

    ***

     

    The rain woke her from a dreamless sleep. Carol blinked a few times to get back to her senses, her limbs heavy from a deep night of sleep. She squinted her eyes at the grey light coming from the window and she wondered what the time was.

    She dozed off again and woke up some time later, fully rested. 

    Carol got out of bed and stretched, groaning as her joints cracked. She put on her bathrobe and walked into the kitchen to make coffee. The clock on the oven announcing it was past eleven in the morning didn’t alarm her one bit.

    She gazed through the window and found a peaceful view. Because of the rain, the lake was deserted, the skies grey and the bright green grass seemed happy. 

    She noticed Zosia’s vintage sedan was not in the parking lot. 

    The buzzing of her phone on the counter took her out of her own head. The screen announced it was Helen calling.

    Carol cleared her throat. “Hello.”

    “Carol, finally! I’ve been trying to reach you since yesterday. I was starting to worry you might have gone on a hike and broken something or got lost.”

    “Sorry, I forgot to check my phone.”

    “It’s fine, I just wanted to make sure you’re okay.”

    "No worries, not dead yet.”

    “So, how is the writing going?” she heard Helen's smile.

The mug in her hand comfortably warmed her. “Pretty well,” she said, sounding more relieved than she wanted Helen to know. “I wrote a couple more chapters in the past days and I’m feeling inspired.”

    “Really?”

    “Yes,” Carol said and her thoughts turned to Zosia. She tightened her lips before she could tell Helen about her new friend. “I think it’s the fresh air away from the city.”

    “Right.” Carol could hear the smirk in Helen’s voice, proud that her suggestion was helping the writer.

    “I can send it to you by the end of the day if you want to take a look at it. I think I finally found what I’m gonna do with Raban this time.” Carol heard the rustling of papers on the line.

    “Good. It’s raining today. I…” She wanted to tell Helen about sailing with real-life Raban, but she didn’t want Helen to think she was distracted. Perhaps it was the case, but what her agent didn’t know couldn’t hurt her. “I don’t think I will come back to Albuquerque soon.”

    “Take the time you need. I managed to save us some time with the first chapters you sent me.”

    “They liked it?”

    “Yes, they were very pleased with it and think the fans will like it too.”

    "Of course they will like it. I could bring him back to life a tenth time and they wouldn't give a fuck as long as he's shirtless."
