    “Ok, I won’t bother you any longer. I’m glad you’re alive and don’t have a broken leg. Have a good -”

    “Helen,” Carol said abruptly.

    “Yeah?”

    Carol closed her eyes and shook her head. Nope. You’re not asking Helen how to know if someone is playing for your team. 

    “Thanks for checking in on me,” Carol cleared her throat.

    “Of course.”

     

    ***

     

    Carol spent the afternoon editing her most recent chapters, not seeing the time pass by, and sent them to Helen. Even four books into the series, she still found herself feeling sick at the idea of eyes that weren’t hers reading her work. 

    She stretched and got down to the kitchen. She poured herself a glass of wine to calm her nerves and stood in the back window, looking over the lake and sipping her drink when a soft pounding on the door startled her.

    She put down her glass on the counter and headed to the door.

    Zosia stood tall in the door window, covered by a yellow rainjacket. She waved at Carol.

    “Hi, Carol,” she smiled when the door was opened. She was absolutely adorable. “I brought you some vegetables from my garden.”

    She handed Carol a small basket filled with tomatoes, cucumbers and salad. “That is so nice of you,” Carol accepted the gift. “I don’t have anything to share with you,” she nervously scratched the back of her neck.

    “Oh, no need. I have a lot, I won’t be able to eat it all by myself anyway. And also, I wanted to thank you for giving me your book. I started reading it last night and I’m hooked. I couldn’t stop reading until very late at night,” she chuckled shyly.

    Carol flushed. “Oh, thanks. I… Thank you. I’m glad you like it.”

    “I love it,” she beamed. “Alright, well, I will leave you to your writing. I would invite you to sail again tomorrow, but the weather predictions say it will rain again for the next couple of days.”

    “Oh, really?” Carol had been so focused on her imaginary world and settling in this new environment that she had no idea what was happening in the outside world. 

    “Yes, there will be a big storm tomorrow night. So, be careful.”

    “Okay, thank you, Zosia. You too.”

    She looked at Carol one last time. The wet strands of brown hair falling from under the hood of her jacket were glued to the side of her face and neck. A vein was popping on her forehead as she smiled. Carol noticed a drop of water trail down her nose and settle on her upper lip. Carol watched it for a brief second and could only swallow hard. 

    “Bye,” Zosia smiled and went out into the rain. She jogged back to her cabin and disappeared through the back door.

    Carol closed the door, feeling her cheeks in flames. The effect this woman had on her was completely ridiculous. Sure, she was absolutely beautiful and had a heart of gold. Sure, she was the real-life version of her ideal woman. Still, it didn’t make any sense why she got so flustered at the sight of a raindrop on her lip. 

    As she put the basket in the kitchen, she looked through the window. Zosia’s cabin was dark. 

    Carol replayed the scene in her mind. She saw the tall woman standing on her porch. She imagined herself stepping on the tip of her toes to close the space between them and capture the raindrop with her mouth. She imagined how the contrast between the freshness of the water and the warmth and softness of her full lips would feel. 

    A shiver ran up her arms.

    Carol took a big gulp of her wine and refilled the glass to the rim. 

     

    ***

     

    Carol was only on her third glass of wine when the phone rang. It was Helen again.

    She took in a deep breath and took the call. “Hey,” she said.

    “Hi, Carol. Is now a good time?”

    “Sure,” Carol said. She was sitting in her living room, reading the last chapters of her novel. She closed the book and put it down on her lap.

    “I read the chapters you sent me.”

    A silence followed. Helen’s voice was clear and energized, but there was a certain restraint to it that told the writer there was something that had bothered her with the chapters. 

    Carol took a long sip of wine, readying herself for the worst. Helen was always pretty smooth with Carol when it came to feedback and her criticism always constructive, always to bring the best out of her texts. She was a talented manager: she could see the potential in stories that even the writers themselves were blinded to and she knew exactly how to talk to them. Still, it was such a personal thing to do, to give access to your mind to other people, that any negative feedback, no matter how constructive it was, was always difficult to hear.

    “So, what did you think?” Carol asked, her voice caught in her throat.

    “There are some really good things in this…”

    Great. She could sense the ‘but’ coming.

    “But,” Helen continued, “There is something I just don’t understand about Raban in your story so far. On one hand, I have to admit that he feels more real than he’s ever felt to me.”

    Carol nodded. Ok, this is not too bad.

    “And on the other hand, there is something about him that is just…. That just doesn’t make sense, Carol. I can see that you are trying to do something with him, I can see you want to take him somewhere, but it doesn’t feel like what you are creating with him is what you really want to. It doesn’t feel… Honest.”

    “Ok,” Carol cleared her throat. “I hear you.”

    Helen was silent for a long moment. The only sound was the rain tapping against the windows and roof of the cabin and the sound of Carol’s breathing. 

    “And I have to tell it to you, Carol, but this feels familiar. It’s the same feeling I had when you wrote the first novel.”

    She meant when Carol talked herself out of making Raban a woman and changed her into a man. It had taken a lot of work, but her mind had been made. Raban would never be a woman. Even if it was what she wanted the most.

    “You can tell them the truth, you know.” Helen’s voice was gentle and kind. “It’s not too late to -”

    “It’s not that, Helen,” Carol’s response was angrier than she intended. She hated herself every time she was mean to Helen, but her agent had struck a sensitive cord. Something that Carol kept buried very deep inside of her and left dormant in a fantasy world that would never see the light of day. “I just don’t know what to do with this fucking pirate anymore. Maybe I should just kill him for good. This will still sell more than if he’d been a woman.”

    “Carol…” She could hear the sadness in Helen’s voice and it made the writer tear up. She so wished she could do as she wanted, but the fear of the world knowing something so personal about her was sickening. Just the idea of revealing to the public that in her first draft, Raban was a woman made her want to throw up.

    “Look, I’m just gonna rewrite the whole thing. I don’t like this draft anyway.” Her voice was cold as she held back the tears. 

    “Don’t do that, Carol. Listen, I wrote down some notes on your doc and I will send it back to you. I just wanted to talk to you first. I think you can easily fix this. I see the potential, Carol. You’re doing great.”

    “Of course. And hey, Carol, I think people are gonna love it.” There was a gentle smile in her voice.

    Carol nodded. “Thanks.”

    She hung up and closed her eyes. She managed to will the tears back in and cleared her throat once more. She swallowed her emotions and buried them deep within with the rest of her sorrow.

    Carol got up and put the rest of the bottle of wine in the cabinet. Enough wine for tonight, she thought as she grabbed a bottle of whisky and filled a glass to the rim. 

    The next morning, Carol woke up lying on the carpet of the living room. The bottle of whisky was half empty on the coffee table and her glass had spilled on the floor and soaked through her t-shirt. The smell made her feel nauseous.

    She groaned as she sat up and rubbed her eyes. She could feel them puffed and red and she realized she had cried. She only allowed herself to release her emotions when she was entirely fucked up by alcohol.

    In the darkness of the bathroom, she let the warm water rain on her. Memories of the previous night flashed in her mind. She remembered Helen’s mention of her notes on the chapters and her stomach twisted. 

    It had taken so much effort for Carol to motivate herself to take this journey. To come here in the middle of the woods, to come to a house that was filled with happy memories with Helen, to come to a neighbourhood where people didn’t like her and cast her out of their communal activities. All to write this stupid book.

    She had been so desperate to find inspiration. And she had, after all, found inspiration.

    Images of Zosia crystalized in her mind. Her little wave through the window on her way to the deck, her kind smile and bright eyes when she looked at Carol and her lying down in the sun. The way the sun had kissed her skin like it needed to reflect on her body to be seen. Her standing in Carol’s porch, soaking wet, in a way that made her skin look embarrassingly delicious. 

    She had found inspiration, alright. Except, she wasn’t brave enough to go on this path. Meeting her, seeing her vision of Raban turn into this woman had been a poisoned gift. It had given her the story she was too a coward to write. Too afraid. It had made the desire to write the lesbian romance fantasy of her dreams almost reachable. It was right there, within reach. Carol looked through the window and noticed the rain was more violent now. The tree branches were restlessly flying in all directions. She saw a lightning strike above a mountain behind the lake that was followed by a loud thunder that made the land tremble. 

    Carol sighed and headed to the living room. She started a fire in the fireplace and sat on the couch, tapping her fingers on her thighs. 

    What was she going to do now? 

    Perhaps I should just go back home, she thought before dozing off.

    She woke up to complete darkness. The fire had almost died in the fireplace and the rain was even more violent than before. She took her cellphone flashlight to put wood in the fireplace and bring some warm light back.

    She headed to the kitchen and sighed as she filled a glass with whiskey. There was one thing she could do: get wasted and find comfort in being miserable. After all, next to writing, that was the thing she was best at.

    Old habits die hard, she thought as she brought the glass to her lips.

    A light tapping on the front door startled her, she almost dropped her glass.

    Who could this be?

    She swallowed, heart pounding in her chest. It was so dark, she couldn’t see anything through the windows. As she headed to the door, she thought that if someone came to murder her, it would save her a lot of trouble. 

    Carol threw the door open, revealing Zosia standing in her yellow jacket. She squinted her eyes, to make sure she wasn’t imagining her. “Zosia? What are you doing here?”

    “Sorry to bother you,” she smiled shyly. “I just… Well, I got bored and thought maybe you would also like to have some company during this power outage.”

    Carol blinked. She should say no. She needed to be alone in her despair.

    She nodded.

    “Uh, sure,” Carol stepped aside. “Come on in.”

    Chapter 5: confessions in the dark

    Chapter Notes

    thank you so much everyone for reading my little fic!! I really appreciate it :) and thank you to anyone leaving kudos and comments, it makes my day every time! enjoy :D

    “There you go,” Carol handed Zosia a glass of whisky, this one reasonably filled.

    Carol sat at a safe distance from her guest on the couch and dipped her lips in the amber liquid, watching as Zosia tasted the whisky. She choked, surprised by the strength of the drink, and tried to pass it as a cough. There was a perfect balance of adorable and so goddamn fuckable in Zosia that was absolutely fascinating to Carol.

"Amphibians are the creatures who enjoy rain the most. Rain provides them with the perfect environment for breeding. And, you know, even if I love to be on the boat, I like rain a lot.     What did Zosia mean by that?

    Carol swallowed hard and cleared her throat. “I wouldn’t know that. I’ve never read those books.”

    Zosia smirked. “Liar.”

    They shared a couple more drinks by the fire until the wee hours of the morning. The awkward interaction of earlier was still somehow lingering over them. Both seemed to be circling around each other, trying to understand what had happened and what it had meant. Both seemed to be walking on eggshells, but some things hadn’t changed. Carol’s quirky remarks, Zosia’s twinkling eyes driving Carol crazy. 

    She pushed down the awkwardness, the possible deception that a certain miscommunication had happened between them and decided that she would deal with that later. For now, she would enjoyed her neighbour's company through the power outage.

    They spent most of night telling each other funny stories of time spent in nature. There had been one story, though, that Carol hadn’t brought up.

    Camp Freedom Falls. 

    She had dropped enough trauma for the night, confessing her inner conflict over Raban’s gender. Her time spent in conversion therapy, where she was sent to by her mom as a teenager, could be discussed another time.     Zosia hadn’t lied. She couldn’t handle alcohol very well. Carol had suggested they stop drinking after the second glass, but the tall woman had insisted. And, well, Carol could not resist those glassy puppy eyes.

    Even if she was much more tolerant, Carol also felt slightly tipsy and, sip by sip, she found herself caring less and less about keeping a safe distance between the two of them.

    When it was time to head back, Zosia got up to her feet and stumbled around. Carol caught her with a firm arm around her waist. It was the closest they’d ever been. For a brief second, Carol looked up at the brunette and found pitch black eyes staring back. Her gaze drifted down to her slightly parted lips.

    “Thanks, Carol,” Zosia said as the writer helped her up the stairs to her cabin. She opened the front door and turned to face her neighbour, holding herself up with the help of the door frame. “And thanks for spending the night with me. I didn’t want to be alone in the dark.”

    Carol nodded. “The pleasure was all mine.”

    “And thank you for trusting me.” In the darkness, she could hear Zosia’s voice was tinged with sadness. “I hope that you can be comfortable writing what you love, one day.”

    Before Carol could reply, the brunette threw her arms around Carol and pulled her in a tight embrace. 

    Carol’s breath caught in her chest as their bodies came together. Pressed close against Zosia, Carol could feel how soft and comfortable she was. Her face was crushed in her long neck and the sweet smell of her, mixed with the remaining scent of fire and rain, made Carol's head spin. 

    Once the surprise passed, Carol hooked her arms around her shoulders. She didn’t want to pull Zosia closer to her, uncertain where she stood after that night, but it was impossible not to be drawn to her.

    Her hips brushed against Zosia’s as the brunette tightened her grip on Carol, if that was even possible. She hoped Zosia wouldn't be able to feel her pounding heart.

    Carol closed her eyes as she felt a gentle hand rub her back. When she bit her lower lip to hold back a whimper, she realized just how touch-starved she was and how the soft but fiery touch of this woman brought her back to life.

    Eventually, she forced herself out of Zosia’s embrace and smiled shyly at her.

    In the moonlight, Zosia’s eyes had never been darker. 

    “Goodnight,” Carol said, but her voice only came in a whisper.

    “Goodnight, Carol.”

    She walked down the porch and headed home. Looking over her shoulder, under the rain, in the dark, she saw Zosia’s eyes still burning on her.

    Zosia was breathless when she closed the door behind her.

    She took hold on the wall to steady herself and stood a moment in the darkness of her cabin, still feeling the warmth of Carol's body against her.

    She threw her rain jacket on a chair and fell on top of the bed, kicking off her clothes. Completely naked and shivering, she slid under the fresh covers and lied on her back. Slowly, her eyes adjusted to the darkness and she could see the patterns of the rain on the window’s reflection on the ceiling.

    Everything had changed. Everything she thought she knew, she could throw out of the window. 

    Every interaction, every gaze, every flush of the cheeks had a brand new meaning.

    All this time, Zosia had cursed herself for desiring a woman that was not only taken, but in a committed, long-term relationship with someone who received nothing but praise from the people she came across.

    Zosia was no home wrecker, it was the last thing she ever wanted to be, but she couldn’t help the way Carol made her feel. She tried to resist it but it was all in vain. She had wanted her since the first moment she'd set eyes on her. 

    Every brush of the fingers, every stare that held too long.

    She had thought Carol didn’t realize the effect she had on other women, that she was just a charmer.

    And Zosia was not immune to her charm. 

    At first, she’d told herself to let it go, that there were plenty of fish in the sea, but she couldn’t stop wanting to be near the woman. And it was a dangerous thing to do, to get this attached to a married woman.

    But she wasn’t married. Not anymore.

    We’re divorced now.

    Zosia’s breath caught in her throat.

    Of course, the misfortune of others didn’t make her happy, but she would be lying if she said that this unexpected turn of events didn’t make her feel much less miserable about this whole situation with Carol.

    It wouldn’t mean that Carol could be interested in her, she was well aware of that. But it changed things. There was a chance that she could be.

    Zosia rolled on her side, facing the window of the bedroom. She closed her eyes and listened to the thunder. Images of Carol flickered in her mind. 

    The writer observing her through her window, the shy glances on the boat. 

    Her mind rewinded to the first day she saw Carol and she replayed the scenes in her mind.

    Knowing Carol and Helen weren’t together anymore gave a new meaning to everything she thought she knew.

    9 days before…

    Henryk had told her when he had briefed her on all the stories of the neighbours that the tall Mauritanian man threw tasteful parties and that she shouldn’t think twice before accepting his invitations, but to keep in mind that he was a real Don Juan. 

    And he’d been right. Since her arrival, she’d attended two of his parties. The food and drinks were of high quality, the atmosphere joyous and she got to meet interesting people from the same area: a lawyer named Laxmi and her husband Aarush, who both owned a summer house down the street, and Kusimayu, the young woman who worked at the local farm market. She had had interesting conversations with them and she was looking forward to meeting them again, but today wasn’t the right day.

    Still, she told herself that after a bad day, it would do her good to see people, rather than to stay at home alone.

When he’d told her about Koumba’s parties, he also took the time to spill all the gossip about her closest neighbours. A man named Manousos who lived two houses down and her next door neighbours, Carol Sturka and Helen Umstead.

She was just starting to get back on her good intentions since she came to the lake house. She found that leaving her cigarette packs in the house while she went on the boat helped.

    Now Zosia realized why the name Carol Sturka had rung a bell when Henryk had mentioned it. She made a joke about her profession, but Zosia could see that Carol was genuinely happy to tell her what she did for a living. 

    She teased Carol again and found it adorable how easy it was for her guest to fall for it and get defensive. 

    They laughed together and Zosia couldn’t help but stare at the woman’s eyes. Everything she learned about her was more fascinating.

    “Anything I might have read?”

    “Have you heard of Winds of Wycaro?”

    Then she realized. 

    She could picture Josie lying down in her hotel room, book open on her lap while Zosia was getting ready to go out with the rest of the crew in London. She had joked that Zosia looked like the female version of the character on the book cover.

    Josie needed her alone time and Zosia respected that, though she thought they never had enough time together. The flight attendant always preferred to stay at the hotel and catch up with her romance novels rather than hang out with the team. When Josie had forced her to watch Outlander with her, Zosia had only obliged because she was crazy about her. 

    Josie hadn’t had time to finish the Carol Sturka novel before she quit her job without a word and left Zosia disillusioned and with a broken heart.

    Well, now that was funny. There was a poetic tragedy to it all and Zosia smiled. She listened closely as Carol talked about her books with genuine interest. It was adorable.

    “Wow… I’m sorry that I don’t know your books.” It wasn’t a lie. Josie hadn't gotten the chance to talk to her about it before she left. “I would love to read them, though. They seem very interesting.”

    When she got hot, Carol took off her white t-shirt. Zosia couldn’t help but watch. Her bright red swimsuit clung perfectly to the right places. She trailed her eyes from her strong shoulders, alongside her collarbone down to her cleavage. Her breath caught in her throat.

    Carol didn’t meet her eyes but Zosia saw a blush on her cheeks reflecting her own. 

    Carol changed the subject and her sabbatical was brought up. For a second, Zosia felt like someone had thrown a bucket of ice-cold water on her. She didn’t let it show and found solace in Carol’s eyes instead. 

     

    ***

     

    It was a perfect afternoon. The day was beautiful and the waters calm. They sailed away smoothly. Zosia even let Carol take the control, which seemed to please the woman. She couldn’t help but watch her, so close to her, as she drove them towards the river.

    Her shoulders had turned red under the sun and Zosia could see light freckles on her skin. It took every ounce of restraint she had to stop herself from reaching out and letting her fingers dance on her body. 

    They listened to music as they made their way up the river. Warm Ways played in the background and Zosia listened to Christine McVie’s voice. 

     

    You made me a woman tonight

    Dream until the morning light

    I, I’m waiting for the sun to come up

    I can’t sleep with your warm ways

     

    Zosia smiled. Carol’s sand coloured hair looked so soft around her face. There was a childish grin on her lips. She turned to Zosia, beaming.

     

    Forever, forever love

    Together, together love

    Zosia turned to look ahead. 

    She was totally screwed. 

     

    She couldn’t tell which footstep had thrown her over the edge, but she was falling. There would be a crash at the landing, but the fall made her head spin. “Head left here, I want to show you a place.”

    Zosia watched Carol as she read. She was lying on her stomach, completely captivated by her book. Her lips moved as her eyes rapidly passed the words.

    Zosia, on the other hand, was restless. She had been on the same page for the past ten minutes, her eyes much more interested in observing Carol than the investigation taking place in her book. Her burning gaze trailed from Carol’s mouth, down her toned back, to the curve of her bottom. Zosia swallowed hard and looked down at Carol’s toned legs. 

    She felt too hot, both from the sight of the woman next to her and the afternoon sun burning on her skin. It was infernal.

    “I’ll go swim a bit.”

    The contact of the fresh water immediately brought her spirits back. She let herself sink underwater and told herself that when she came back up the surface, all those thoughts would be gone. 

    Zosia emerged and pushed her wet hair away from her face.

    Carol was watching her with an amused smile.

    Fuck.

    “It’s very refreshing. You should come, Carol.”

    Carol frowned and shielded her eyes from the sun. “I don’t really like to swim in lakes. What if a fish bites my toe?”

    Zosia swimmed away, feeling ridiculous. There she was, losing her goddamn mind over a woman who was probably counting the days until she was reunited with her partner. She felt deception wash over her and decided to ignore it.

    She didn’t want to go there. No, she would only appreciate the company and the next time, she would come alone. Away from the temptation.

    Chapter 7: guilty as sin (part II)

    Chapter Notes

    second chapter out of two from Zosia's perfective...... enjoy!! (apologies for the typos as I proof read this myself and am sick at the moment lol) (also, it's quite a long chapter... sorry I'm a yapper I guess!)

    Zosia closed the front door behind her. 

    She was completely exhausted. From the sleepless night. From the burning sun. From fighting her own mind. 

    She was sure she had heatstroke. She wet a towel and went to lie down in bed, the cold fabric on her forehead and Carol’s book next to her. She closed her eyes and she slowly felt herself dozing off.

    When she woke up later, it was dark and crickets were chirping in the night. Zosia got up, feeling better. She headed to the kitchen and downed a tall glass of water. She cooked vegetables for dinner and enjoyed her meal in silence, eyes always darting back to the book resting on the table in front of her. 

    Once she was done with dinner, she took her sweet time cleaning her dishes before setting herself comfortably on the bright orange couch in the living room and opening the first pages. She moved through the chapters fast, hooked by Carol’s smart but fluid prose. 

    It felt strange to read Carol’s book, like she was entering her privacy. She had never been close in any way to a writer before, so reading the work of someone she knew -- of someone that she desired -– felt weirdly intimate.

    Zosia found herself captivated by the universe Carol had created. While she was a bit disappointed that the romance was canonically heterosexual, she couldn’t help but imagine Raban as a woman. It just came naturally to her. She found herself charmed by Lucasia, who, in many ways, reminded her of Carol. 

    She flipped through the pages, flushing at the love scenes between the main characters. The vivid descriptions of their love making made the blood rush through her system and her head spin as she imagined Carol imagining the scenes. Carol writing the scenes. 

    As she imagined herself and Carol as Raban and Lucasia.

    She often moved, switching her position after sitting the same way for too long. She heard the first drops of water tap on the roof and the rain rocked her as she read. She stopped for the night only when her vision became blurry and headed back to bed. 

    Lying in the darkness, she replayed scenes from the novel in her mind. Her hand slowly traveled down her stomach but stopped at the waistband of her pyjama shorts. 

    She had fallen over the edge, but there was still a line that she didn’t dare to cross. Zosia pushed her legs together and closed her eyes, her mind suddenly awake and her imagination running wild. 

    The morning after, Zosia regretted staying up so late. She was sleepy as she drove to town. She had booked a hot yoga class, thinking it would help her to calm her spirit and body. It helped a bit, but she still couldn’t get Carol and her damn novel out of her mind.

    On her way out of the yoga studio, she got a text from Koumba, inviting her to join him and Laxmi for brunch.

    She met with them at a fancy brunch spot in town. She parked the sedan on the main street and joined them inside, where they were already sitting at a table for three by the window. 

    “Good morning,” she beamed as she hung her rain jacket on a hook.

    Koumba got up and kissed Zosia’s knuckles. “Zosia, so honoured to have you join us.” He pulled the chair out for her and helped her sit.

    A waitress came and filled her cup with coffee. She added two milks. 

    “So, we haven’t seen you around much,” Laxmi said. 

    “I’ve been spending every day on the lake,” Zosia smiled. 

    “I can tell. You have the most magnificent tan, ma chérie,” Koumba batted his eyes at Zosia.

    They ordered brunch: eggs and potatoes, topped with fresh fruits from the market for Zosia. She ate silently while she listened to Koumba and Laxmi tell stories about the previous parties she had missed. 

    There was a knot in her stomach from holding back from asking the burning questions on her lips. Both of them knew Carol and Helen. If she wanted to know more about their life together, what their deal was, she could simply ask them. And she would get a better understanding, a clearer view of what she was getting herself into. She knew that it would make sense of what was going on with Carol, while she was also afraid that what she’d learned would hurt her.

    She pushed the urge down and half-listened to the stories. Luckily for her, she was very good at showing interest while mentally being somewhere else entirely.

    “Ah, I guess that with all this, I do miss Helen a bit. Maybe if she could come back soon.”

    At the mention of Helen’s name, she was brought back right in.

    “If only she could’ve come instead of Carol,” Laxmi sighed.

    Zosia swallowed hard, feeling her mouth completely dry. “You said that usually they are always together. Do you know why this time she’s not here?”

    Komba shrugged. “Mystery to me. Maybe they separated. Carol looked miserable when I saw her.”

    “She always does, though,” Laxmi added.

    Koumba couldn’t disagree. “If you find out,” he told Zosia, “I want to know.”

    “How are they? As a couple I mean.”

    Koumba and Laxmi watched her. They didn’t comment on her strange, personal questions, but were happy to gossip with someone who wasn’t in on all the talk of the town.

    “Well, they seemed to be a happy couple. They didn’t really show any physical affection. I have to admit it took me a moment to realize they were a couple.”

    Koumba was a man after all, Zosia knew he was biased and probably didn’t care to notice the obvious. 

    “But Carol always stayed close to Helen. You could sense that she was… her grounding force.” He said, thoughtful, before grinning. “How poetic was that?”

    Laxmi rolled her eyes and they joked together. 

    Zosia couldn’t hear what they were saying over the ringing in her ears. She stared at the rain falling against the window of the restaurant and heard their laughter in the distance.

    The knot in her stomach was real. She felt the lump get back in her throat, but her mouth was too dry to swallow it. She took a sip of water.

    She had been a fool. 

    Of course Helen was Carol’s grounding force. Of course Carol wanted to be around Helen all the time. 

    Zosia realized her fist was tightly curved into a bump and her nails were digging in her skin. She released the pressure and tried to change her mind.

    “Oh, yes,” Koumba lifted his hands. “You want to know something funny?” he asked Zosia, chuckling as he looked over at Laxmi knowingly. “Otgonbayar said he saw Carol on your boat. Can you believe the man?” They laughed it off, but Zosia frowned. 

    “What do you mean?” Zosia was confused.

    “Well, no one goes boating with you. What the hell would Carol Sturka, of all people, be doing on your boat?”

    Laxmi studied Zosia’s face. Her smile dropped when she realized the old man had not been spreading rumours. Koumba wasn’t so quick to realize.

    “Carol went boating with you?” It was a question that was said like a statement.

    “Yes,” Zosia said faintly. 

    Koumba and Laxmi exchanged a look. “Oh,” he said, leaning closer to Zosia, “I didn’t realize you two were friends.”

    “We’re just neighbours. If you’ll excuse me, I need to get going. I still have a lot to do. Thank you for asking me to join you.”

    Zosia went to pay and walked out of the restaurant. She ran to her car and sat behind the wheel. She didn’t start the engine just yet. She stayed in the silence of the car, rocked by the rain. 

    It had always been only a matter of time before her illusion fell apart. 

    That’s what you get for falling for someone that’s married.

    Zosia started the car and drove away, lost in her thoughts. When she got back home, she spent the afternoon working in her garden. The muddy work and rain cleared her mind and allowed the lump to slowly pass.

    She should stay away from Carol, she knew it. But as treacherous as it was, she knew there was one thing that she could do that would cheer her up. 

    And it was to see Carol.

    So she filled a basket with the prettiest vegetables from the garden and headed to her home. She could be a good friend to Carol. They had shared good moments. Longing for her and for her touch had taken over Zosia’s mind, but she was certain that if she took some distance and saw the bigger picture, with time, she would be able to feel normal about Carol and be a good friend to her.

    She softly tapped on the door and saw Carol approach. The writer opened the front door and smiled at the sight of her. 

    It warmed her heart.

    “Hi, Carol.”

    As she handed the basket to Carol, Zosia felt a drip of water run down the back of her neck, sending shivers down her spine.

    “That is so nice of you,” Carol accepted the gift and observed the content of the basket. “I don’t have anything to share with you,” she scratched the back of her neck.

    “Oh, no need. I have a lot, I won’t be able to eat it all anyway. And also, I wanted to thank you for giving me your book. I started reading it last night and I’m hooked. I couldn’t stop reading until very late at night,” she chuckled shyly.

    Carol flushed. “Oh, thanks. I… Thank you. I’m glad you like it.”

    “I love it,” Zosia beamed. There was so much she wanted to tell Carol about her book, but she found herself lost for words.  “Alright, well, I will leave you to your writing. I would invite you to sail again tomorrow, but the weather predictions say it will rain for the next couple of days.”

    She cursed herself. What the hell was she saying, talking about the weather? Was this her definition of what being a friend was?

    “Okay, thank you, Zosia. You too.”

    Her eyes lingered on Carol. Before she even felt it, Zosia noticed the deep blue of Carol’s eyes turn to dark as it followed a raindrop that slid down Zosia’s face and settled on her top lip. 

    Surely, she wasn’t making this up? Carol’s eyes were hungrily looking at her mouth and she wet her lips before swallowing hard.

    Zosia’s entire body went inflamed. It took everything for her to stop herself from closing the space between them, pressing her mouth against Carol’s and pushing her inside the cabin.

    “Bye,” she forced a smile and ran away in the rain before she could do something she would regret.

    Perfect way of showing her my friendship, Zosia thought.

    Zosia paced around the house. She didn’t know what to think anymore. She desperately needed to know why Carol, who had a grounding force of a partner, had looked at her mouth like it was the first meal she was seeing after starving for weeks.

    There was clearly something she was missing out and it was driving her insane. She was already too involved in this. Her desire for her neighbour had increased too much in the past days and it was now out of control. The flames consumed every piece of her they came across. 

    It was too late to get back, but at the same time, whatever was going on between them made her head spin.

    It made her feel depressed just as she felt intoxicated.

    Rain kept falling for the rest of the day and long into the night. The following morning, she woke up to the sound of the drops tapping on her window. She tried to keep her mind occupied during this slow day. She cleaned the cabin and cooked a soup with the vegetables from her garden.

    When the afternoon came, she found herself bored to death. She had occupied herself throughout the morning to keep her mind away from Carol, but the temptation to throw herself back in the book was too strong.

    She eventually surrendered to it and made it to the end of the novel. She was left wanting for more. There was something in reading a romance book written by a woman she was attracted to that excited her.  

    To change her mind, she put on a record and let the music comfort her. The lonely chords of The Blue Nile appeased her, but only briefly.

    The music was cut abruptly when the power went down and Zosia was left in complete silence. 

    She put on her rainjacket and stood on the back porch, hiding under the small roof to smoke a cigarette. The rain was pouring down and clearing the entire area of any other sounds. She watched the drops violently fall on the grey lake. The trees were dripping and the grass was bright green. Nature was happy with the rain.

    Zosia held the cigarette between her lips as she lit the tip and took a long inhale. 

    There was a peaceful quietness to the scene that managed to soothe her worries. She had been so caught up in the rapid growth of her feelings for Carol that she hadn’t taken the time to stop and put things into perspective.

    A glance in the direction of the writer’s cottage showed her that there was no sign of life in the windows, but the car was parked in the gravel lot. Surely, her neighbour was home. She wondered what Carol was up to, if she got easily bored like Zosia.

    Probably not. She was probably reading by the bay window overlooking the lake or working on her next book. Zosia flushed as memories from her reading session flooded back.

    To ignore the growing flushing of her cheeks, she took another inhale.

    A tempting thought crossed her mind. She tried to push it away, but it was dangerous and thrilling. It made her stomach turn into knots. 

    Zosia crushed the cigarette in an ashtray and headed back inside before the temptation pulled her across the backyard, up Carol’s stairs.

    When night came, she told herself there was no reason to fight it. Even if she weren’t attracted to Carol, it would be a normal thing for neighbours to do, to hang together during a power outage. There was nothing weird about it. And if she were being honest with herself, the complete darkness of the forest, the rain making it impossible to hear anything else around made her feel uncomfortable. She would much rather have company.

    She threw on her rain jacket and ran all the way to Carol’s house. There was a low flickering of orange light coming through the windows. She gently tapped on the door, not to frighten her.

    Carol opened the door and looked over at her. She squinted her eyes. “Zosia? What are you doing here?”

    For a second, she thought perhaps this had been a terrible idea and that Carol shared this belief.

    “Sorry to bother you. I just… Well, I got bored and thought maybe you would like to pass this power outage with some company, too.”

    Carol blinked and for a moment, Zosia’s heart dropped in her chest when she thought that the blonde woman would send her home. Until, eventually, she nodded.

    “Uh, sure,” Carol stepped aside to let Zosia in. “Come on in.”

    Zosia followed her inside. Carol closed the door behind her and locked it. “Uh, you can hang your coat on the hook here.”

She followed Carol into the cabin who pointed at the living room. “I was just having a drink. Do you want one? You want one.”

    “Uh, sure, thanks.”

    Zosia sat by the fireplace while Carol fixed her a drink. The cold rain had made its way through her bones and she was shivering. Yet, inside Carol’s cabin, in Carol’s presence, she immediately felt more at peace than alone in her own cold, dark cabin. 

    “Your place is cozy,” she smiled, looking around at the wooden walls. “It’s more rustic. Henryk and Iza renovated a few years back and now it’s all modern. I mean, don’t get me wrong, I’m not complaining, but I like yours better.”

    “There you go.” Carol came behind her and handed her a full glass of brown liquor before sitting across from her on the couch.

    They took a sip and Zosia tried to make it pass easily, but it burned down her throat. Her attempt at looking cool failed and she cleared her throat, wiping the corner of her mouth with her thumb.

    “You must be excited to get back on your boat,” Carol said after a moment of comfortable silence. 

    “Yes! But you know, rain is important for the ecosystem and I will go along with what nature needs.”

    They stayed silent again. Zosia sipped her whisky, her eyes intent on the flames in the fireplace. Her head had already started to spin. She would have to slow down on the whisky if she didn’t want to embarrass herself in front of Carol. 

    The thunder roared in the distance and the echoes in the mountains shook the cabin. 

    She finally brought up the subject of her book in the most casual way she could. She obviously left out the delirious thoughts it had put in her head for the past two days.

    “Stormshadow of Wycaro. How fitting. From Bestselling author Carol Sturka.”

If Josie had noticed, clearly the writer could tell that some traits were familiar. He reminded Zosia of a very specific picture of herself, taken in front of the Eiffel tower, many, many years ago during her first stopover in Paris.

Perhaps it would be a good way to continue to get to know each other better after their boat trip. Perhaps it would be a good way to get some answers to her burning questions. And she felt so comfortable around Carol, she wanted to tell her everything. “To be fair, it was a good colleague who recommended it to me. I would not have thought to watch it if it wasn’t for her passion for it.”

    Carol took a sip of her whisky. “Uh… Sure.”

    “When you told me you were a romance writer… Well, I assumed…” she smiled shyly. “Well, my brother told me about you and your partner. Helen, right?”

    She circled back. “I guess, what I’m trying to say is I assumed you wrote…” Zosia’s voice trailed off. Carol seemed troubled. Zosia felt her chest squeeze hard. She had clearly overstepped. “Sorry, is this too personal?” 

    “No, no,” she replied after a long moment. “It’s okay.” 

    Carol was lost looking inside her glass. Zosia could see the thoughts spin in her head. For what seemed like an eternity, Zosia held a breath, waiting.

    Carol finally cleared her throat and looked up at Zosia. “I first wrote Raban as a woman, but I talked myself out of it. I was too afraid.” 

    The rain echoed through the cabin. Zosia was speechless. 

    Carol smiled sadly. “Still am.”

    Zosia reached out and pressed Carol’s warm hand in hers. “I’m sorry, Carol.”

    She felt so terrible, forcing a confession like this out of Carol. 

    “It’s okay.”

    Zosia searched her eyes. It was impossible to see what the woman was thinking, but she saw a light in the blue irises that she loved so much. Suddenly, the grip on her chest released. She gave Carol’s hand a soft squeeze. “Thank you for telling me that.”

    She smiled reassuringly as Carol looked deep into Zosia’s eyes. Carol nodded.

    This felt too intimate. Too right and too wrong.

    “Well, at least you can share your life with someone who is supportive. Iza told me that Helen was very kind. And fun.”

    She didn’t know why she had to break this moment, but her curiosity got the best of her. Once more, the words that had spilled out of her mouth weren’t her own.

    Zosia anticipated the cold shower that would soon follow. A light in Carol’s eyes at the mention of a woman that wasn’t herself, a confession of a love that crossed decades and the hardships of life but remained stronger. 

    She was ready for all her hopes and desires to be crushed.

    “We’re divorced now.”

    Zosia was pretty sure her heart skipped a beat. She blinked and felt a warmth burn up her neck and settle into her cheeks. “Oh,” she frowned.

    Confusion clouded her mind. Carol had confessed this so plainly and the reality of her relationship with Helen had been so unexpected that she was at loss for words. 

    Of course, she had hoped that Carol would be in a position to be interested in Zosia. Every time Zosia felt her eyes on her, she became alive. Yet, a confession that should have made her hopeful and happy weirdly saddened her.

    She had clearly got Carol to share a painful confession and for what? For her own benefits? She had never felt more like a selfish prick than at this very moment in her entire life. Zosia felt ashamed of herself, but Carol’s eyes were so sweet on her, despite the great pain in them. 

    Her cheeks burning red, she told herself that she didn’t even deserve to touch Carol. “I’m so sorry.” She slowly released Carol’s hand.

    The pain she saw in Carol’s eyes was ten times more painful than if she’d said that she and Helen were so happy together and that she was coming over next week. Zosia had completely left out the possibility that they weren’t together anymore, but that Carol was still heartbroken by it and wasn’t looking to fall in love again.

    Zosia frowned, looking away. 

    How could have I been so ridiculous?

    Once the shock of the revelation passed, so many questions rushed through her mind. What had led to this divorce? Was it recent? Who had ended the marriage? Was it a mutual decision?

    Zosia looked up and saw Carol had turned her body away from her. Shame washed over her. She wanted to blame it on the alcohol, but she knew it was her own selfish desires that had made her cross that line. “I’m sorry,” Zosia cleared her throat. “I… I overstepped. I don’t handle alcohol very well.”

    Carol shook her head, distant. “It’s okay. You couldn't have known.”

    Her head spined. She had lost her balance and didn’t know what to say to make things better. How to fix this misstep.

    “I was engaged once.” Was the first thing to come to her mind. 

    A confession for a confession. Even if Carol hadn’t asked her anything.

    Carol’s eyebrows shut up. Clearly, that revelation surprised her. She turned her head to look at Zosia. “Really? What happened?”

    Zosia went back in the memory and slowly, her blood began to flow normally through her body. Carol was attentively listening, her kind eyes making her relax. Perhaps she’d been over dramatic. They were, after all, getting to know each other better. These were normal questions.

    She got more confident and told Carol the story of her fiancé. She didn’t know why, perhaps she wanted to go slow after the missteps of the night, but she kept his gender blurry, even when Carol seemed to hand her an opportunity. 

    “You weren’t in love with… with them?” Carol asked.

    Zosia shrugged. “I thought I was at the time. Now, I know I wasn’t,” she admitted. Zosia brought her glass to her lips and they stayed silent.

    Carol sighed as she smiled, lost in her thoughts.

    “You said the fans must be beaming over Raban. You didn’t?”

    Zosia laughed. She wasn’t expecting this question, but she was happy that the conversation was going in a lighter direction. “He’s… He’s certainly something.”

    “Meaning?”

    “Well,” Zosia’s cheeks had turned crimson. More images of the book flashed in her mind. “I suppose it would be fair to say that someone so tall and dark might be irresistible, but…” she trailed off. She looked up at Carol. Time for another confession. “I found myself more captivated by Lucasia.”

    Carol watched Zosia, trying to read her face. Perhaps she had come off too strong, but Carol’s lingering and darkening eyes on the tall woman couldn’t lie. 

    Perhaps Zosia hadn’t been the only one urging to get to know each other better. 

    There was only one way to find out.

    Zosia teased Carol and the way she reacted made her stomach twist. She played it all in. At this point, she had nothing to lose.

    “What can I say… She knows how to go get what she wants.”

    Carol’s eyes quickly met Zosia and she held her gaze. Her slightly parted lips told Zosia that she had caught on the double meaning.

    “I wouldn’t know that. I’ve never read those books,” Carol finally said after a silence.

    Zosia smirked. “Liar.” 

    After this strange conversation, Carol downed her glass. She offered Zosia to keep her company for another drink. She should say no. She was already ranting too much, but she enjoyed the moment too much to sober up.

    They shared a few more glasses – she lost count – and felt her head spin. She would pay for this in the morning, but it didn’t matter. After those confessions in the dark, she saw Carol in a different light. 

    They shared funny stories of their adventures in nature, but it was mostly superficial. The liquid courage made her feel bold and she was determined to show her affection to Carol, who, unlike her, seemed to handle her alcohol like a pro. She teased Carol and Carol’s quirky remarks suddenly had a different meaning. Like she was also trying to get something to Zosia.

    While she didn’t want the night to end, Zosia suddenly felt fatigue take over her body. All the emotions that had washed over her in the past days suddenly released into a comfortable numbness. “I should get back,” she hiccuped. She got to her feet and lost her balance. “Oops -”

    She was surprised by Carol’s solid arm around her waist. The touch was electric. Her entire body came alive at the way their bodies connected so naturally.

    When Carol looked up at Zosia, she found blue eyes completely dark. Many dangerous thoughts crossed her mind and she was too disinhibited to control them.

    It was Carol’s gaze who fell first. “Alright, I’ll help you home.” 

    As she gently helped Zosia in her jacket, the brunette hoped that when morning came, she would remember every detail about that night. Every brush of fingers as Carol helped her dress and the firm hold of her waist as she carried her back home. 

    When they reached the porch, words came out of her. She couldn’t help it. When she knew Carol probably wasn’t ready to hear more of what she had to say, she closed her arms around Carol and pressed her against her.

    She closed her eyes at the contact, breathing in the sweet smell of her hair and felt the other woman’s body warm her. When Carol’s arms closed around her shoulders, she felt a shiver run down her spin. When her hips brushed with Carol’s, she had to hold back a sigh.

    Surely, she wasn’t making this up. This had to mean something. Zosia grabbed Carol tighter and rubbed her back. 

    She never wanted this moment to end, but Carol eventually released herself from the embrace. She smiled shyly at Zosia and it took every inch of her will not to devour the shorter woman’s mouth.

    “Goodnight,” Carol whispered.

    “Goodnight, Carol.”

    Carol walked down the porch and headed home. Zosia’s gaze held her until she closed the door of her house behind her.

    Zosia closed her eyes, feeling out of breath as she got back inside.

    Her mind was clouded with the whisky and she knew everything would make more sense in the morning, but she couldn’t help it. She replayed the scene in her mind. The confessions. 

    Everything had changed.

    Chapter 8: alpenglow

    Chapter Notes

    thank you so much for the lovely comments on the previous chapters, they really made my day thank youuu

    Things went back to normal after the power was restored. 

    The clouds cleared, the birds began to sing again and people came out of the isolation of their houses.

    Everything went back to normal, except Carol and Zosia’s relationship. 

    After their drunken exchange, Carol wasn’t so sure where they stood. 

    Had Zosia friendzoned her or had she flirted with her? She couldn’t really say, even if the way she had hugged her was probably more than friendly. 

    Did Zosia even remember what happened that night? She had been quite drunk when Carol had left her at her place.

    Carol worried about her the following morning. What if she’d been sick? She wanted to go check in on her, see if she was feeling alright or needed anything, but she worried maybe the brunette wouldn’t want to see her right away.

    So Carol stayed home, did some laundry and paced around restlessly, ignoring Helen’s notes on her chapters.

    Eventually, at around noon, Zosia walked out of her house and headed to her garden. She looked as resplendent as usual. When she disappeared behind the corner of her cabin without shooting a look over to Carol’s place, she felt disappointed.

    She thought about going outside, pretending to take a break in one of the Adirondack chairs, but the truth was: she wanted to avoid Zosia as much as she wanted to talk to her.

    There were too many questions raised and there was an option of what had actually happened that she wasn’t ready to face, no matter how low the chances of it being true were.

    Zosia was simply too good to be true: she was her ideal woman in flesh and blood. She was kind and easygoing. She made everything feel so effortless and made Carol think that she could possibly want to try and give love another chance. She always made her feel like she really wanted to be in Carol’s presence, and that she hadn’t felt in a very long time.

    A part of her worried that this was all an illusion. If she risked opening the door to feelings, then she risked heartbreak when Zosia would tell her that the feelings weren’t mutual. That she could never want her the same way Carol wanted her. 

    She had learned to keep her heart secure from the moment her first declaration of love had sent her straight to camp Freedom Falls. Of course, things were different now. If Zosia didn’t feel the same way, she certainly wouldn’t make Carol feel offensive for wanting her. She knew it, but she simply couldn’t face the idea of swallowing this shame back up again.

    Because even after all these years, after nurturing a real and honest love with Helen, after disguising romantic stories into heterosexual fiction, after almost drinking herself to death on multiple occasions, that pain was still an open wound. 

    So, they didn’t see each other for the following days. 

    Tired and morose, Carol stopped setting an alarm and started drinking earlier than before. She didn’t get much writing done and decided to completely delete the chapters she’d written without even checking Helen’s notes. 

    She was too ashamed to face it. Helen had seen right through her and it was humiliating. She was convinced Helen must think of her as a coward. Even after everything she’d lost in their divorce, she hadn’t learned to be strong and still wasn’t working on becoming her authentic self. 

    For a couple of days, Carol strolled around the house. She still went to town to buy the third Wycaro novel, just in case. 

    To make sure no one would recognize her buying her own book – and because she thought a change of scenery would do her good – she went to a nearby town and spent the day there, wandering around and reading on the terrace of a café.

    As she drove back home, she passed the local country club and thought perhaps playing golf would be a good way to change her mind and occupy her restless self. She was still rusty, not having played in at least a year.

    The sun was still up when she parked her car in the driveway. Zosia was still away on her boat. Carol found a dusty practice putting mat in the shed and set it in the straightest part of the backyard. 

    Carol was so focused on getting the techniques right, rolling the ball at perfect speed down the hole, that she didn’t hear the sound of the motor coming back to port.

    “Oh, hi, Carol!”

    The sweet voice made her jump. She turned and saw Zosia stand in loose sports shorts and a loose blue shirt, opened to her swimsuit top and glorious body.

    Carol’s grip on the putter involuntarily tightened and she forced her eyes to stay above Zosia’s shoulders. Staring right back at those soft brown eyes wasn’t much easier. 

    “Hi,” she said, her voice hoarse from staying silent all day long.

    “I was starting to think you were avoiding me,” Zosia observed Carol. Her voice was so neutral, the writer couldn’t tell if she was joking or confronting Carol.

    “I’ve been busy.”

    Zosia smiled at the golfing set. “You golf?”

    Cleary, she’d been busy with important things. Carol wanted to roll her eyes at herself.

    It was obvious she did play golf, but no witty remark came to her mind. “Yeah, I want to go again soon, so… I’m just warming up.”

    “I haven’t played in ages.”

    “You play?”

    Zosia shrugged and walked up to her. “I played a couple of times, but it was a very long time ago. I’m probably very rusty too. I could play again, though.”

    She held Carol’s gaze. 

    Does she want me to ask her to come with me?

    “You must be good,” she pointed at Zosia with her putter. “Very tall and all.”

    Zosia smiled, pleased. “I was a natural. I have an idea. If you come to a hot yoga class with me, I’ll go play a game of golf with you. This way, you are sure to win anyway.”

    Carol finally smiled. Zosia was as friendly as usual; her brown eyes twinkling with excitement at the smallest things of life, talking actively with her hands and creating moments to spend with Carol. It was a big relief for the writer. She cursed herself for waiting this long before talking to the brunette and breaking the ice.

    “I don’t want an easy win,” Carol crossed her arms.

    “Oh, I never said it would be easy,” Zosia lifted an eyebrow.

    Carol pondered and smiled. “What the hell, why not? I don’t get why you want me to come to yoga so bad, unless it’s to see me wearing tight pants,” she joked.

    Zosia laughed, unfazed by the underlying meaning, and took a step too close to Carol. Carol held her breath as Zosia lifted her arms and her warm hands rested on her trapezius. “Almost,” she smirked and her strong fingers gave a light squeeze that made Carol wince, “because you are too stiff.”

    “Ooch!” she instinctively lifted her shoulders to release the muscle from Zosia’s grip. 

    “Exactly what I mean,” Zosia smiled, pleased with herself, and let her arms fall on each side of her body. She took a step back. “There’s a class tomorrow at 11 that I like to take. Join me and we can have brunch after.”

    The prospect of having plans tomorrow after days of boredom gave Carol a burst of energy. Not to mention plans with Zosia… The image of Zosia doing hot yoga hadn’t left her mind since she’d first mentioned it the night of the power outage, and now she would get to see it with her own eyes.

    Carol felt her cheeks flush. “Sounds good.”

    Zosia smiled proudly. “Wonderful. I’ll see you tomorrow morning then. Have a good evening, Carol.”

    She started trailing back to her cabin.

    “Wait!” 

    Zosia turned around. 

    “What about golf?”

    “You tell me,” she smiled and headed home.

    The following morning, Carol got out of the house and locked the door behind her. She walked to the parking lot and waited after Zosia. It was still quite early and the freshness of the forest made her feel cold. She only wore loose sports shorts and a light sweater.

    Chapter 9: fireworks

    Chapter Notes

    I meant to post this way earlier than this but between life and ao3 being down....
    thank you for reading and to everyone leaving comments you have no idea how happy they make me!!

    Carol wrapped the blanket around her shoulders as she watched Zosia push the logs around the fire pit with a wood stick. It was a chilly night, but the fire kept her warm. 

    Or was it the presence of the woman sitting next to her?

    Zosia sat back in the chair with a happy sigh and gaze up at the stars shining bright in the darkness of the night. She took a sip of wine and seemed to relax. She wore a loose, worn out hoodie and her hair was messily tied in a bun. 

    She had never looked more beautiful.

    She flashed a smile at Carol, clearly already tipsy on her second glass of wine.

    “This is nice,” she said, closing her the around her shoulders. “I wish we’d done it sooner.”

    Carol let her head fall to the side, holding her gaze. She wasn’t even intoxicated, she just didn’t want to fight it anymore. She wanted to show Zosia that she cared about her, that she found her fascinating. That she wanted to know everything about her. That she was the prettiest woman she’d ever laid eyes on. That every time she was with her, she felt truly content and peaceful. 

    “We’ll have other cold nights,” she smiled, her voice low. 

    “You are right. Maybe tomorrow we could do it again after our day on the lake.”

    She had a child-like wonder and it softened Carol’s hardened heart. Everything she talked about, she was passionate about it. Carol had never heard her say one negative thing since she’d first met her. 

    “How did you get to start driving boats?” Carol asked her.

    “It was my father who taught me. My city is a port city so I was always watching the ships coming and going, wondering where they were heading. My dad owned a small boat. It was nothing like Henryk’s boat. It was an old thing and it smelled like fish -- at the time, we were living pay check to pay check -- but he loved it,” she spoke with bright, shiny eyes at the fond memory. “And he loved to take us with him. Only my mother never went because she got seasick.”

    “Do you have other siblings? Besides Henryk?”

    Zosia froze and sadness took over her features.

    Carol immediately felt the shift in Zosia and her heart dropped. “I’m sorry, did I say something wrong?”

    Zosia shook her head and cleared her throat. “I’m sorry. No, you didn't. I... I had another brother, but he died.”

    Oh.

    “Oh, Zosia, I’m so sorry,” Carol reached across and gently grabbed Zosia’s arm to show her support. Carol wasn’t really a family person. She had a small family she wasn’t close to, but she could see in her sad eyes that it wasn’t the case for Zosia and the pain in her translated the feeling directly to Carol’s bones.

    “Thank you,” Zosia smiled sadly and put a warm hand over Carol’s. She linked their fingers together and they held hands.

    “Do you want to talk about it?”

    Zosia shook her head. “Not for now, but thank you.”

    Carol nodded and they stayed silent, hands linked, while Zosia let the sadness pass.

    She cleared her throat and went on with her story. “Anyway, that’s how I started to like boats. I wasn’t able to have one in New York, obviously, and I didn’t have the time to own one anyway. But everywhere I went, when we stopped in different towns for work, I liked to take boat tours. I find it’s a good way to visit new cities. But, I have to say, there’s nothing like the feeling of being behind the wheel.”

    Carol smiled at her.

    “And what about you?”

    “No, it’s not that.”

    Carol was no expert, but she could see clearly that these were serious scars and that they seemed to be quite recent. They seemed to be entirely healed, but there was still a pink tinge to them.

    The corners of Carol’s mouth curled up. She had to be honest, Zosia’s interest in her writing flattered her.     “Honestly? Not so well,” Carol sighed. “I’m a bit lost at the moment. I haven’t written much since I got here. And what I wrote, I pretty much threw it away.”

    Carol looked away at the riverside. She watched the houses where kids played outside while the families watched from the terrace, sipping cocktails at 2 in the afternoon.

    It was true. After her talk with Helen, she had decided to kill Raban once and for all. She’d written a few pages, but then she came back to her senses and realized she didn’t really want to get rid of him.

    She had ignored Helen’s calls in the past days and she knew that in her Albuquerque office, Helen was mad as hell at Carol. She hated to be doing this, but Carol couldn’t lie to her and she didn’t want Helen to pressure her even more than she already did. This time, it wouldn’t help.

    “Oh, I’m sorry.” Zosia was thoughtful. “It must be difficult to do that. Write when you have an established fanbase waiting for the next story.”

    “When I try to give them what they want, I go against my will. It worked for the first four books, but this time… I don’t know.”

    “Well, why don’t you try to write what you want?" She asked like it was the easiest thing in the world. Carol looked over at Zosia who was smiling at her, encouraging and confident. “They will see the truth in it and they will love it.”

    “I don’t know about that.”

    “Well, you won’t know until you try it.” 

    Carol nodded, but she knew Zosia couldn’t understand. She herself had admitted that she mostly loved the books because she knew Carol. The fans, they loved the books because they wanted Lucasia and Raban to be together. And they wanted to imagine there could be a charming loverboy, a real man or whatever their fantasy called for, like him somewhere waiting for them. Not to read about a lesbian romance with Raban now turned into a woman.

    Yet, she wasn’t doing anything with her writing right now.

    She got herself distracted by Zosia, and as much as she had told herself that it was ‘research’ in the beginning, enough time had passed that she could admit it wasn’t exactly true. She just wanted to be around Zosia. She was catching feelings for her and she hid away from her responsibilities in the warmth of her presence.

    What did she have to lose? She would throw it away at the first chance she got, anyway.

    So that night, when they parted ways, Carol got home, sat in front of her computer and tried it. Just to see how it would feel.

    She let herself write Raban as a woman.

    Chapter 12: prince charming

    Chapter Notes
