    She finally looked up and met Helen’s eyes. She was invested in Carol’s story and there was a glimmer of compassion for both parties in her eyes.

    “And then, she was silent the whole way home. She held on to me all night long and only started crying again when she was asleep. The next morning, she was acting like nothing had happened. There was such a clean cut between the two events that for a moment I thought I’d imagined it all, but when I drove by that road the following morning, I saw the animal rescue picking up the dead body.”

    Carol ended the story, taking a long sip of whiskey and putting down her glass, catching the bartender’s attention. She signed him for a second one.

    “Wow,” Helen finally said.

    The bartender nodded at Carol and started pouring another drink for her.

    “I feel maybe she’s not telling me everything, because… Because she’s not telling herself everything.”

    “I mean, what she’s been through must be very traumatic. I can imagine it will take a while to process it. Maybe she’s still in denial.”

    Carol shrugged. “I suppose so. I don’t know what to tell her. I don’t know how to be around her anymore.”

    “Maybe don’t bring it up. Be there for her and be honest with her. Give her the space to work her way through these feelings.”

    Carol felt her cheeks turn bright red. It was true that if there was one thing she hadn’t given Zosia since they met was space. She wanted to be around her all the time.

    But then again, when it wasn’t Carol reaching out to Zosia, it was Zosia reaching out to her.

    The bartender brought her new glasses and Carol absent-mindedly mumbled a ‘Thank you’. She could only think about the beginning of their friendship. Zosia was always coming to Carol and inviting her. 

    Carol had thought it was because she wanted to be with her.

    What if it was simply because she didn’t want to be alone?

    Carol swallowed down her entire glass. “I need to sleep. Do you mind if I leave you?”

    “No, it’s alright. Are you sure you’re okay?” Helen covered Carol’s hand on the table and gave a reassuring squeeze.

    “Yes, I’m just tired. Have a good night, Helen.”

    “Good night, Carol.”

    Carol turned to walk away but hesitated. She turned back to face her. “Helen? Thank you for being here.”

     

    ***

     

    Once she got back to her room on the seventh floor, Carol headed straight to the shower. She let the burning water wash over her and release the tension. She tried to calm her anxieties over what she’d just told Helen. 

    Zosia wasn’t afraid to be alone. After all, she’d come alone to a cabin in the woods to be alone. She didn’t know a neighbour would be there to spend all her time with her. She had come to be alone. If she wanted to spend so much time with Carol, it was because she wanted it.

    Carol put on her warmest pyjamas and lied down under the heavy duvet of the bed. She played on her phone, trying to change her mind.

    She’d almost managed to get Zosia out of her mind when her screen announced an incoming phone call from an unknown caller.

    Her heart skipped a beat and she immediately took the call, hoping it was her. “Hello?” 

    “Hi, Carol,” Zosia’s smiling voice said across the line.

    Carol let out a breath of relief and closed her eyes, content of her presence at the other end of the line. Content that Zosia had called her. 

    “How’s Chicago?”

    “It’s… populated,” Carol said and Zosia chuckled. 

    “I bet. And how’s the event? Have you met a lot of fans?”

    “A bunch of them, yes. They’re all very excited for the upcoming book.”

    “That’s wonderful, Carol. I’m so happy for you.”

    "Thanks," Carol couldn’t help but feel her cheeks turn pink. “I just had a drink with Helen. She asked how you’ve been.”

    “Oh,” Zosia said, surprised. “That’s very nice of her to ask. And what did you say?”

    She heard the muffled sound of sheets as she assumed Zosia moved in her bed. 

    Carol froze, panic creeping up her chest. She couldn’t really tell Zosia what she’d told Helen. “I said you are well…” Carol closed her eyes and weighed her options. It was easier to tell Zosia how she felt over the phone, when she couldn’t see her face, and when she was pleasantly tipsy. “Actually,” Carol said, clearing her throat. “I told her that…”

    “What?” Zosia asked quietly.

    “She asked and I quote: ‘How’s your hot girlfriend?’”.

    There was a moment of silence before Zosia’s bright laughter broke through the line. Carol couldn’t help but smile. 

    “Well, thanks Helen. And to that you said ‘She is well?’” Zosia asked, her smile clear in her voice.

    “I… Well, I said that you are not my girlfriend,” she said matter-of-factly, with a hint of teasing.

    “Damn, Sturka, don’t break my heart like that,” Zosia joked again, but Carol heard the tentativeness in her voice and it encouraged her.

    They were silent for a moment. “I did… I didn’t want to say that.”

    “Say what?”

    “I mean, I didn’t want to… I didn’t want to correct her. I want you to be my girlfriend,” Carol said. “If, if you’d want that too, of course,” she added, rolling her eyes at how much she sounded like a love-stuck teenager.

    “Well, considering how we are and what we do, I just assumed I already was, kind of. But if it wasn’t clear, then, yes. I want to be your girlfriend,” Zosia chuckled.

    Carol threw her wrist up in victory and mouthed a happy ‘Yes!’.

    “Ok, cool then,” she said coolly. 

    “Cool,” Zosia said, clearly amused by this situation.

    They stayed silent for a moment, Carol smiling like an idiot across the line. “I’m thinking about you a lot. Probably more than I should. This event is nice, but I can’t wait to get back home.”

    “Me too,” Zosia said in a low voice. “I can’t wait to take you boating with me again. It gets lonely here without you now. Especially in the bay.”

    Carol felt her cheeks turn pink remembering what had happened the last time they’d found themselves boating at the bay. “Soon enough.”

    “You should get some sleep. You have a big day tomorrow. I just wanted to hear your voice.”

    She just wanted to hear my voice and now we are officially dating, Carol smiled. 

    “Thank you for calling. Good night.”

    “Good night, Carol.”

     

    ***

     

    The second day of the book event was much busier. It started with an early call, the mediating of a panel on books that shaped successful writers and a few interviews in a freezing-cold green room at the hotel. 

    There was a short break for lunch that Carol had wanted to use to get back to her hotel, lie down and call Zosia, but the general manager of the festival had insisted that Carol come have lunch with her and other guests.

    Even if these events stressed her, Carol always found a way to enjoy them, enjoy the attention and praise of her work. 

    Except this time, she was uncharacteristically giddy, replaying her phone call from the night before.

    Zosia really was her girlfriend now.

    It sounded almost too good to be true. 

    Carol found herself joking and making everyone around her laugh at her silliness, she found herself smiling and laughing brightly. Even Helen seemed pleasantly confused by her behaviour.

    While they were walking back to the convention center, Helen pulled at her arm and whispered in her ears. “Don’t get me wrong, I like to see you like this, but what has gotten into you?”

    “What do you mean?” Carol asked innocently.

    "You look… Exhilarated.”

    Carol shrugged. “Well, I suppose I am.”

    “You talked to her?” Helen lifted an eyebrow, an amused smile curling up her lips.

    Carol looked around but there was no one within hearing range. “Yes.”

    “Oh,” Helen said, surprised by the rapidity of the turn of events. “I can only assume it went well.”

    Carol smiled and opened her mouth to reply but was interrupted by a fan coming to get her book signed. “Excuse me, Carol, do you mind signing my book?”

    “Of course!” Carol exclaimed, her enthusiasm surprising both Helen and the fan herself. “To whom should I make this?”

    “Dana,” she blushed.

    “Alright, Dana. Thank you so much for reading,” she smiled, holding the book with her arm to sign “Dear Dana, let the world show you just how brightly it can shine. Carol Sturka X”

    “Do you want a picture?” Carol asked, closing the book and handing it back to Dana.

    “Ah, yes, please. I would like that very much.”

    Dana took out her phone, which Carol gently grabbed and handed to Helen. 

    Helen took the phone, a careful look on Carol, before relaxing and taking the picture.

    “Thank you so much,” Dana muttered and walked away.

    “Thank you, Dana. Have a good day!” Carol called out to her. She turned to Helen. “Isn’t she sweet?”

    Helen watched Carol like she was absolutely mad.

    “Miss Sturka?” A production employee called as they approached the convention center. “They are ready for you.”

    Carol turned to Helen and wiggled her eyebrows, walking backwards to the backstage entrance. “See you on the other side, Nellie Lady.”

    She turned and disappeared behind the stage door.

     

    ***

     

    It was only by the end of the afternoon that Carol was relieved from her duty for a brief moment. She headed back to her hotel room, with merely three hours in front of her to relax before she was expected at a fancy cocktail party.

    It was an expensive soirée, one where the entry tickets started at two thousand dollars and all profits were used to fund a writing workshop for young writers. As the spokesperson of the book festival, Carol was to show herself to the guests, give a short but powerful speech and take a lot of pictures in front of the media wall.

    Usually, she hated these events, even when the cause was dear to her. 

    This time, she didn’t mind it so much, as long as she had time to rest (and by resting, she thought of calling Zosia and hearing her voice) before getting ready to go out again.

    When the elevator doors closed behind her and left alone in the confined space, she let out a sigh of relief. She clicked on the 7 button and took her phone out of her handbag. She unmuted it and a bunch of notifications popped up.

    She saw she had three missed calls from Koumba Diabaté. She didn’t remember she had Koumba in her contacts, but she assumed they had exchanged years ago over something relating to the cabin. 

    Why was he calling her now?

    Frowning, she couldn’t help but worry something had happened. With trembling hands, she scrolled through the notifications and saw he had sent her a text message three hours before.

     

    Koumba Diabaté (neighbour - cabin)

    Hi Carol, this is Koumba Diabaté. I tried calling you without success. Please call me as soon as you get this message. Merci.

     

    What the hell is going on? Carol thought. In the five years she’d known him, they had never called, nor texted.

    Carol tried to find if Zosia had called her, but there was no sign of the unnamed phone number from last night.

    She couldn’t have texted her, because, as Zosia had told Carol before she left for the book event, her cellphone had been destroyed in the car accident and she simply hadn’t bought one ever since.

    If she wanted to call her, she had to call the landline in Henryk’s cabin.

    Carol felt her breath catch in her throat. 

    Did something happen to Zosia?

    The elevator doors opened to the seventh floor and she burst through them, almost running to her room at the end of the corridor. 

    She pushed the front food open and entered the quiet, ordered room. She immediately called Koumba back and nervously paced around while the phone rang.

    “Carol, finally,” he replied after too many rings.

    “Koumba, what the hell is going on?”

    Koumba let out a small breath. “Zosia had a seizure. She’s stable now, we’re at the hospital with her, but we were very worried for a moment.”

    Carol blinked, confused. “A seizure?”

    “Yes. They think it's a post-traumatic seizure, related to a car accident she was in a few months ago. It’s the second time she’s had one since then."

    Carol was silent. She felt hands wrap around her throat and press harder and harder as Koumba talked. 

    “Can I talk to her?”

    “She’s asleep now. They made her do a bunch of tests and she was exhausted after.”

    Carol sat down on the side of an armrest, eyes veiling with tears. She took a deep breath. “Are you with her at the hospital?”

    “Yes. I’m here with Laxmi. Aarush was here earlier too. We’re staying with her until the hospital kicks us out for the night.”

    “Are you in the room with her?”

    “I got out to take your call.”

    Carol nodded. “I wish I could be there with you.”

    “She told me not to call you. She didn’t want to disrupt your book event. I understand you are across the country?”

    Carol felt her eyes water up, cursing everything that had led her away from Zosia.

    Silly woman, she thought, asking Koumba not to call me and disrupt me.

    “Yes,” Carol finally said, clearing her throat.

    “I think she wants you here, Carol. I don’t know if this is possible for you. She didn’t say it, but I can see it in her eyes. She…” Koumba hesitated and sighed. “She called for you in her sleep.”

    Carol closed her eyes and swallowed back the tears. “How did this happen, Koumba?”

    “I’m not sure. We are lucky because it happened outside at the farmer’s market. It was Kusimayu who was with her and called the ambulance. She really took good care of her and reacted well. She saved her from hitting her head on the pavement when she collapsed.”

    “Jesus,” Carol breathed, only beginning to comprehend what Koumba was telling her. “Listen, I'll leave tonight and come back home as soon as I can. How long will she be in the hospital?”

    “At least for the night. The doctors want to monitor her.”

    “Alright, I will let you know.”

    “Ok, Carol.”

    “Koumba,” she said before hanging up. “Thank you for calling me. I really appreciate it.”

    “Of course, Carol.”

    “And Koumba?”

    “Yes?”

    “Thank you for being with her at the hospital.”

    “Don’t mention it.”

    Carol hung up and slipped down on the seat of the armrest. She looked outside. The sky was grey and light rain was tapping against the window.

    Carol took the time to take it all in, but it wasn’t long before she started moving. She went straight to the closet, pulled her clothes off the hangers and threw them in her suitcases.

    She was putting her toiletries back into their case when she heard a knock on the door. She went to get it and found Helen standing in the doorframe. 

    Her smile fell at the sight of Carol. “What’s going on?”

    “Come on in.”

    Helen closed the door behind her and followed Carol into the room and spotted the mess around a half-packed suitcase on the bed. 

    “Carol, what are you doing?”

    “I’m going back. I need you to book me a flight back home.”

    “What?”

    Carol went to her. “Zosia… she’s at the hospital. She had a seizure. I need to go see her.”

    Helen blinked, surprised by the information. “Is she alright?”

    “She’s stable, but…” Carol's voice caught in her throat and she turned to go on with packing her stuff.

    “She has epilepsy and you didn’t know?” Helen asked, confused.

    Carol rubbed her eyebrows, trying to ease the tension that had set since the phone call. “I don’t even know if she knew herself. Koumba said it’s related to the car accident.”

    Helen watched speechless as Carol buzzed around the room and packed her things. “Carol! Stop this,” she finally exclaimed.

    Carol froze and turned to her. “What is it?”

    “You can’t leave today. You still have three more days here.”

    Carol went to her, pulling at her biceps for grounding. “Helen, please. I have to go. She’s at the hospital, I need to be there for her.”

    “Carol, you are halfway across the country. You are at work, people are waiting on you here. You can’t just disappear. Especially not just before tonight’s cocktail party. This is really important.”

    “So is Zosia’s health!”

    “She’s not dying, isn’t she?”

    Carol took a step back. “Why would you even say that!”

    “You know what I mean! If she is stable, then you can wait until you get back to see her.”

    Carol threw her toiletries case on the bed and sat down, running her hands through her hair. “I want to be with her. I want to be there for her.”

    Helen sighed and made her way to the bed, sitting down next to Carol. She kindly rubbed her back for reassurance. “I know, Carol. But I’m sure Zosia doesn’t want you to give up everything to run to her, especially if her situation is stable. I’m sure she’s looking forward to seeing you, but understand that you need to do this.”

    Carol grunted and straightened her back. “I can’t just leave her at the hospital.”

    “Can Koumba stay with her?”

    “He’s there already.”

    “See? She’s not alone there.”

    “It’s not that,” Carol cried. “I want to be there for her.”

    “Carol, you can’t drop every professional engagement for someone you’re sleeping with.”

    Carol frowned. “She’s not someone I’m sleeping with. She’s… she’s my girlfriend.”

    It was the first time she declared it to someone else. She hated that it was in such a tragic situation. 

    “Is she?”

    Carol nodded. “I asked her last night on the phone.”

    Helen sighed and her hand fell from Carol’s back to the mattress. “Damn,” she said. “I’m sorry, Carol, but you have to think clearly. You can see her and be with her when you come back.”

    “What if I leave tomorrow morning?”

    Helen gave her the look. This conversation was over and even if deep in her heart, Carol knew Helen was right, she couldn’t help but feel angry at her, at the world and at the universe for this situation.

    “Can you at least change my flight from Monday morning to Sunday night?”

    Helen nodded. “Yes, that I can do.”

    “Thank you,” Carol said in a mere whisper. 

    The rest of the night passed in a blur. It was an out-of-body experience and Carol would only be able to believe that she actually was there when she saw pictures of the night.

    It was exhausting. The knot in her stomach made it impossible for her to swallow anything the cocktail had to offer except champagne, which she tried to wait for until at least until after her speech, which was coming much too late for her own liking.

    It was past midnight when she finally made it back to her hotel room. She didn’t care, she called Koumba and he replied after five rings. “Carol,” he said.

    “How is she?”

    “She was a bit better when I left. She ate and she’s sleeping again. I stayed with her tonight and Laxmi will come in the morning.”

    Carol let out a breath of relief. 

    “When will you arrive?”

    Carol closed her eyes, biting her lower lip. “I… I won’t be able to leave before Sunday night.”

    “Oh,” Koumba said, clearly surprised. 

    It was Thursday night. 

    That night, Carol stared at the ceiling until the sun rose from behind the building across the street.

     

    ***

     

    Zosia was able to get back home on Friday afternoon, or so Koumba told her via text. 

    She was still confused and tired, so Koumba suggested it might be best if Carol didn’t call her that day and waited until Saturday.

    Even if it was tearing her apart and Carol would’ve killed just to hear her voice, she understood and refrained from calling her. She tried her best to smile at the fans, at the festival team and attendees her best, but her eyes were dead. 

    Worry was eating her alive. Worry over Zosia’s state, physical and mental, worry about what would happen when Carol went back, worry about Zosia’s reaction when she found out that she hadn’t come earlier even after learning the state she was in.

    Every five minutes, Carol considered dropping everything  and leaving against Helen’s wishes, even if she knew it would be very bad for her career and her integrity. How would she even begin to explain the situation?

    So she waited and waited, her eyes always on her phone, waiting for an update from Koumba, but the more time passed, the less frequent they became.

    They became so rare she even started wondering if he’d forgotten about her.

    Saturday afternoon, she had a free hour and made her way back to her hotel room. Her hands were shaking as the elevator took her to the seventh floor. 

    Carol entered her room and kicked off her shoes before sitting on the bed. She crossed her legs and looked at the phone in her hands.

    It was raining again and she could hear the water tap violently against the window. It was late afternoon but the room was dark from the clouds shielding the sun.

    Carol tapped on the phone number that had called her Wednesday night.

    A trembling hand brought the phone to her ear and she listened as it rang.

    It rang forever and no answer came. She closed her eyes and hung up. 

    Carol let out a breath and listened to the thunder rolling outside. She was just about to get up again when the phone rang in her hand.

    She stared at the screen and recognized the number. She immediately replied.

    “Hello,” she breathed.

    There was a moment of silence. It was so quiet, she thought there was no one at the end of the line.

    “Hello?” she repeated.

    “It’s me,” finally said Zosia’s voice.

    “Zosia,” Carol let out a breath she hadn't realized she was holding. “How are you?”

    “I’m okay,” she said, then was silent again. Carol could hear nothing but her soft breathing and heartbeat.

    “How do you feel?” she finally asked. She didn’t know what to ask her, what to tell her. Carol simply wanted to be there in the cabin with her and hold her closely.

    “I feel very tired and still a bit confused from time to time. I didn’t think this would happen again.”

    “It happened before? After… after the accident?”

    “Mmm,” Zosia said in a low voice. “A few weeks after.”

    Carol nodded and then realized Zosia couldn’t see her.

    “I wanted…” she stopped herself. She didn’t want to make this about herself so she stayed silent.

    “I know, Koumba told me.”

    Carol nodded again. She was ready to tell Zosia anything she wanted to hear, but she wasn’t giving her anything. She had never been so distant and silent before and it was terrifying to Carol. 

    She swallowed hard. 

    “How’s the book event?” Zosia asked.

    “I don’t… I can’t really bring myself to enjoy it. I’m worried about you.”

    “It’s okay, Carol,” she said and Carol heard the ghost of a smile in her voice. “I’m okay, really. It was just… unexpected.”

    “Still. I wanted to be with you.”

    Another heavy silence.

    “It’s okay, don’t worry about this. Please try to enjoy the rest of the event.”

    “I’m gonna leave Sunday night instead. I will come see you when I get back.”

    “If you arrive too late, can you please come in the morning? I feel so tired and need to sleep more.”

    Carol swallowed hard. She knew this wasn’t against her, she knew it was nothing bad, still, it hurt more than a slap in the face. “Yes, of course,” she said, but her voice broke as she spoke the words.

    “I will go lie down. The medication is making me too sleepy. Thank you for calling. I’m really happy to hear your voice. Please try to enjoy the rest of the festival, okay? I don’t want to be a burden.”

    “Zosia,” Carol breathed out and closed her eyes. “God, you’re not a burden. Not at all. I am dying to be with you.”

    “I know. Goodbye.”

    “Bye-” 

    The line went dead before she finished her sentence. 

    Carol fell down on the bed and rested her phone against her chest. She closed her eyes and let the feeling take hold of her.

    She had never heard Zosia like this before. Zosia, who was always a ray of sunshine sounded dried out of all her colours and it broke Carol's heart.

    Something had changed. No matter what Zosia told her, she could hear it in her voice that something was different. Like it was someone else talking.

    Suddenly, she felt like she didn’t know her anymore. The woman she was just starting to call her girlfriend.

    She burst into tears and let herself release the tension that had been building up in the darkness of her room.

    A fear settled in her chest and now, she realized she was dying to see her again as much as she dreaded going back home to her.

    Chapter End Notes

    ..... I'm sorry......

    Chapter 23: the last time

    Chapter Notes

    thank you so much for reading and leaving comments, I appreciate it so much! sorry for those who were sad after the last chapter... I hope you can still enjoy this one!

    Zosia sat on the couch by the window, her knees up under her chin and staring as the rain fell down against the glass. Her face was neutral and it was hard for Carol to read her thoughts. One thing was for certain, she looked exhausted.

    Carol looked at her over her book, trying not to get Zosia’s attention. Every time the brunette felt her eyes on her, she could hardly hide her annoyance and tried to tell Carol that she was okay and she didn’t need her to look after her.

    The truth was, she wasn’t fine.

    Carol could see it clear as day, but Zosia kept repeating that everything was alright. 

    Carol did everything she could: she gave her space, she only talked to her when it seemed like Zosia wanted it, she tried to ask her questions about the accident, tried to get her to finally let it all go and express the emotions she had buried so deep within her that even she couldn’t access.

    Days passed and Carol remained patient. It wasn’t always easy, she was often tempted to walk to Zosia and scream at her to just let it out. Just let herself feel it. Just let herself acknowledge that what had happened was a tragedy, that it was heartbreaking and that it wasn’t alright or fine. That it had left a deep cut in her.

    But who was Carol to tell her that? 

    The woman who couldn’t confess to her lover a trauma that had happened decades ago.

    So she patiently waited. She laughed with her when Zosia wanted to laugh. She was silent when Zosia didn’t want to speak.

    Carol tried to make it up to her for not showing up earlier, for not giving up on the book festival to come be with her, even if Zosia kept repeating that it was fine, that she never would've wanted Carol to leave the event for her.

    And that went on for days. Days during which Carol barely worked on her novel and for each day her manuscript stayed unopened, she grew more anxious.

    They were stuck in between two dimensions where time didn’t exist, where things didn’t move on and Carol knew that if they stayed still too long, both of them would drown.

    Maybe, after all, what Zosia needed was simply time and space.

    On one particular tense afternoon, Carol got up and sighed. “I should get to work a bit. I’ll leave you to rest, but if you need anything, don’t hesitate to come knocking at my door any time.”

    “Okay, Carol,” Zosia said, voice neutral, her eyes on the lake outside the window.

    Carol stood still, waited for Zosia to turn at her. She grew more annoyed at her avoidance, but when she opened her mouth to say something, Zosia turned to watch her and Carol caught a sadness in her eyes and the anger immediately disappeared, leaving place to a terrible ache in her chest. 

    She walked to Zosia and her hand caressed her hair like she was made of glass. “Just come find me when you need me.”

    Zosia nodded and Carol leaned down to kiss her. Zosia kissed her back softly. Her thumb even found Carol’s chin and despite the tense circumstances they found themselves in for the past few days, Carol felt a warmth take over her body under Zosia’s healing touch.

    She left and gave her one last look in the doorframe.

     

    ***

     

    Time passed and Zosia didn’t ask for her. So, Carol didn’t go to see her.

    And that was how she ended up not seeing her for three whole days.

    Even not in Zosia’s company, Carol didn’t work much on her book. She spent a lot of time staring at her manuscript and the notes she, her editor Val and Helen had written for her.

    She spent a lot of time with a glass in her hand, looking down at the bottom of the golden liquid and wondering where things had gone wrong.

    Carol tried not to lose control. If someone had to be strong right now, it was her. 

    Rain came for a few days, locking them both in their respective homes and Carol could only stand by the window and search for any signs of Zosia, wondering if things would ever be the same. 

    She woke up multiple times at night, drenched in sweat from fever dreams. Each time she woke up, she found herself looking for Zosia and when she didn’t find her in bed next to her, she would get up, walk to the kitchen and stand in the kitchen looking through the window and hoping that she was sound asleep, getting rest.

    When she sat at her computer for too long without getting any work done, she listened to music, paced around and cleaned, or got a drink.

    Carol was restless and she ached to go see Zosia.

    After a few days, it was Zosia who came to her. Carol almost fell down at her feet when she opened the back door and saw her standing in the doorframe They ate dinner together and sat by the fire in the living room at night. Zosia fell asleep her head on Carol’s lap while Carol caressed her hair and studied her features, like she knew she would need to remember them. She tried to enjoy every second she could spend in her presence because there was a sticking feeling in her chest that felt like their time together was running out. 

    The truth was, she could feel that Zosia was slipping away. She was further away and heading in a direction that Carol was certain didn’t involve her.

    She couldn’t be surprised. Really, she had sensed it from the beginning. It had been the one thing weighing over their relationship. The only difference was the reason why that was.

    And that she had imagined they'd get more time than this.

    Every memory they had once shared seemed so far away when she caressed her brown locks and stared down at her face, traits relaxed with sleep. The first boat tours, their confessions in the dark during the power outage at the beginning of the summer, the yoga class she’d brought her to and that Carol had thought she’d go back to but never did, thinking the summer would be endless and she’d have many opportunities.

    She thought of their almost-kiss by the fire. She wondered how things would’ve been different if the kids across the lake had decided to light up fireworks at any other time than this one.

    She thought of their night out in town. How they had flirted with each other so loudly that Koumba and Laxmi had been able to see what was going on between them before even Zosia and Carol had fully realized themselves.

    She thought of all the things Zosia had made her do, things Carol never have done in a million years if it weren’t of her: skinny dipping in the middle of the night, hiking in the forest in the middle of a storm, kissing frogs and hands tentatively feeling each other.     At some point, it became too much she wanted to leave the cabin.

    She was stuck where Zosia had left her, unable to move on from what had happened and facing the fact that what she had hoped for them would never happen.

    Instead of working on her novel that was almost complete, she stared ahead, eyes empty and replayed the moment she had passed the door over and over again.

    In moments like this, her eyes would water up and she would immediately go grab a bottle.

    Two weeks later, it was finally dawning on her that Zosia was gone for real and that she wouldn’t come back.

    One night, she was completely wasted, spread out on the couch, bottle of whiskey in hand, watching television. She was passed out and the first thing she saw when she gained consciousness was another rerun of The Awful Truth. 

    She thought of Zosia’s laughter when they'd watched in during their road trip and it was then that she realized the pain would never go away if she stayed here.

    The next morning — or afternoon, whatever the time was when she woke up and was able to stand up without wanting to throw up — she packed her things and left the lake house.

    Chapter 25: only time

    Chapter Notes

    can't say this enough: thank you so much for reading, for leaving kudos and comments!! and for sticking through these depressing chapters lol
    this one is a bit shorter than the others. I hope you like it!

    A month later 

     

    Some days were better than others. And with time, most days were fine.

    Carol slowed down on the drinking to focus on finishing her book. She managed to focus on her work, even if it was painful in the beginning as each word reminded her of Zosia. Each scene reminded her of moments they had shared, of a peaceful, carefree feeling Carol had felt in her presence for the first time in her life and knew would not feel ever again.

    She came to accept the truth. Zosia was gone and she wouldn’t come back. She didn’t know where she was, how she was doing, who she was with. She stopped wondering after a moment because most of the possibilities she imagined were too painful.

    She stopped asking herself if what they had shared had been real. With a clearer mind, she came to accept that Zosia hadn’t processed what had happened the months before their meeting and that her second seizure had forced her to face the ugly truth about the accident. Something she wasn’t ready to share yet. 

    She needed time to process all this.

    And while Carol was mature enough to know she had to let go and move on, accept things as they were, a small part of her still hoped that when she would feel better, Zosia would come back.

    So each time she checked her messages or emails, a part of her expected to see a message from Zosia. Each time, the pang of sadness and heartbreak was inevitable. Still, she continued hoping.

    ***

    A month later (Two months after Zosia left)

     

    The nights Carol didn’t spend wasted on her couch, she spent tipsy behind her desk. It was really hard for her to focus on her novel. She could hardly work and she knew Helen was done running after her.

    A part of her felt ashamed to be so heartbroken and such a mess after getting dumped by a woman she’d only known for a few weeks. Her heartbreak over this relationship had now lasted longer than the time she’d known Zosia.

    She kept telling herself that, perhaps, if she hadn’t based an entire book on her, she would’ve been able to move on earlier than that. She wanted to finish that damn book and end her misery, but it took all her energy to focus on work. When the pain became too much, she drank and crashed in front of the TV. She was caught in something she had created and she was the only one who could take herself out of this limbo.

    One night, she was staring at a page, vision blurry with whisky and lack of sleep, sighing as she watched her words. She hated how lovestruck she sounded.

    And I call myself a professional writer.

    Elbows resting on her desk, Carol ran her hands through her blonde hair, eyes closed as she tried to will inspiration to come to her. She was so close to the end. Just a few more weeks and it was going into print.

    This inferno must end.

    And then, an idea struck her.

    Carol grabbed her cellphone. She didn’t take a second to think this over. She dialed the number she knew by heart. It was past ten at night, but Helen answered after the second ring. “I want to make a change at the end of the book,” she told her.

     

    ***

     

    A month later (Three months after Zosia left)

     

    The book was over.

    The edition for members of the industry to read was out and Carol was terrified.

    It wasn’t even out for the public that Raban would be a woman, Carol wasn’t publicly out just yet, but she already acted like some of her dirty secrets had been exposed to the light of day and barely left the house.

    Anything she could order, she bought online and had delivered to her front door. 

    Her entire team briefed her of what would come next with the release of the book: the interviews, the questions, how to reply, how to position herself now that she was writing a lesbian love story.

    Everyone around her was too kind and too supportive and she was too scared.

    At night, she lied in bed and hated herself for wishing Zosia was there. She would know what to say to ease her nerves. She would only have to get lost in her beautiful brown eyes and everything would be okay.

    It was her that had inspired her to take this creative turn and now she felt that she had to get through the hardest part of it alone.

    She had never been a religious or spiritual person, at least not since she was a teenager, yet she found herself calling out to any stronger being to bring her back to her. She thought about trying to reach out to her again. Maybe even ask Koumba if he had any way of communicating with her. But how embarrassing would that be? Months later, to crawl to find a way to contact the woman who had left her. 

    So, at night, she continued sometimes waking up in sweat, believing she’d find her lying down next to her, snoring softly. Thinking she might do this for the rest of her life. 

    One day, she risked getting out of her house because she needed to exchange an online order that was wrong. She did as fast as possible and ran back to her car.

    She finally felt her rapid heartbeat slow down as the vibrations of the car eased her. The radio was playing softly in the background and she hadn’t even realized it was on until the voice sounded too familiar.

    Her blood froze in her veins when she heard the spoken lines of the beginning of the song.

     

    I've heard people say that

    Too much of anything is not good for you, baby

    But, I don't know about that

    As many times as we've loved and

    We've shared love and made love

    It doesn't seem to me like it's enough

    There's just not enough of it

    It's just not enough

    Oh, oh, babe

     

    She should’ve turned it off before but she was frozen. 

    No clear memories of that night came to her mind. Only fractured images of that summer flashed in no coherent chronology. Zosia smiling on the rocks by the waterfalls, holding a frog between her palms like it was the most precious thing in the world. The way she’d clapped her hands when Carol sung her that song. The way her tall, warm hand could hold her, the electricity her touch sent through her body.

    Carol smashed the volume bottom shut.

     

    ***

     

    A month later (Four months after Zosia left)

     

    It was still a month before the book tour started. 

    Carol was growing exponentially nervous by the day and Helen started to worry about her. Even more than she already did since she first told her about Zosia leaving. 

    She thought about booking vacations for herself somewhere, but nowhere sounded like a good idea. There was only one place she felt a pull to consider, even if she knew it would only break her heart to go back. She was starting to accept that she was gone and maybe she wanted her heart to break for Zosia one last time. 

    Now  that the book was done, she was going to spend the next few weeks talking about it and reading parts of it, swim in this world she’d created one last time. The world that was only a big memory of Zosia would be out in the open for the rest of the world to see and make their own.

    She would release this memory of Zosia to the world and they would give it a new meaning. And then, she might be free.

    Accepting that Zosia was only going to be a memory hurt less and less with each passing day. Maybe she did this to avoid the pain because deep down, she still hoped she would come back. The tour was coming, a part of her was delusional enough to think she might show up. A part of her, the part that still loved Zosia, wanted to keep hoping for a happy ending.

    So she found herself on her way back to the cabin, taking the same route as the months before, stopping at the same rest area.

    Maybe doing a pilgrimage of this lost love would heal her. Or break her heart one last time like she seemed to desperately want. Maybe she wanted to see if she had come back and was waiting for Carol.

    It was ridiculous. Zosia had ways to contact her and she hadn’t done it. There was simply no way she was back at the cabin.

    Yet, Carol knew a part of her would never be able to live in peace if she moved on from this love without even going to see first.

    The sun was setting earlier and it was almost dark when she arrived. As she passed the frontiers of town, her heart was beating against her chest in anticipation.

    An anticipation the most realist part of her wanted to quiet down, while her most romantic side wanted to nourish, even if she knew would only be disappointed.

    Her heart skipped a beat when she saw a light of Zosia’s cabin on.

    Her mouth turned dry and in a millisecond, she imagined a lifetime together.

    Maybe they had a chance.

    She took the turn to the parking lot and her hope died as fast as it’d been born.

    It was Henryk’s car in the driveway.

    She parked in front of her own abandoned, lonely house. She killed the engine and sat in the silence for a moment.

    She could see inside. She watched the cabinets, she watched the modern lamps. She remembered how she used to watch Zosia through these very windows.

    Iza appeared in front of the window, talking to someone outside the frame.

    It didn’t hurt as much as she’d imagined. 

    She just felt numb and dumb.

    Carol unfastened her seatbelt with a sigh and opened the door. She stepped on the gravel and walked to the back of her car. This time, she only had a travel backpack and her work bag with her.

    She grabbed them, locked the car behind her and moved to her cabin when the door to Henryk’s cabin opened. He got out to his car, not seeing her.  Carol froze in her steps. She didn’t know what to do.

    He must have felt her presence because he was just about to open the driver’s door when he saw her and stopped. “Oh, Carol, I didn’t see you there.”

    “Hey,” she said. Her voice was rough from keeping quiet all day long. She cleared it. “How are you?”

    Henryk hesitated and moved towards her. “Good, thank you. We have a vacation without the kid so we came to spend some time here.”

    Carol nodded. Her heart was beating so fast, she was scared he could see her chest moving. There were so many things she wanted to ask him, but she knew that even speaking her name was dangerous.

    “Are you staying here for long?”

    Carol blinked and managed to form a thought. “Uh, just for a week or so. I’m taking some time off before the book tour.”

    “Ah yes, the new Wycaro novel.”

    Carol’s mouth was dry. Did Henryk actually follow her career so closely or… Or had Zosia talked about it?

    “Yes,” she only said instead and wanted to roll her eyes at herself.

    “Great,” he forced a smile and they stared at each other, unsure what to say or do.

    “How…” Carol finally spoke, unable to stop herself. “Is Zosia doing better? Did she have another seizure?”

    Henryk’s face softened, which helped Carol relax a bit. “She’s doing much better, yes. But these past few months, she’s been through a lot. And no, she did not have another seizure, thank God.”

    Carol let out a breath of relief. At least, Zosia was fine. She would be sad for the rest later.

    “That’s wonderful news,” Carol said in a low voice.

    “Yes. Actually, she came back to the country last month.”

    “Oh,” Carol said. 

    Is she back in New York? Will she come back to the cabin? How come she hasn’t called me? Maybe she needs to settle in first.

    “I need to go to the supermarket before it closes otherwise Iza will kill me. But, I’ll see you around,” he said, heading to his car.

    “Yeah, bye,” Carol said, but she was miles away in her own mind.

    She made her way to the cabin and with every step, her mind cleared. 

    She’d thought that coming back to the lake house would be like doing a tour of a haunted house. She’d thought that every tree, every piece of furniture would be painted with Zosia’s memory and that she would be depressed from just looking at a chair.

    But as Carol walked back inside the place she had left completely devastated months ago, she let herself feel hope.

    Zosia was back in the country. Zosia was doing better.

    Maybe she would find her way back to her.

    Chapter 26: say you'll remember me

    Chapter Notes

    thank you so much for reading!!! I appreciate it so much!!

    The tour went by in a blur. 

    Eight weeks gone in a flash.

    Eight weeks that had felt like eight days, and sometimes like eight months. 

    There were two more stops before the end of the tour and Carol felt truly content professionally. Like she hadn’t in a very, very long time.

    The reception to the new book was much better than she had ever dared to hope. Her longest fans hadn’t turned their back on her now that Raban was a woman. Most came to talk to her and pressed just how important her new book was.

    For the first time, she had to admit to herself that she felt appreciative of them. The prejudices and annoyance that she used to associate with them faded away with each new stop on the tour.

    She found a pride in her work that she had never felt before. She found herself truly listening to her fans’ comments, she found herself looking forward to the new readings and meetings.

    On top of her dedicated fanbase, new faces showed up to the readings. Faces of people like her. She found herself in the new crowd the new book brought and was surprised to feel seen and comfortable.

    It went by so smoothly, she almost regretted waiting this long for doing it.

    There was only one thing missing.

    She avoided thinking about Zosia as much as she could. Slowly, she felt the memory of her, of her kind eyes and bright smile, of the way she called her name in the darkness of the night and the sound of her laughter, fade away with time. 

    It hurt, knowing that the vivid memory of her would eventually fade and that it would only exist in her book.

    Unless, Zosia came to the New York reading.

    Since her discussion with Henryk, the most logical part of her brain couldn’t control her heart. She knew that hoping Zosia would come and see her would only cause more suffering when she wouldn’t see her walk past the doors.

    But there was a feeling inside of her, a shuddering of her heart when she thought about it, like if her body felt that she was going to be there.

    She was able to ignore it for the entire tour, but now that she was sitting in the car on her way to her New York hotel, she couldn’t ignore it anymore. Her body was tense, fingers twirling together without her even noticing and her breathing harder than normal.

    Of course, Helen noticed.

    She knew Helen was pissed off at Zosia. Hell, she worried about Zosia’s safety if she did walk in the bookstore the following night. 

    And Helen was right to be angry. She had been the one to pick up the pieces and manage to glue her back together magically before the tour. She had spent so much energy hyping them both. More than usual.

    “Do you want to stop at that burger spot on Washington Square Park?” Helen asked tentatively.

    Carol was watching the buzzing street outside. People passing by, downtown lights and streetlights casting shadows on the streets. “I don’t think I could swallow anything right now.”

    Helen simply nodded. The silence was tense. Even without looking at her, Carol could feel the wheels running inside Helen’s head.

    “Carol…”

    “I know, Helen. Don’t worry, I’m okay. I just want to sleep and be ready for tomorrow.”

    Her voice wasn’t angry or pressing. 

    Tomorrow would be the last time she’d hope to see Zosia again. She was tired of expecting and waiting. It was the last night she would allow herself to hope to see Zosia again before it would be gone forever if she didn’t show up.

     

    ***

     

    When her alarm buzzed, Carol knew she hadn’t slept at all, tossing and turning all night long. 

    Yet, she felt more energized than she should. Perhaps it was an energy powered by despair or delusion but Carol immediately shut it off and jumped out of bed.

    Though her mind was awake, her body was slower to follow. She showered to kick on the machine and wrapped herself in a bathing robe. She applied the usual concealer that was running out by the end of the tour and added more make up than usual. Some discreet navy blue eye-shadow that brought out the blue of her eyes and some nude gloss. 

    She placed her hair and put on the best outfit she’d brought on the tour: cream coloured slacks tied in a pirate-like laces on the front and a loose white shirt. She threw on a brown leather jacket and was ready to go.

    Helen was waiting for her in the lobby when she got out, holding two cups of coffee. She seemed reassured when Carol smiled at her as she made her way towards her.

    She handed Carol her cup who took in a swift movement, betraying the unspoken coordination they still shared after the divorce. “Thank you,” Carol brought the cup to her lips immediately.

    The caffeine hit her system as soon as she took the first sip of her latte and the remaining fuzz in her body was gone. 

    “Ready?” Helen asked and they made their way to the front door of the hotel where their driver was waiting.

    The entire morning was spent at a small bar the editor had privatised for interviews. They met Julie, a publicist sent over to them from the editor’s New York offices, who coordinated the interviews with different outlets, magazines and websites. 

    By now, Carol was at ease with every question. She’d seen all of them throughout the tour: questions about her sexuality, why now, why Raban as a woman and simply not new characters or new books. She answered like a pro, polite smiles that allowed the journalists to feel a connection, looking deep into their eyes to make them feel she was there with them.

    Helen watched from the side, a pleased, proud smile on her lips.

    The morning passed by quickly and the interviews ended with lunch at the restaurant with the team.

    In the afternoon, Helen and Julie took Carol to a few radio stations and a television studio where she discussed her reading tonight at Barnes & Nobles.

    As time passed by, a dormant knot started growing in Carol’s stomach. She pushed all thoughts of Zosia away and for a good part of the day, managed to keep her out of her mind and focus on work. Sometimes, her guard slipped and she found herself wondering if Zosia was listening to the radio show while she was on air.

    Once the press tour of the day was complete, it was happy hour. 

    Carol fell down in the back seat of the car, slowly followed by Helen. “We have a bit under an hour,” Helen stated, looking at her watch. 

    “Fancy a drink?”

    “Sure.”

    She looked over at the driver. “Can you please take us to Dante’s?”

    As they took seats at the back of the bar, the pianist started playing an instrumental version of Wildest Dreams. Carol hated the defeated acceptance of it and almost proposed they change bar in case it brought her bad luck for tonight.

    They ordered the house martinis and Carol pushed the negative thoughts away, but couldn’t tame the fires in her body.

    If Zosia indeed did come tonight… It would be a moment she’d been waiting for months.

    Carol realized Helen was watching her, concerned. “It’s fine,” she said quietly, not needing to ask Helen what she was worried about. She knew all too well.

    She was only able to relax a bit when the waiter brought their drinks and the pianist ended the song and played the first notes of At Last.

    Helen raised her glass up to Carol. “To this successful book tour. And to the change that will have an impact on many, many lives.”

    Carol felt her cheeks turn pink. “Please.”

    Helen lifted her eyebrows. “Shut up and click my glass.”

    Carol obeyed and took a small sip of her drink. She sighed as it pleasantly burnt on her tongue and immediately relieved some tension she’d been building up during the day. She put the glass down and leaned down against the counter.

    Her eyes wandered around the place. She watched the faces of the people sitting around the large bar. All so different, a miss-match of clothes and types of people that somehow fitted together. She appreciated how the afternoon light warmly reflected on the white-brick walls. She watched the barmen expertly pour the transparent liquid in Martini glasses, topping them with multicolour olives.

    She had always loved this spot.

    “Do you think she’s gonna come tonight?” Carol asked before she could stop the words from escaping her lips. 

    Helen didn’t react at first. Carol knew she’d been expecting this question all day long.

    “Does it really matter if she comes?” Helen asked kindly.

    Her point in the past months had been that the hurt Carol had felt over Zosia leaving without a word wouldn’t be able to disappear in the blink of an eye if she eventually did come back.

    While Carol didn’t want to think that things would never go back to what they used to be, Helen had gently tried to shake Carol from this daydream that only hurt her more.

    “I can’t help but think… Forget it,” Carol breathed and brought the glass to her lips.

    “Think what?” Helen encouraged her, nudging at her arm. 

    Carol swallowed her sip, letting the words settle in her mind.

    “That I wouldn’t even be upset about the hurt if she came back,” she said in a low voice.

    Helen was silent again, trying to find the right thing to say. Normally, it came easily to her. Not this time.

    “But if she doesn’t then… What we had… What did it even mean?”

    Carol’s eyes caught a man sitting alone at the other end of the L shaped bar. She watched him and felt a wave of compassion for him. She didn’t know what he was going through, but the sight of him felt like she was staring in a mirror. His back was crouched down, the fabric of his suit tense at his shoulder and his crossed hands held the base of his martini glass. He was maybe in his late thirties. The circles under his eyes were darker than Carol’s and he had an unattended few days old beard.

    “It doesn’t change anything to the way you felt about her, Carol,” Helen finally said. 

    Carol tore her gaze from the man and watched Helen.

    “I don’t know about that.”

    Carol realized this conversation depressed her too much and changed the subject to the martinis of Dante’s and told Helen the story of when she first came here. Helen listened even if she had already heard it many times before.

     

    ***

     

    Everything became foggy and dream-like the second Carol stepped into the Barnes & Nobles.

    There were already fans dressed in purple awaiting her. New York often had the biggest readings and there were much more people than most of her stops on the tour. 

    Carol couldn’t stop her eyes from scanning the room in search of a tall, brown-eyed pirate look-alike. She swallowed the deception when she didn’t see Zosia, but reminded herself that it was still early in the evening and that most regular attendees hadn’t arrived just yet.

    She didn’t remember meeting the manager who told Carol and Helen about the procedures for the night. She found herself nervously biting her fingers and pacing around the back of the store, waiting for the event to begin. 

    Finally, after what seemed like an eternity later, Carol made her way to the corner of the store that had been set up for the reading. There were at least a hundred people there and they all got up and applauded when she walked in. Carol almost tripped down as she walked to the podium, looking over the crowd, hopelessly in search of Zosia.

    Carol sat down in the chair and opened the newest Wycaro novel, eyes scanning the room. The manager presented her to the crowd, but all noise was blocked by a ringing in her ears.

    Carol's eyes looked in different directions, quickening their movement as they didn’t lay on Zosia. She tried to find dark hair, piercing brown eyes watching in her direction, but there were too many.

    It all happened in slow-motion, yet too fast at the same time.

    “Carol?” the manager kindly encouraged.

    Focus, Carol told herself.

    She cleared her throat and pushed the thought of Zosia away, unable to calm the aching in her stomach. She plastered her best reading smiles, the plastic one she’d spent her entire career using. “Sorry,” she grinned and ran a hand on the open page.

    The words in front of her blurred. She blinked a few times, willing her vision to clear. Her heartbeat quickened as she realized that she probably looked like a fool. 

    She found Helen on the side of the crowd who nodded in encouragement. 

    Just act like Zosia is in this crowd. You’ll digest the hurt later, she told herself and went on with the reading.

    For the people attending the reading, Carol was pleasantly reading the excerpt from her book, at the right pace, voice almost sensual and pausing at the right moments, unaware her heart was shattering in thousands of pieces in front of them.

    Not thinking about Zosia while doing the reading was an arduous task, as each word reminded her of her, as she could recall what had happened at the time that she wrote those very words.

    At some point, she heard footsteps in the bookstore and risked a look up.

    A tall, brunette stopped at the back of the crowd. 

    For a split second, before her gaze adjusted, Carol’s heart skipped a beat when she thought it was Zosia.

    She quickly realized it wasn’t Zosia at all.

    The sudden flicker of hope that had happened nearly broke her.

    She swallowed hard and went on with the reading.

    After closing the book on her lap, she looked up at a sea of wet eyes. Some readers were crying, others on the edge of tears. 

    She sighed and there was a long moment of silence before the crowd broke into applause. The readers got up, clapping loudly.

    The tension in her body released and Carol felt a down of energy. This time, she took the time to really scan the faces in the crowd and accepted that there was no sight of Zosia.

    Just another hour and you can let go.

    Her cheeks hurt from the broad, fake smile that made the fans beam. She sat at the table, hand sore from writing her name repeatedly. Helen stood next to her, preparing the books as new fans approached. 

    Carol kept her eyes on each new person, forcing herself not to look at the unending line of readers waiting to get their copies signed in search of her.

    Her brain was fuzzy, she couldn’t hear what people were telling her, but the readers were so emotional over the reading that they didn’t seem to realize.

    At last, Carol saw that the line was finally smaller.

    The last fan approached and Helen asked her name.

    “Greta,” she breathed, eyes intent on Carol.

    Helen lowered the book in front of Carol who personalized a note and signed.

    “I’m such a big fan,” Greta said, her voice trembling.

    “Thank you for coming, Greta,” Carol said brightly and handed her the book.

    It was finally done. 

    And Zosia hadn’t come.

    Carol swallowed the tears, ignored the pain that clung to each cell of her body. Helen turned to her and her hand found her back. She rubbed a gentle circle between her shoulder blades and the tenderness of it almost made her break. “I can’t…”

    Helen nodded and her hand was gone.

    Carol got up and barely noticed the manager on her way out. She knew fans would be waiting outside and she couldn’t be seen bursting into tears.

    The short chatter and photo with them almost killed her. Helen saw it and ended the meeting short. Helen opened the back door for Carol who threw one last desperate look over her shoulder, willing Zosia to burst out of the darkness. 

    Of course, she wasn’t there and Carol sat in the back of the car, defeated.

    They made their way back to the hotel, both with heavy hearts. Helen kept silent and risked taking Carol’s hand. Carol locked their fingers together so strongly, it hurt Helen, but she let her.

    Carol barely heard Helen thank their driver as he dropped them at the hotel. They walked inside in silence and the elevator doors closed behind them.

    The closer she got to her dark, quiet room, the harder it was to hold back her emotions.

    “Do you want to order room service and watch The Golden Girls?” Helen asked.

    Carol shook her head. “I can’t, sorry.”

    The elevator doors stopped on the fifth floor. Helen nodded and gave a squeeze on Carol’s shoulder. “Good night, Carol,” she said and walked out.

    Carol didn’t meet her eyes when she nodded. She pressed her lips tightly together for the rest of the ride and almost ran to her bedroom. She opened the door and slammed it behind her, letting the darkness of her hotel room swallow her. The only light coming through the suite was the neon sign of the theatre across the street.

    Carol leaned back on the door and let the mask down.

    A guttural cry escaped her throat and she fell to the floor, slipping down against the door, unable to keep the broken cries that couldn’t stay in any longer.

    It was finally over.

    Chapter 27: in Boston

    Chapter Notes

    thank you everyone for reading and leaving nice comments!!! I'm sorry if the last chapters were sad, I guess I'm still processing the stursia breakup in the show haha. I really hope you enjoy this chapter! thank you for still reading after all this time. I appreciate it so much!!

    The light coming through the window was blinding. Her migraine was pulsing and she felt a strong urge to bang her head against the wooden floor. Carol grunted, covering her eyes with the back of her hand and tried to fall asleep again.

    It took time, but she managed to sit up and swallow the need to throw up at the change of balance in her body.

    One step at a time, and groaning in pain at each movement, she made her way to the bathroom. 

    The darkness of the windowless room allowed her eyes to relax. With the daylight coming through the open door, she saw her reflection in the mirror. 

    It certainly was a sight: hair disheveled and make-up from last night smeared under her eyes, red and puffy from crying herself into oblivion. She looked like she’d been hit by a truck. 

    Steadying herself with her strong hold on the counter, she filled a large glass with freezing cold water from the sink and gulped it all down. Then another one.

    She yawned as she turned on the shower and stepped under the warm water. The relief was instant. Her body relaxed and she found she still had a heartbeat. 

    Flashes from the previous night came to her mind and the pain in her chest that had been burning like an open wound became stronger.

    This is the last I can cry, she told herself, but no tears came out. She was too drained from an entire night of breaking down and drinking herself to death.

    She let herself feel that pain for the last time under the shower and told herself that when she’d get out, she would get a grip.

    She felt a whirlwind of emotions. Pain, sadness and anger. 

    Anger at Zosia for giving up on her, for not being stronger, for not loving her. Sadness at the thought that Zosia had never been in love with her. Pain that she’d been heartbroken for months over someone who had probably forgotten all about her already while Carol thought about her all the damn time.

    Carol stayed in the shower until the water turned lukewarm and took a deep breath. 

    For the last time, she remembered her fondest memories of Zosia. 

    She pictured her in her boat, sitting happily as the summer sun kissed her darkened skin. She could almost taste it. She thought of her warm hands brushing against her on the star-gazing chair. She thought of the way her heart had almost stopped the first time that she had kissed her. She thought of the bliss that had come that first time their naked bodies had moved together. 

    It would be the last time she remembered it.

What if she took something stronger than alcohol and forgot and she was actually just bad-tripping?

    Apparently she was wrong and life still had some surprises for her.

    “Won’t you miss traveling?” Carol asked. 

    “Yes, but I will still be able to travel on my time off. I think I’m ready to find a place to settle in and really make a life for myself. I found that at the cabin, that I like to take the time to see things. To see plants grow, to see the sun rise and move in the sky until it disappears behind the mountains.”

    Zosia looked down and blushed. She shrugged and occupied herself by pouring tea in their cups. “Not sure, yet. Sugar or milk?”

    “No, thanks,” Carol said. She swallowed hard and took the cup Zosia offered her. “Thanks.”

    “Of course.”

    They sipped their tea silently. 

    Carol forces her lips tightly shut because she knew there were so many things that threatened to come out from her lips. Starting with how much she’d missed her, how she’d hoped she would show up to her readings and there she was.

    “Are you going back to the cabin after the tour?”

    Carol nodded. “I’m gonna take a little vacation and then I might stay there to continue working on my book."

Staring at her features, getting lost in her warm eyes, watching her full lips as she spoke. She watched her hair, she watched her neck and she felt the pull in her.     Carol looked up again at Zosia and hesitated. What was she even supposed to do? She had found Zosia – or Zosia had found her – but she had been distant. Too polite after everything they had shared.

    I'm sorry I had to leave. Please call me. - Carol

    Carol walked out to her hotel.

     

    ***

     

    Carol kicked the door of the lake house shut behind her. She let out a sigh and closed her eyes, listening to the comforting sound of the silence. At this time of the year, she wouldn’t be surprised to find out she was one of the rare neighbours still around.

    After weeks on a book tour, the idea of not seeing a single soul for days enchanted her. 

    She felt tired from traveling, from all the emotions she’d felt over the past weeks and mostly, over the past thirty-six hours.

    Carol pushed all thoughts of Zosia away and abandoned her luggage in the entrance of the house. She dropped the pile of letters she had received during her absence on the counter and headed straight for the shower.

    She came out relaxed, numb and ready to get to sleep and wake up with nothing to do. A luxury she hadn't experienced in weeks.

    She went to the kitchen and poured herself a glass of whiskey. She didn’t feel the need to drink any pain away, she simply wanted her body to relax more. To let go. To feel at ease.

    She sat at the kitchen island and took a long sip. She set the glass aside and went through her mail. Most of it was just junk, but there was a thicker orange envelope that had caught her attention from the moment she entered her house. It had been sent by Helen's office the previous day via express shipping. Clearly, Helen had wanted Carol to see the letter as soon as possible.

    Carol tore it open and what it contained triggered even more curiosity: another envelope.

    Frowning, she slid the second envelope out and a note fell down. She immediately recognized Helen’s handwriting. 

     

    Carol,

    This somehow got mixed with the fan mail. I’m really sorry it’s only getting to you now.

    I thought you’d want to see it.

    Helen.

     

    She pushed the bigger envelope away and took the smaller one in her hands. The paper was older, originally white but dirty from traveling back and forth between different places.

    She turned it around in her hands and her heart skipped a beat at the sight of her name and the address of Helen’s managing company written in capital letters. She knew right away this was Zosia’s handwriting. 

    She looked at the top corner and her guess was confirmed.

    Zosia Wicherek. 

    The return address was in Poland.

    Carol swallowed, troubled by the sight in front of her. 

    Suddenly, her mind spinned and the fatigue, emotions and sips of whiskey she had seemed to have caught up with her. She blinked, trying to clear her mind and understand what she was seeing.

    Zosia had been back to the country for a while already.

    So this meant…

    She looked at the faded ink of the numerous stamps on the paper. The oldest one dated from October.

    Two months after she had left.

    Right when Carol was at the lowest of her heartbreak, screaming at the sky for Zosia to come back, crying herself to sleep when she woke up in the middle of the night to find her bed empty, she had sent her this small package by the mail and it never got to Carol.

    With trembling hands, Carol tore the envelope and discovered that it contained the pictures from their trip. The ones taken with Zosia’s film camera. She had developed them and probably sent her the doubles. 

    There was also a note on a small piece of paper.

     

    I’m sorry for leaving so suddenly. I hope one day you’ll find it in your heart to forgive me. 

    I love you, Carol. 

     

    She signed with only a Z and below was a European phone number.

    So she had reached out to her. She hadn’t directly called Carol, but had made contact and eventually given Carol the choice to call her or not.

    Carol put the note down and took the pile of pictures in her trembling hands. The first one was a blurry image of Carol’s car in the parking lot, trunk door opened and half filled with their luggage.

    The second one was of Carol at the wheel, facing Zosia behind the lens and smiling brightly as they started their road trip. 

    Carol blinked and looked at her own face. Memories of that day flooded over her. This time, they weren’t fragments of it, but clear feels and images of it. She could smell the smell of the forest coming through the open windows, she could almost see the shape of the mountains in front of her. She could almost sense Zosia’s presence with her.

    Carol flipped the picture and saw her. Zosia taking a picture of herself in the mirror of their first hotel room. Her face wasn't clear because she held the camera close to it and the reflection of the flash blinded the image, but the lines of her body were very distinctive. She traced her bare leg with her finger before flipping the picture again.

    Carol almost dropped the pile of pictures as her eyes met Zosia’s. She was sitting at the diner, holding the old paper map across the table. Her untouched eggs sat at the corner of the picture. 

    The picture was taken just as Zosia moved to look at Carol, not quite smiling yet, but the warmth in her eyes was there and Carol had to hold back a choked cry at the sight of it. She cleared her throat and allowed herself to observe this look on Zosia’s face. At that moment, it had felt like the most reassuring thing in the world.

    And after months thinking she would never see it again, she realized it still did.

    A tear almost threatened to reach Carol’s eye. She shut them close and willed it away. She wasn’t going to cry over a picture.

    She opened her eyes again and looked at their joined mouths. She remembered the feeling of Zosia’s lips on her. How good it felt to be flesh to flesh, how her entire body relaxed and how happy she felt with her.

    The rest of the pictures were of canyons, breath-taking landscapes and some of the best meals they had shared during the trip. 

    When she landed on the first picture of her car again, Carol set down the pile and closed her eyes.

    She let it in, she let herself feel the confusion, the whirlwind of emotions until she realized what was going on.

    She opened her eyes and looked down at the content of the letter. It truly dawned on her. 

    Zosia had reached out to her. She even dared to conclude that Zosia had missed her. Missed her enough to send her the pictures of them kissing and Carol’s naked back after a night of passion in Vegas.

    Carol smiled and for the first time in months, allowed herself to feel happy and truly hopeful, without the shadow of a doubt. 

    She was going to get Zosia back.

    Then it hit her. 

    Her radio silence. Zosia’s weird distance in Boston. 

    She probably thinks I didn’t want to talk to her.

    Carol grabbed the note and reached for her phone. She dialled the number Zosia had left her and brought it to her ear. It rang a couple of times before the voice of an old Polish woman probably let her know that she couldn't take her call right now.

    She assumed it might be Zosia's mother and she could try to call later and ask her how to contact Zosia in Boston.

    Except, she didn't have all that time.

    Carol got up suddenly from her chair.

    I need to go to Boston.

    She checked the time. It was past midnight. She was completely exhausted and taking her car at this hour after not sleeping for days and drinking was a terrible idea.

    She grabbed her computer, bought the first tickets to Boston she saw from the nearest airport for the following day and headed to bed. 

    For the first time in months, she didn’t wake up drenched in sweat, haunted by the presence of Zosia next to her. 

    She woke up like she did when she was little before a trip that she had looked forward to for months. 

    Blissfully nervous and eager for morning to come.

    A month after Zosia left

     

    Heavy breaths.

    Rapid heartbeats.

    Sweaty chests.

    Carol straddled Zosia, moving her hips against her. 

    Eyes closed, lips parted.

    “What?”

    Zosia opened her eyes, body still too heavy to move. Her vision adjusted to the lack of light around her and the first thing she saw was the same old painting that hung on the wall of her childhood bedroom. It hit her then. She wasn’t at the lake house, waking up in Carol’s arms. She was back home.

    She closed her eyes, letting the sadness consume her once more. It had been only a month since she did the hardest thing she’d ever done and left Carol, the lake house and the happiest life she'd ever lived to move back to Poland with her mother.

    Only a month but it felt like a lifetime. Ever since she got back, every day felt the same. It depressed her and she dreaded thinking about her future.

    Zosia sighed and let her body wake up before she left the bed. She knew there was no use in trying to go back to sleep. She often woke up from dreams of Carol, which felt both like a blessing and a curse. Not all her dreams of her were so vivid, though, and not all of them were so…

    She blushed.

    No matter how early she woke, she was always there and Zosia could never get a moment of peace.

    “Dzień dobry,” she said, not even bothering to look up at her.

    “Dzień dobry,” Zosia replied, heading to the empty coffee machine. Her mother should’ve known by now that she always woke up not much longer after her, yet, she never made enough coffee for Zosia. 

    It was a small detail and she knew she was too lost in her own foggy mind to notice but it was enough to start her day on the wrong foot.

    She thought of Carol bringing her coffee before her days on the lake.

    And more like resting than running
    Peace is coming
