    Chapter Notes

    It feels so strange to post the last chapter of this fic!

    Back when I started it, I was having a really, really hard time at work and writing this story was an escape and made me feel better each time. Opening my laptop and picturing this peaceful love story in a peaceful setting was so comforting. And that you guys reading it, leaving kudos and kind comments throughout the fic even more. So, I wanted to say thank you so much for that! (Now, I've left my toxic work environment and love my new job yahoo so there are two happy endings in this haha).

    I hope you like this final chapter with these two lovebirds.

    Thank you so much! Please find me on twitter if you want so we can keep chatting about the show, the ship and everything pluribus (user manhattanhpburn)

    Enjoy ;)
    (also apologies, I do not speak one word of polish except the two I've learned reading fics haha so this is all research on google and different translator...)

    Carol wrapped chapter six of the new Wycaro novel and sent it to Helen. She closed her computer, stretched her back which cracked over the top of her chair. She eased the tension in her neck that had been building from working on the computer all day long.

    She got up from the desk, grabbed the coffee mug that had gathered a thin line of ground coffee at the bottom and headed downstairs. 

    It was now Spring: trees were turning green, days were starting to get longer and the nights warmer and Carol could finally leave winter behind. It had been long and painful but now each time the sun warmed her skin, she felt a part of her heart heal.

    Carol rinsed the mug in the sink and left it next to the bowl she’d used to eat cereal for lunch when she took a little break in the afternoon. She looked over at the lake. Zosia had gone boating with Koumba. Ever since her seizure, she stopped going alone. Carol knew it pained and annoyed her to depend on someone but Carol was happy that when she couldn’t be the one to be her passenger, her friends from town were all enthusiastic to go along with her.

    She’d taken the pontoon out of hibernation the week before and almost every evening after work, Carol and Zosia went on a little tour together. It was always chilly but there was no better place to watch the sunset than the lake.

    Carol poured herself a glass of wine and went to sit on the back porch. She closed her cardigan around herself, watching the tree branches dance in the wind, listening to the birds sing and watching the cotton candy clouds in the purple sky. She was noticing things she had never really noticed before. Her mind was clear, her heart was healed. 

    Some time passed and the sun hid behind the mountains when she heard the motor of the boat. She felt a quickening in her heartbeat and her body woke up with anticipation at the idea of seeing Zosia again. Once more, she was coming home to her. Carol thought she could never get enough of this.

    She heard their voices from afar as Carol knew they were parking the boat for the night. The following day was Saturday and Carol knew that Zosia would want them to go together and Carol would gladly follow.

    Finally, Carol heard their footsteps make their way up the gravel path. Zosia’s head peaked out of the trees and her face lit up at the sight of Carol, waiting for her on the porch.

    Zosia jogged to her, leaving a breathless Koumba walk the last strikes up alone behind. She went to Carol, climbing the few steps to the porch and bending down to give her a kiss. “Good evening,” she said, pressing her mouth to her. 

    "How was your day? Did the writing go well?"

    Carol couldn’t help but smile at her like an idiot. “It was productive.”

    “Not good?”

    “You know there aren’t any good writing days for writers.”

    “You poor, tortured artist,” Zosia smiled, giving her one last kiss before turning to Koumba who was standing on the steps of the porch, watching them happily. 

    “Don’t stop on my account,” he said, lifting his hands. “I wouldn’t want to interrupt the lovely reunion.

    Zosia rolled her eyes and walked to him. “Thank you for coming along with me today.”

    “Anytime. Alright, I’ll head back home and prepare supper for myself since you guys are not inviting me to stay over.”

    Carol fought back a smile, rolling her eyes. “I mean, you could stay…”

    “No,” Zosia smiled, turning to Carol. “Not tonight. I’m taking you out to the village.”

    “Oh,” Carol said, her surprise quickly turning to excitement at the determination in Zosia’s eyes. “A date? What for?”

    “Why do I need a reason to take my girlfriend out on a date?”

    Carol tried to hold back the ridiculous smile that threatened to break on her lips in front of Koumba, but could hardly control it. 

    “It’s great to see you guys back together,” Koumba smiled. “Have a great night! We should get to the lake the three of us soon.”

    “Absolutely. Thanks for coming along,” Zosia smiled at him.

    Carol waved at him and he disappeared behind the house. Zosia turned to Carol and watched her, a small smile on her face. 

    “What?” Carol asked shyly.

    “You don’t mind?”

    “What? That we go boating with Koumba?”

    “That I called you my girlfriend.”

    “Oh,” Carol said, feeling her cheeks turn pink. “Of course not. I loved it. I thought we were.”

    “We are, I just…” Zosia’s face darkened for a moment and Carol knew exactly what was going on behind those beautiful eyes of hers. 

    They had told each other all this before Zosia left. Now that she was back, they had simply picked up things without having these kinds of conversations. Not that they avoided it, they simply moved together so well that it didn't feel necessary. But maybe it was.

    “Zosia,” Carol said, taking her hand. Zosia moved to sit in the Adirondack chair next to her. “Of course you are my girlfriend. I want you to be, you know that.”

    “I know,” Zosia nodded. “I want you to be mine, too.”

    “I hope so,” Carol smiled, an attempt to bring back a cheerful mood after that sudden turn in the evening. “You know I don’t resent you for leaving, right?”

    Zosia’s eyes met hers. They were wet with tears and Carol hated seeing that sad look on her face. She had been so happy just five minutes ago. “You should,” her voice broke when she said it.

    “Well, I’ve never done anything that I ‘should’ do in my life and I won’t start now,” she squeezed her hand and tried to reassure her, but it was hardly working. Carol knew Zosia still felt guilty for leaving Carol behind. Of course, Carol was healing from the heartbreak but this ache easily went away every day she spent loving Zosia.

    Zosia finally gave a little smile.

    “Did anything happen? Why do you think that?” Carol asked.

    “I think about that all the time,” Zosia confessed. “I wonder if you still feel hurt, if you still feel anger at me for leaving.”

    “I don’t.”

    “Why aren’t you angry?”

    “Zosia, I saw with my own two eyes the pain you were in at that time. How could I be angry at you for this?”

    Zosia shook her head. “I was a coward.”

    “I wouldn’t say that. And if someone is familiar with being a coward, it’s me.”

    “Don’t say that,” Zosia frowned. “You’re brave. Look what you’ve done.”

    Carol looked over at the lake, trying to find the best way to let Zosia know that she just wanted her. No matter what she’d done, no matter what she’d do. “It’s normal to hurt the people you love, even if you don’t want to, you always end up doing it somehow.”

    Zosia watched her not fully convinced, but it was enough for now.

    “Come on, Zos,” Carol said, getting up from her seat, “Take me out on that date.”

     

    ***

     

    Zosia took her to a cozy restaurant in town, where they sat by the window overlooking the lake as they enjoyed the best meals of the village. They talked for hours to the low glow of a candlelight, making plans for the summer, for the set up of the garden that Zosia wanted to grow in Carol’s yard.

    It started raining just as they were heading out of the restaurant. 

    Carol held the door open for Zosia, standing behind her under the awning as Zosia opened her umbrella.

    She turned to Carol. “Do you want to walk around?”

     

    ***

     

    The streets were quite busy for a Friday night even in the off-season. The main street of the village had already been blocked to become pedestrian for the summer and strings of lights were already hanging above the paved alley. 

    Outside the opened bars and cafés, a couple of people gathered under the roofs to smoke a cigarette and chat before heading home for the night.

    Under the umbrella, Carol linked her fingers with Zosia, resting her head on her shoulder as they trotted along the main street. They didn’t talk, they simply enjoyed each other’s company on this perfect evening. 

    “Thank you for taking me out tonight,” Carol looked up at Zosia.

    Zosia turned to her and smiled. The sudden sadness that had taken over her earlier on the back porch was now gone and Carol knew she trusted that she was forgiven. 

     

    ***

     

    Carol hadn’t planned to confess her darkest secret to a random journalist on a Wednesday at three in the afternoon. 

    Leslie Park was a veteran literary critic that Carol had known for many years. They weren’t friends. No, Carol wasn’t friends with the press, but Leslie had always seemed to understand Carol before Carol even understood herself. 

    A queer woman from Florida who had also suffered discrimination growing up in a conservative family, she had seen through Carol’s books things most people could never see. 

    And it was the first time that they talked since Carol’s coming out. 

    Even if she knew she was in good hands with Leslie, Carol was restless before she hopped in their scheduled zoom call. It was the last of the three interviews her publicist had accepted regarding her upcoming career award. 

    The first part of the interview went smoothly. As always, Leslie’s questions were smart, well calculated and deep. It was impossible not to reveal more to her about oneself than planned. Still, the last thing Carol expected was to open up about the traumatic months she had spent at Camp Freedom Falls.

    Not many people in her life knew about this. In her close circle, only Helen and Val knew that she had been sent to conversion therapy but neither truly knew the details of the horrors Carol had endured there as a scared teenager. She had never shared those with anyone, because doing so would make them real.

    Not even Zosia knew.

    When the interview ended, Carol sat in silence behind her desk in the mezzanine. She looked ahead at the lake, where she and Zosia had planned to go the next day for the last time before they left to travel to L.A.

    Carol took a deep breath, resting her hands on the solid oak of the desk. She had managed to keep this ugly part away from Zosia long enough. Now, she had to tell her the truth. 

    She knew it would be easier to tell it to Leslie than to Zosia. Leslie, even if Carol liked her more than most journalists, was someone she didn’t love. The sadness in her face hadn’t broken Carol’s heart in thousands of pieces. Sure, her compassion had been more than welcome and sharing her experience with someone who had gone through this as well had been more healing than expected.

    But she knew that Zosia’s soft and kind heart would break to know this.

    Now Carol knew it wasn’t her job to protect Zosia from her traumatic teenage hood and that she had to let Zosia in. She had to let Zosia be there for her in pain and happiness.

    The front door of the cabin opened and the whole place came to life as Zosia walked in. She didn’t have to say any word. The energy shifted, her light footsteps reassured Carol and it seemed like every piece of furniture looked better when Zosia was around.

    Carol realized that she had been staring into oblivion for hours. Her screen had turned black and her butt hurt from sitting in the same position too much. She blinked, her vision readjusting to her surroundings. She got up and made her way downstairs. As she approached the kitchen – where she could hear Zosia make her way around to prepare dinner – she realized that the prospect of telling her this story didn’t make her want to run away through the back door.

    On the contrary, she felt ready more than she ever did.

    Zosia turned and jumped, seeing Carol stand behind the island in the kitchen. “Jesus, Carol! You scared me,” she chuckled, bringing a hand to her chest. She was still wearing her work outfit: tight leggings, a tight cami and a tight sports vest. She looked absolutely ravishing. 

    “Sorry,” Carol smiled and made her way to her.

    They had settled into a new, comfortable routine with Zosia’s new work and Carol’s new book. And each time she came home, Carol greeted her with a kiss.

    She did the same as always and climbed on the top of her toes to kiss her. Unlike the previous nights, Carol let the kiss linger. Her hands came up to cup Zosia’s cheeks and she let her mouth melt against hers. Zosia hummed in delighted surprise and kissed Carol back, her tall arm wrapping around her waist to pull her closer.

    When Carol finally leaned back, Zosia watched her with a curious look. “What was that for?” she joked, but Carol’s look on her was intent.

    “I need to talk to you.”

    Zosia frowned, worry creeping on her face. “Is everything okay?”

    Carol nodded and smiled encouragingly. “Yes, don’t worry.”

    She seemed to relax a bit and Carol stood to give her one more kiss, before taking Zosia to the living room.

    They sat facing each other and Carol let her hand rest on Zosia’s thigh. Even if Carol had told her not to worry, she could feel her body tense under her touch. 

    “I have to tell you something that I haven’t had…” Carol let out a breath, searching for the right word. “Maybe something I’ve been trying to protect you from. I don’t really know. It’s not something that I share with people. I’ve kept it hidden from everyone my whole life. Val knows because one night I was so drunk, almost passed out and miserable because Helen and I were going through a difficult time in our relationship and I confessed it to her. And Helen, well, I think it was after two, maybe three years of dating that I told her.”

    Zosia took a deep breath, steadying herself for what was to come. She sensed that it was something big and pushed her own worried thoughts away. Her tall hand covered Carol’s and she linked their fingers together in a reassuring gesture. Her brown eyes were kind and compassionate even if Carol hadn’t started telling her.

    “And today, I told it to Leslie Park at The New York Times. I hadn’t planned for it to happen. I thought you would be the next person I told it to, but then I realized that telling you would be easier if I told someone else first. Someone I didn’t want to protect or for whatever reason I folded myself and kept this hidden from you.”

    “It’s okay, Carol,” Zosia simply said. “You can tell me what you what when you feel ready.”

    Carol’s mouth curled into a sad smile. “Thank you. I’ve been wanting to tell you this for so long… It’s just… So hard,” her voice caught in her throat and she felt her eyes tear up. “But I told Leslie and then I realized that I feel ready to tell you. Maybe I unconsciously gave myself some sort of ultimatum by telling a journalist first so I wouldn’t be able to coward away before you find out in a paper.”

    Carol took a deep breath. The tenderness in Zosia’s eyes was almost too much to bear. She needed it in order to tell her story but she knew that an incoming breakdown was inevitable.

    “I think the reason why I’m so fucked up is partly because my mom sent me to a conversion therapy camp when I was sixteen.”

    Zosia’s face didn’t fall like she worried it would. She didn’t cry, she didn’t suffer from Carol’s words. She listened, giving Carol all the space to let it all out. One of her eyes twitched and Carol knew she was working hard to control her expression for her: she needed to be strong for her and Carol appreciated her for that.

    So Carol let it all out.

    At first, she told the major points of her story: her first love betraying her, her mother finding out about her and sending her to the camp. She told her about the ride there, about the scared teenagers there and about some of the terrible things the most terrible people she had ever known had done to them. 

    But as she told the limited version of the story, the one she had told Val, even Helen and especially Leslie, Carol realized there was so much more lying underneath that she had to let go. 

    So, the more she spoke, the more she got into details.

    As she spoke, voices came back to her, she remembered smells and noises and blurred faces crystallized. She relived some of it from the point of view of an outsider. Some things were not really as bad as she had remembered. Some others… Her heart broke thinking about the sixteen year-old version of herself. She wanted to go back in time and hug her, tell her that things would not always be like this. Tell her that these people wouldn’t have control over her life forever…

    But then, Carol was over fifty years old and she was still trembling at the memory of them. 

    Maybe they had.

    Maybe they always would.

    Only if you let them.

    Zosia listened, the grip on Carol’s hand tightening to the point that it physically hurt, but Carol let her. It kept her grounded, it kept her here in the cabin with the woman she loved, away from the worst time of her life.

    Finally, only when she was done telling every last bit of her memory, Carol broke down. It was a strange cry at first, a single sob stuck in her throat. She almost laughed at the sound of it. But then, it unleashed something deeper. Something she hadn’t even begun to realize she was holding somewhere in her tiny body.

    Zosia held her close against her, pulling her so hard Carol thought she might break. She cradled her hair like a sad child – maybe she was – and whispered words of comfort in her ear.

    She didn’t know how long she cried. From the throbbing headache it left her, she assumed it might have been a very long time.

    Eventually, there were no more tears to cry. And only then did she realize a lie she had spent her entire life telling herself. Only then did Carol realize that she wasn’t the most monstrous person in her life. 

    They were.

     

    ***

     

    That night, Zosia held Carol against her in bed. Her arms were wrapped around her protectively, pressing her body against her to try and save her from any bad dream that might come to harm her. She fell asleep before Carol, her tight grip on her never releasing.

    Carol melted back against her, letting the slow rise and fall of her chest against her back comfort her. Her body was warm, strong and firm. Carol held on to it like an anchor even if since her confession she had felt a weight off her shoulders.

    Carol watched the reflection of the moon on the ceiling of the mezzanine and she thought about the first time she came back to the cabin. She thought of how her life had been swept around in the most unimaginable ways by Zosia. She thought of how much she had changed in only a few months.

    She smiled and closed her eyes, linking her fingers to the hand that was holding her. She breathed in Zosia’s smell and told herself that the next morning, she would wake up and she might really give herself a real chance at happiness. 

     

    ***

     

    Carol was packing the last of her belongings for the trip to Los Angeles in the mezzanine, listening to Zosia speaking in the living room. Her voice was light, talking fast with energy, though she couldn’t understand one word she was saying – some of them she was starting to catch on, but never enough to understand the discussions Zosia had started having with her mother once a week.

    She heard Zosia laugh and her heart softened. She was beyond happy that Zosia’s mother was slowly doing better and that they could have a good relationship again. She saw how Zosia seemed happier since they started talking regularly over the phone and all she wanted was for her to be happy.

    Carol heard them hang up and Zosia stormed up the stairs to the mezzanine and crashed on the bed, an incredulous look on her face. “Carol, you’ll never guess what.”

    The corners of her mouth curled up and Carol put the pyjama she was folding in her luggage. “What?”

    “My mom… She’s seeing someone!”

    Even Carol was surprised. She looked up at Zosia, mirroring her bewilderment. She didn’t know the woman but from everything Zosia had told her in the time they had known each other, that was a surprising turn of event.

    “Really?”

    “Yeah!” she exclaimed, incredulous. “They met at her dancing lessons.”

    “You mean the dancing lessons you encouraged her to take? I hope she told you ‘Thank you my beautiful daughter. Because of your tenacious and dedicated encouragement and love for me I have met a man who rocks my world’.”

    Zosia snorted, pulling her phone out of her back pocket. “Please, stay realistic. She just said that it wasn’t a bad idea after all. Let me check his Facebook.”

    Carol smiled, watching Zosia search for the man. Her brows furrowed when she clicked on his profile picture. “Oh,” she hummed.

    “What is it?”

    Zosia let out a sigh and turned the phone to Carol. “I really don’t understand straight women.” She was staring back at a serious-looking old man who only had a twinkle in his eye to make up for decades of not taking good care of himself. “Can you believe this dude takes dance lessons?”

    Carol chuckled. “He looks… Kind?”

    Zosia lied down on the bed and scrolled her phone. Carol watched her. She kept her eyes on Zosia as she put the pile of clothes she was folding in her suitcase and got on the bed. She moved up to Zosia and straddled her. She took the phone from her hands and put it on the nightstand. 

    The brunette smiled at her, running her hands up her thighs. “We should sleep early tonight. We have a long week ahead.”

    “Mmm,” Carol said, leaning down to steal a kiss from her. “We can be quick.”

    It was Zosia’s turn to laugh. “Where’s the fun in that?”

    Carol kissed her again, letting her mouth rest on her. She let out a deep breath from her nose, releasing the pressure and tension at the touch of Zosia. She pulled her head away and watched her. 

    “What?” Zosia smiled.

    “Thank you for coming with me. It means a lot.”

    “I wouldn’t miss it for anything in the world.” She watched Carol with such tenderness, she could almost feel the broken pieces of her heart stick back together. “I’m so proud of you,” Zosia said. Her face was happy and eyes warm. “Do you realize how far you’ve come in such a short period of time?”

    “Well, it was long overdue,” Carol shrugged.

    “Don’t try to take any credits from yourself, Carol. You should be proud. Are you proud of yourself?”

    “I guess. Sure, yeah.”

    “Say it.”

    “What?” Carol asked.

    “Say that you’re proud of yourself.”

    Carol sighed, but there was a wide grin on her face. “You’re so cheesy.”

    “So what?”

    Carol chuckled and let her head fall. She rested her forehead on Zosia’s chin, trying to conjure up the words she did believe, but simply couldn’t say out loud.

    “The longer you take, the less time we’ll have for sex, by the way.”

    Carol’s laughter echoed into the room. She looked up at Zosia, feeling her heart hammer against her chest. She really was crazy in love with the woman.

    “Ok, I am proud of myself,” she said in a low voice.

    Zosia frowned, tilting her head like a kid. “What was that? I thought I heard you say something.”

    Carol rolled her eyes, but her smile widened. “Jesus, you’re impossible. I’m proud of myself.” Carol said louder.

    “Good. You should be,” Zosia nodded, bringing her head down for another kiss.

     

    ***

     

    They spent a day in L.A. before the award ceremony. They walked down Hollywood boulevard and took a walk up to the Hollywood sign. Carol didn’t shy away from holding Zosia’s hand, from resting her chin on her shoulder or wrapping her arm around her waist while they waited in line. In a moment of pure happiness, she even surprised both herself and Zosia by giving her a kiss.

    It was still early when they got back to their hotel and took a bubble bath while drinking tea, making it a new traveling tradition. They lied on the bed wrapped in their bathrobes, occasionally chatting, Zosia flipping through magazines while Carol worked on the speech she didn’t want Zosia to read until the ceremony the following day. They often stopped what they were doing to make out, pressing their bodies together and enjoying the presence of the other.

    They ordered take out, watched television while eating and went to bed early.

    Carol couldn’t keep her eyes closed, tossing and turning with nervousness about what was coming. She was grateful to be receiving the award but so many worries crossed her mind. She had come out in interviews and even if those were read by hundreds of people, it was different to talk about her sexuality during a one-on-one discussion with a journalist than in front of a crowd of hundreds of people. She also felt like an imposter. She was receiving a LGBTQIA+ career award when she spent her life hiding and had written straight smut until only one book ago. What if people, maybe people who had been out longer than her, called her out?

    Carol grunted and turned to face Zosia in the dark. “Zosia?” she whispered. “Are you sleeping?”

    “I can’t sleep with you tossing and turning around like that, kochanie,” Zosia said in a fully awake voice. She couldn’t see her face, but her voice was kind and soft.

    “Sorry,” Carol sighed. 

    “It’s normal to feel nervous, Carol. It’s a big thing, you know?” Zosia said, finding her hand under the bedsheet to link their fingers together. 

    “I just worry… You know, I really am… Proud of myself, like you say. But this…”

    “I know,” Zosia said, moving to snuggle against Carol, resting her head on her chest. Carol’s body immediately relaxed under her touch. She wondered if Zosia could hear the pounding of her heart. “I don’t think it would be normal to not feel anxious about this. Maybe today you want to throw up from stress,” Carol smiled at her words, “but tomorrow, you’ll be so much prouder of yourself. And so will I.” She placed a small kiss on Carol’s chest.

    They were silent for a moment, Carol’s fingers dancing on Zosia’s back. The room was pitch dark and quiet, but the silence was heavy, like they both had something in mind.

    “You know what would help calm my nerves?” Carol asked innocently. 

    Zosia snorted. “You should rest.”

    “I can’t rest. But if we… you know… I will be able to because you’ll completely exhaust me.”

    “Are you a frat boy?”

    “You love me,” Carol said.

    Zosia laughed quietly. Her hand brushed over Carol's abs and slipped under her tank top to rub against her breast. Carol’s breath caught in her throat at the feeling of Zosia’s hand on her. “Yes, I do,” Zosia whispered honestly. 

     

    ***

     

    The night went by in a blur. Carol allowed herself a glass of wine to take the edge off while getting ready, then another before leaving. She went through a red carpet, she even took some photos with Zosia who posed like she had been doing this her entire life, and then she gave a few interviews. When she sat down at the table for the ceremony, she couldn’t remember walking in in the first place.

    Zosia lifted her arm around the back of Carol’s chair and leaned down closer to her. “Are you okay?” she asked, searching Carol’s face.

    She met her eyes and smiled encouragingly. “Yep,” she said, grabbing her hand under the table. “Thank you for being here.”

    “Of course. Any time. Fun fact! Did you know that it was the Swedish author Selma Lagerlöf who was the first woman to receive the Nobel Prize in Literature in 1909. And another important prize first won by a woman was Edith Wharton in 1921, she won the Pulitzer Prize for the Age of Innocence.”

    “Not sure Lucasian will get me a Pulitzer Price, though."

    The ceremony unfolded too slowly. Carol was bouncing her knee under the table, forcing herself to stay away from the wine if she didn’t want to slur her speech, and absent-mindedly playing with Zosia’s fingers, that she was still holding under the table.

    Finally, after what seemed like forever, it was Carol’s turn. They presented Carol’s career and the recent news of her coming out. The reception in the audience was really good, which made her relax a little and the proud, warm looks that Zosia was giving her made her feel confident.

    “So, it is with an immense pleasure that I welcome to the stage the best-selling author of the Wycaro novels Carol Sturka to come receive her award.”

    Carol squeezed Zosia’s hand under the table one last time and got up, heading to the small stage in front of hundreds of people. 

    She took her prize, looking at it for a brief moment, and realized what was happening. How her life had turned. She walked to the podium and put down the prize to unfold the paper with her speech scrambled. The audience’s applause went quiet and Carol cleared her throat in the microphone.

    “I, uh… Thank you, wow. Thank you so much for this honor. As you might now know after I revealed it in an interview earlier this week, when I was sixteen, my mother sent me to conversion therapy. For almost four decades, I believed that this part of me was something to be ashamed of. I carried a shame that dictated my life, that ruined so many beautiful things in it.” Her mind wandered to Helen. “My original version of my character Raban was a woman. In my heart, Raban was always a woman and the love story was a lesbian love story. But once again, I let fear decide for me and this wonderful thing that I was pretty decent at and loved doing turned it into a chore, into something I grew sick and tired of over the years. I was about to give up on these books until I met, well, I guess you could say the female version of Raban. Or at least, to me she was. Everything changed from that moment and I realized that there was still a part of me – no matter how small – that was ready to fight for what I loved. 

    "And then, I thought 'I’m over fifty, why does it even matter to do this now?' So, I googled things that you shouldn’t be doing once you are past fifty. Coming out and changing the gender of the fan-favourite character of your best-selling novels was surprisingly not on these lists. The more I looked into it, the more I realized that there aren’t many things that you have to stop doing or cannot do once you pass fifty, besides rocking certain hairstyles, keeping the same skin routine as when you were twenty years old and picking up on all fashion trends at once. So, yeah, I did it. Fuck yeah,” she shrugged and people in the audience reacted to that. 

    “Thank you,” Carol smiled. “I was terrified and worried and anxious but the reaction to all these scary changes was so positive and encouraging that I thought I should’ve done it earlier, in the end. And what I learned from doing that is to keep taking big scary steps in the hope that one day, it makes this path easier for someone else. I would never have been able to do all that without the help of the wonderful people around me. Helen, my manager, who has been looking out after me and taking care of me for over thirty years. She’s the real strength behind those books. Val, my editor, who has kept up with me for the same amount of time, except that we weren’t married so I don’t know if that makes it better or worse for her. They are the dream team and I wouldn’t stand here without them. And there's another person without whom I would clearly not be here today receiving this award. She might’ve started as the female version of Raban, as the spark of inspiration but it didn’t take time before she became so much more than that. She is a friend, a lover, an unending stream of inspiration and I still cannot believe that she has decided to love me too. I love you, Zosia,” she tried to find her in the crowd, but was blinded by the spotlight. “And to the fans, thank you for your passion and for embracing these characters, for accepting these huge changes with such an openness and love that I never thought was possible. Thank you.”

    Carol smiled one last time, waved as the audience cheered for her and grabbed her prize before leaving the stage, still trembling. As soon as she stepped outside of the limelight, she felt the weight fall off her shoulders and she couldn’t help the wide proud smile on her face.

     

    ***

     

    It was mid-July, in the midst of the summer vacation thrill. Zosia and Carol were lying down at the front of the pontoon, grilling under the sun in their private bay. They had come across many boats that day, but the weather really called for it. It was the best week of the summer so far. 

    For the past weeks, Carol and Zosia had occupied their days hiking, fishing, shopping at the farmer’s market and sailing on the lake. They occasionally went out with Koumba and Laxmi either to the pool bar or to Koumba’s place. Sometimes, they even invited the neighbours to come boating with them.

    That day, though, they were blissfully alone.

    “And how do you say ‘I think I’m gonna get sunburnt in this heat’?” Carol asked.

    They were lying side by side, shoulders brushing on their towels, their fingers loosely linked between them. 

    Zosia laughed, turning her head to watch Carol. Her eyes were hidden behind her sunglasses but Carol knew all too well that she was looking at her with that adorable look on her face. “Chyba dostanę oparzenia słonecznego w tym upale.”

    Carol went silent.

    “Slower, please?” 

    Zosia repeated, slower. Carol tried to say it, but it was a mess and it made Zosia laugh at her. “I’ll get there, trust me. In the meantime, I’ll just say kocham cię,” she said, turning to press her mouth against Zosia’s.

    “It’s more than enough,” Zosia smiled against her lips. “And I can tell you this: Nigdy nie chcę żyć bez ciebie.”

    Carol watched her. “What does it mean?” she asked in a low voice, like a kid talking a secret language with her friends, unable to know the depths of Zosia’s words.

    “You’ll find out one day,” Zosia smiled.

    “No, don’t do that! I must know.”

    “It means you look really hot in this bathing suit,” Zosia smirked, leaning for another kiss.

    “Really?”

    “No. I mean, you do. But that would be Wyglądasz naprawdę gorąco w tym stroju kąpielowym.”

    “What did you say then?”

    Zosia smiled shyly and her cheeks turned pink and Carol knew it wasn’t because of the sun. “I said I never want to live without you.”

    Carol’s eyes widened in pleasant surprise. Her smile grew and she watched Zosia like she still couldn’t believe that she was hers. 

    “Ja, uh... Ja też nie,” Carol said and smiled proudly at her broken Polish.
